**Revisit if:** A future consumer of `checks.depth` needs to distinguish "TypeScript-side depth" from "SQL-side depth" specifically (e.g. to diagnose which mechanism is closer to exhausting its budget) — split the field then, with a real consumer driving the shape, not preemptively.

## D-039 — `buildDivergenceRecord` is extracted as its own pure, exported function specifically so the Phase 6 path-threading claim is unit-testable without Postgres or unsafely mutating a shipped resolver file

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 6 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** The `DivergenceRecord`-building logic inside `runSoundnessFuzz`'s own loop (classify, then attach `referencePath`/`productionPath` present iff that resolver's own boolean was `true`) is pulled out into `buildDivergenceRecord(input): DivergenceRecord | null`, a pure function taking already-computed booleans/paths and `classifyResult`'s own (untouched) output. `runSoundnessFuzz` now just calls it in a loop; nothing about `classify.ts`'s classification logic changed.
**Alternative rejected:** Leave the record-building logic inline inside `runSoundnessFuzz`, and satisfy `test/isolation/differential-soundness.fuzz.test.ts`'s formerly-`.todo()` resolution-path test either by (a) driving the real `runSoundnessFuzz` against a real, temporarily-broken production resolver file, or (b) leaving the test `.todo()` a while longer, pending a real Postgres-backed fixture in the `.integration.test.ts` sibling file.
**Why it lost:** (a) is exactly the practice this repo's own `test/isolation/differential-soundness.fuzz.test.ts` doc comment already rules out for its "fuzz harness has power" tests — mutating a shipped source file at test-run time is unsafe (a crash mid-mutation leaves the repo broken), not parallelizable, and not repeatable the way a committed CI test needs to be; that discipline should apply here too, not just to the tests that happened to state it explicitly first. (b) would leave a satisfiable claim un-tested past the phase that implements what it needs, which D-001 already calls a signal the phase isn't actually done. Extracting the pure function costs one small, uncontroversial refactor and makes the actual new behavior (Phase 6's field-population rule) testable the same way `classify.ts`'s own tests already work — hand-supplied inputs, no I/O, no synthetic engine needed — while a real, live differential-fuzz run against a deliberately broken engine (verified manually for this task — see the final report for the actual numbers) still covers the end-to-end claim, matching this project's own established verification discipline for Phase 5.
**Revisit if:** Never, for the extraction itself — a pure `build*Record` function factored out of an I/O-heavy orchestration loop specifically to keep it unit-testable is a pattern worth repeating, not a one-off.

## D-040 — `expand()`'s cycle guard is real production code, but its own `boundReached`-equivalent (`cycleGuard`/`depthLimitReached`) is a rendering signal, not a claim a downstream consumer needs to re-verify the way a check's `boundReached` disproof leaf does

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 6 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `expand()`'s `cycleGuard`/`depthLimitReached` nodes are surfaced as-is in the returned tree (so a caller rendering it can show _why_ a branch stopped) with no accompanying certificate proving the cutoff was legitimate — unlike a check's resolution-path `BoundReachedDisproof`, which only ever appears inside an _allowed_ check's exclusion disproof and therefore must independently re-verify for the check's own `allowed: true` conclusion to be trustworthy.
**Alternative rejected:** Give `expand()`'s cycle/depth nodes the same certificate-level rigor as a check's `BoundReachedDisproof` (e.g. embedding the ancestor path that proves the cycle, so a consumer could independently confirm the cutoff rather than trusting `expand()`'s own bookkeeping).
**Why it lost:** `expand()` never asserts a security-relevant boolean the way a check does — it answers "who's in this set," and a `cycleGuard` node is disclosure ("this branch was cut off here, for this reason"), not a load-bearing premise of an `allowed: true` verdict that Phase 5's differential-fuzz soundness claim depends on. Nothing in §9 Phase 6's exit criterion asks `expand()`'s cycle-safety to be independently re-verifiable the way a resolution path's is — only that it terminates on a real cycle instead of hanging, which is a termination property (verified directly for this phase — see the final report's account of temporarily removing the guard and observing the walk fail to terminate within 15 seconds, versus well under a second with it intact) and a shape property (the tree the caller sees), not a proof obligation. Building a certificate here would be real, unrequested complexity for a claim nothing downstream actually needs to check.
**Revisit if:** `expand()`'s output is ever used to _drive_ an authorization decision directly (rather than shown to a human deciding whether to trust one) — at that point its cycle/depth nodes would carry the same weight a check's disproof leaves do, and would need the same rigor.

## D-041 — `runSoundnessFuzz` never routes its per-query checks through `performCheck`; only a real, application-facing check gets an audit-log row

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 6 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `src/audit/checks.ts`'s `performCheck` (the `checks`-table-logging wrapper around `productionCheck`) has exactly one caller in this codebase: the CLI's `authz check` command. `src/soundness/runner.ts`'s `checkAllQueries` continues to call `productionCheck` directly, unchanged, exactly as it did before Phase 6.
**Alternative rejected:** Route every one of a fuzz run's `SOUNDNESS_FUZZ_QUERIES` (up to 5,000 by default) per-query checks through `performCheck` too, so the `checks` table has a complete record of literally every `productionCheck` call this system ever makes, fuzz or real.
**Why it lost:** A fuzz run's queries are checks against generated, seed-salted, synthetic fixture data (`src/soundness/generators.ts`) — they exist to test the engine, not to answer a real caller's real authorization question, and the run that produced them is already durably recorded in full as its own `soundness_runs` row (query count, every divergence, the seed needed to reproduce it byte-for-byte). Logging all 5,000 to `checks` on every run — including every CI run, per PR — would make the audit trail's actual signal (real application checks) a rounding error against synthetic noise within days, undermining the exact thing §9 Phase 6's exit criterion exists to make trustworthy: a human being able to read `checks` and understand what this system was actually asked and answered.
**Revisit if:** A future consumer needs `checks` to double as a complete `productionCheck` call log for performance/debugging purposes regardless of source — at that point, add a `source` column (`'application' | 'fuzz'`) rather than routing fuzz traffic through unconditionally, so the distinction this decision protects stays queryable rather than lost.

## D-042 — A failed `checks` audit-log write fails the whole check operation; `performCheck` never returns an unlogged answer

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 6 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `performCheck` computes the real answer via `productionCheck` first, then inserts the `checks` row; if that insert throws, `performCheck` itself throws — the already-computed `allowed`/`path` result is discarded, never returned to the caller. In the CLI, this surfaces exactly like any other infrastructure failure: exit 3, not a printed answer.
**Alternative rejected:** Catch a logging failure, `console.error` or otherwise note it, and still return the real, correctly-computed answer — "best-effort" audit logging that never blocks a caller who just wants to know if a permission is granted.
**Why it lost:** §9 Phase 6's exit criterion states "every check, allowed or denied, is logged" with no qualifier — not "every check that could be logged," not "logged when convenient." A caller who received `ALLOWED` from a check whose audit-log write silently failed would have a real, unrecorded grant decision — exactly the kind of gap that makes `docs/RELATIONS.md`'s and this project's own audit-trail claim (§6.7: "every allow decision records the path that justified it") an overstatement rather than a fact the moment it happens even once. `productionCheck` itself already draws this same line for a genuinely unreachable database (throws, never returns a smoothed-over `false`) — treating a failed audit write any more gently than a failed read would be an inconsistent standard applied to the same underlying database, for no principled reason.
**Revisit if:** A real deployment needs check _availability_ to survive a temporary audit-log outage (e.g. `checks`' own disk/partition full while `relation_tuples` is healthy) — that's a legitimate, different tradeoff (availability over completeness) a future phase could opt into explicitly (e.g. a `--best-effort-audit` flag), but it must be an explicit opt-in with its own stated risk, not this function's silent default.

## D-043 — A non-linear resolution path (`intersection`/`exclusion` anywhere in the tree) renders inside a fenced plain-text block, never a native nested markdown list

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 7 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `src/report/markdown.ts`'s `renderPathBlock` renders a fully linear proof (`directGrant`/`usersetMembership`/`tupleToUserset`/transparent `union` only) as one `→`-joined chain line, exactly §8's worked example. The moment an `intersection` or `exclusion` appears anywhere in the tree, the whole path renders instead as one fenced ` ```text ` block (plain text, no markdown syntax inside it), with the closing `_(this proves ...)_` annotation as its own separate markdown paragraph directly below the fence, never on the fence's own last line.
**Alternative rejected:** A native, indented markdown list (`- `/numbered, nested per combinator) for the structured case — the first draft built.
**Why it lost:** Verified by actually rendering the first draft's output through a real markdown parser (`markdown-it`), not by inspection: GitHub-flavored markdown's lazy-continuation rule silently folds a sibling line that isn't blank-line-separated and isn't itself a new list marker into the _previous_ list item's own content. This swallowed the renderer's own "excluding:" label and its closing "(this proves ...)" annotation into the wrong branch of the tree — a structural corruption of exactly the evidence this file exists to render faithfully, and precisely the kind of failure mode a `false_grant`'s resolution path (§6.7, §8) can least afford, since it's the one piece of output a reader is meant to trust literally. A fenced code block sidesteps GFM's list-nesting rules entirely (its content is never markdown-parsed), at the deliberate cost of losing inline bold/code emphasis inside the tree itself.
**Revisit if:** Never for the mis-nesting risk itself (that's inherent to GFM, not a fixable renderer bug). Revisit the code-fence tradeoff specifically if a future phase's UI (§8's actual screens, Phase 9) needs richer inline styling inside a non-linear resolution path — a real DOM-rendered tree component wouldn't carry GFM's own list-nesting hazard the way a markdown string does, and could use markdown-style nested lists safely.

## D-044 — `false_grant`'s reserved marker is a bold label, never an emoji, even inside a plain-text fenced block where the label itself can't render as bold

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 7 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** The one reserved treatment for `false_grant` throughout `src/report/markdown.ts` is `**FALSE_GRANT**` — bold, all-caps, backticked entity references around it — never an emoji anywhere in this file's output.
**Alternative rejected:** §8's own design-direction text names "a fixed emoji reserved only for `false_grant`" as one of two equally valid options alongside a bold label. The `report-designer` subagent's own instructions separately state a stricter copy rule: "no exclamation marks, no celebration, no emoji anywhere a `false_grant` could appear."
**Why it lost:** The two instructions conflict, and the more specific, more binding one wins — §8's own phrasing offers the emoji option casually ("or"), while the copy-rule section is written as an explicit prohibition with its own stated reasoning ("this is a security instrument, not a feature announcement"). A security-bug marker rendered as an emoji reads, to most engineers, closer to a notification badge or a celebratory reaction than a hazard marker — exactly the register mismatch the copy rules exist to prevent. Bold-plus-all-caps is unambiguous in every rendering context this report appears in (a GitHub PR comment, a raw `--format markdown` terminal dump, a `<summary>` tag where bold doesn't apply and the plain-text fallback `FALSE_GRANT` — still all-caps — still reads as unambiguous on its own).
**Revisit if:** Never, for PR comments/CLI output. If Phase 9's actual UI screens (§8) get a real one-hard-alert-color palette (which markdown categorically cannot express), that's the medium where §8's "the one hard alert color" instruction is literally satisfiable — revisit the marker question there with a real designer's tools, not markdown's.

## D-045 — `.github/workflows/soundness.yml` runs its own ephemeral Postgres `services:` container, never `secrets.DATABASE_URL`

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 7 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** The soundness workflow provisions a fresh `postgres:16-alpine` GitHub Actions `services:` container per run and points `DATABASE_URL` at it — never the real, long-lived Railway database this project's own `DATABASE_URL` secret (if configured) would point to, even though §7's own YAML stub literally shows `env: DATABASE_URL: ${{ secrets.DATABASE_URL }}`.
**Alternative rejected:** Follow §7's literal YAML — run the soundness fuzz against the real, shared project database via `secrets.DATABASE_URL`.
**Why it lost:** The standard fuzz budget writes up to `SOUNDNESS_FUZZ_QUERIES` (5,000 by default) worth of generated namespaces and tuples into `relation_tuples`/`namespace_configs` on every single run, on every PR, forever. Against a real, long-lived database, none of that data is ever cleaned up (D-033's seed-salting prevents cross-run _correctness_ collisions, but does nothing about accumulation) — a project with even modest PR volume would see its real database grow by tens of thousands of synthetic rows a week, purely as a side effect of CI existing. `ci.yml`'s own `test-integration` job already establishes the right precedent for this project: a disposable, per-run Postgres instance for anything that writes real rows during a test, never the shared project database (testcontainers there, since that job is a set of Vitest files that can start their own container in-process; a native `services:` container here, since `authz soundness run` is a plain CLI invocation with nothing to start a container itself).
**Revisit if:** A future phase wants to track soundness-run history _across_ runs in one persistent place (e.g. a dashboard of every PR's fuzz result over time) — that's a real, different requirement (durable `soundness_runs` history) this decision doesn't provide for, and would need its own design (e.g. a separate, dedicated reporting database this workflow writes a summary row to, distinct from the disposable database it fuzzes against).

## D-046 — The PR-comment upsert is a plain Node script calling the real `decidePrCommentAction`, never `actions/github-script` with the decision re-implemented inline

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 7 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `scripts/post-soundness-comment.mjs` — a plain Node 22 script using the built-in `fetch` to call the GitHub REST API directly, importing `decidePrCommentAction`/`SOUNDNESS_REPORT_MARKER` from the real build output (`dist/report/prComment.js`/`dist/report/markdown.js`) to decide whether to create or update a comment.
**Alternative rejected:** `actions/github-script`, a common GitHub Actions pattern for exactly this kind of task (list comments, decide, act) — no new npm dependency needed, and it ships an authenticated Octokit client for free.
**Why it lost:** Using `actions/github-script` would mean re-implementing "which comment counts as ours, and which one do we update" as inline JavaScript embedded in the workflow YAML — a second implementation of exactly the logic `src/report/prComment.ts`'s `decidePrCommentAction` already provides, tested (§10's own `the-soundness-pr-comment-updates-in-place-instead-of-posting-twice` bullet), and reviewed. Two implementations of the same decision can drift; a plain script that imports the real function cannot. Node 22's built-in `fetch` makes the REST calls themselves trivial enough that `actions/github-script`'s only remaining advantage (a pre-authenticated Octokit client) doesn't offset the cost of the duplicated logic. No new npm dependency either way — `@octokit/*` was never added to `package.json` (build spec §2's stack has no GitHub API client listed, and none was needed).
**Revisit if:** This project's CI ever needs a broader set of GitHub API interactions (issue triage, release automation, etc.) where a real Octokit client's convenience genuinely outweighs one script's worth of raw `fetch` calls — at that point, evaluate `@octokit/rest` as a real devDependency addition (asking first, per build spec rule 6), rather than reaching for `actions/github-script` piecemeal per workflow.

## D-047 — The build never copied `.sql` migration files into `dist/`; the built CLI silently applied zero migrations against a fresh database

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 7 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `npm run build` now runs `node scripts/copy-migrations.mjs` after `tsc`, copying `src/store/migrations/*.sql` to `dist/store/migrations/`.
**Alternative rejected:** None seriously considered — this is a bug fix, not a design tradeoff. (The nearest real alternative, resolving `MIGRATIONS_DIR` against `src/` instead of the compiled file's own location regardless of build vs. source, was rejected because it would make the _built, distributed_ CLI depend on a `src/` directory being present alongside `dist/` at runtime — wrong for anything actually installed as a package, and not how `src/cli/commands/doctor.ts`'s existing `new URL('../../store/migrations', import.meta.url)` pattern is meant to work.)
**Why it matters, found live, not by inspection:** `tsc` only ever compiles `.ts` files — it has never copied `.sql` files into `dist/`, since Phase 0. This went unnoticed through Phases 2–6 because every verification of migrations in this project's own history ran `tsx` against `src/` directly (`npx tsx src/cli/index.ts doctor`), never the _built_ `dist/cli/index.js`; Phase 0's own CHECKPOINT did verify the built CLI, but at that point zero real migrations existed yet, so "Migrations: 0/0 applied" was the correct output either way and the gap was invisible. Found while building `.github/workflows/soundness.yml` (Phase 7) — the first thing in this project to actually run `npm run build` and then execute the _built_ CLI against a genuinely fresh Postgres database (a GitHub Actions `services:` container has none of this project's tables) rather than against this sandbox's already-migrated local dev database. `doctor` reported "Migrations: 0/0 applied" — misleadingly exit-0 successful — while the fresh database silently had none of `namespace_configs`/`relation_tuples`/`checks`/`soundness_runs`, which would have made every subsequent step in the real CI workflow fail with a raw `relation "..." does not exist` error instead of a clear, actionable message.
**Revisit if:** Any future build output (an npm-published package, a Docker image) also needs these files — `scripts/copy-migrations.mjs` already handles the general case (copies the whole `migrations/` directory, not one file at a time), so this should already cover that; revisit only if a packaging format needs the files somewhere other than alongside the compiled `store/` output.

## D-048 — Phase 8 delegation split: `report-designer` designs response/error shapes, main agent builds the server, routing, and auth

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `report-designer` was given already-computed domain result types (`PerformCheckResult`, `ExpandNode`, `WriteTupleResult`, `SchemaCompileResult`, `PublishResult`) and designed `src/api/responses.ts`/`errors.ts` — pure, DB-free, no Fastify import, no request parsing. The main agent built `src/api/server.ts` (Fastify instantiation, routing, Zod request-body validation, the `ADMIN_API_KEY` auth wiring in `src/api/auth.ts`) and `src/cli/commands/serve.ts`.
**Alternative rejected:** Route the whole of Phase 8 through `report-designer` alone, per §14's delegation table literally assigning "Phase 8 API surface" to that subagent.
**Why it lost:** Direct continuation of the precedent this project already established twice — Phase 5 (`soundness-engineer` built the fuzz harness; the main agent wired the CLI command around it) and Phase 7 (`report-designer` built the markdown/JSON/exit-code/PR-comment modules; the main agent built the `--format` flag, `soundness.yml`, and `post-soundness-comment.mjs`). Build spec rule 11 assigns "the API/CLI/CI surface" to the main agent generally, which is in real tension with §14's own per-phase table — resolved the same way both prior times: the subagent owns pure presentation/response logic with no I/O; the main agent owns the infrastructure that calls it. Splitting it this way also kept `report-designer`'s own stated scope honest — they were never asked to reason about Fastify routing, HTTP framework error handling, or a secret-comparison scheme, none of which is a "how should this look/read" question their brief is suited to.
**Revisit if:** A future phase's own owner assignment in §14 is unambiguous and doesn't touch main-agent-owned infrastructure (e.g. a purely descriptive doc) — apply the table literally there; this split is specifically about phases that mix presentation logic with infrastructure wiring.

## D-049 — The API is unversioned, flat, and RPC-shaped (`POST /check`, `POST /expand`), not a REST resource model

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** Every route is `POST` (or `DELETE /tuples` for the one delete), named after the CLI operation it mirrors (`/check`, `/expand`, `/tuples`, `/schema/compile`, `/schema/publish`, `/health`), with no `/v1/` prefix and no resource-oriented nesting.
**Alternative rejected:** A conventional REST shape — `GET /checks?subject=...&relation=...&object=...` for a read-only check, `POST /v1/tuples` with a versioned prefix, `201 Created` + `Location` header for a tuple write.
**Why it lost:** §9 Phase 8's own wording is "exposing check/expand/write/schema _per the CLI's own operations_" — an RPC shape, not an invitation to invent a resource model this system doesn't have. `check`/`expand` both take a structured, nested query (`subject`/`object` as `{ns,id}` pairs) that a flat GET query string would force apart with no benefit (no caching layer exists to reward `GET`'s cacheability). None of the five operations exposes a single fetchable-by-URI resource for `201`+`Location` to point at — `report-designer`'s own `src/api/responses.ts` doc comment already reasons through this for status codes; this decision extends the identical reasoning to route _shape_. `/v1/` versioning was rejected as premature: there are zero external consumers yet to protect from a breaking change, and adding a version prefix now is a decision this project has no information yet to make well (v1 of what contract, exactly?).
**Revisit if:** This API grows a genuine caller outside this repo's own CLI/tests, at which point both a stable version prefix and `GET`-for-reads (for caching/idempotency semantics real HTTP infrastructure understands) become worth their cost.

## D-050 — An unconfigured `ADMIN_API_KEY` fails every write closed, never open

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `src/api/auth.ts`'s `checkAdminAuth` returns `{authorized:false, reason:'admin_api_key_not_configured'}` whenever `env.ADMIN_API_KEY` is unset or blank — every write route (`POST /tuples`, `DELETE /tuples`, `POST /schema/publish`) then returns `401`, regardless of what (if anything) the caller supplied as a header.
**Alternative rejected:** Treat an unconfigured `ADMIN_API_KEY` as "no auth required" — let writes through unauthenticated when the operator hasn't set a key yet, on the theory that a fresh local/dev deployment shouldn't be locked out of its own write routes.
**Why it lost:** Build spec rule 10 states this system "never says a permission is granted because it seems like it should be" — applied here to configuration rather than a relation tuple, "no configured secret" failing open into "any caller may write" is exactly the kind of implicit grant that rule exists to rule out. `.env.example` ships `ADMIN_API_KEY=` blank by design (build spec rule 5: never invent a secret in code), so an operator who hasn't generated one yet gets a clear, specific `401` naming the reason ("ADMIN_API_KEY is not configured for this deployment — writes are disabled") rather than a silent open door they might not notice until it matters. This is also the more useful failure for the exact exit criterion Phase 8 has to prove — "an unauthenticated write attempt is rejected" reads as unconditionally true only under this choice.
**Revisit if:** Never, for this deployment model. A future phase adding a genuine multi-tenant/self-serve deployment story might want a distinct "setup mode" flow, but that's a different feature, not a reason to relax this default.

## D-051 — `GET /health` is deliberately unauthenticated

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `/health` requires no `Authorization` header, unlike the three write routes.
**Alternative rejected:** Gate `/health` behind the same `ADMIN_API_KEY` check, on the theory that namespace names/versions are internal deployment detail.
**Why it lost:** `/health` exists to answer "should this instance stay in rotation" for a load balancer or uptime monitor — tooling that, by convention and often by design, can't be handed an application-level secret just to poll liveness. Nothing in the response is a permission decision or a credential: namespace names and version numbers are schema metadata (what shape of data this deployment understands), never tuple data, never a check result, never anything a real ReBAC decision depends on. Requiring auth here would either force every monitoring setup to carry `ADMIN_API_KEY` (widening that secret's blast radius for no security benefit) or push operators toward a second, unauthenticated liveness endpoint anyway — solving nothing by adding a gate.
**Revisit if:** A future phase adds genuinely sensitive detail to the health payload (e.g. per-tenant tuple counts, connection pool internals that could aid an attacker profiling the deployment) — that's a different payload than today's, and would need its own auth decision.

## D-052 — `invalid_request` added to `report-designer`'s `ApiErrorCode` catalog, by the main agent, for malformed raw HTTP bodies

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `src/api/errors.ts`'s `ApiErrorCode` gained a sixth value, `invalid_request` (400), with its own constructor `invalidRequestError(detail)`, added directly to `report-designer`'s file rather than kept in a separate main-agent-owned module.
**Alternative rejected:** Build a second, main-agent-owned error-shape file for request-parsing failures, keeping `report-designer`'s original file untouched; or construct an ad hoc `{status:400, body:{...}}` object inline in `server.ts` wherever Zod validation fails.
**Why it lost:** A second file would duplicate the entire `ApiErrorBody`/`ApiErrorResponse` shape and the `API_ERROR_STATUS` table's own reasoning for existing as one small inspectable source of truth (see that table's own doc comment) — splitting "the status/body pairing for an API error" across two files for no reason but who originally authored which one would be exactly the kind of drift risk the table was built to prevent. An inline ad hoc object in `server.ts` would be worse: every other error path in this API goes through a named constructor with a documented shape, and a raw literal for just this one case would be the one response a reader of `server.ts` couldn't trace back to a single definition. Extending `errors.ts` is a genuinely additive change (a new discriminant value, a new constructor, a new table row) to a file whose own top-of-file doc comment already frames it as "the one error envelope every route returns" — a raw-body-parsing failure is squarely inside that scope, just a case `report-designer`'s original brief (already-computed domain results) didn't reach, since nothing about a malformed HTTP body exists before request parsing, which is explicitly main-agent territory.
**Revisit if:** A future reviewer wants stricter ownership boundaries between subagent-authored and main-agent-authored files — at that point, consider a lint rule or file-header convention marking "who may extend this file," rather than re-splitting this specific file after the fact.

## D-053 — Sign-off: `no_published_schema` stays a `400`, never a `404`

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** Confirmed `report-designer`'s own call in `tupleValidationError`'s doc comment (`src/api/errors.ts`): a tuple write against a namespace with no published schema returns `400 tuple_validation_failed`, the same status every other `TupleError` code gets — never a `404`.
**Alternative rejected:** Split `no_published_schema` out as a `404`, on the theory that "no schema for this namespace" reads like "this namespace doesn't exist as a resource."
**Why it lost:** `report-designer` flagged this explicitly as the one genuinely debatable call in their Phase 8 work and asked for main-agent sign-off before wiring status codes around it. Reviewed against this API's own shape (confirmed via D-049): none of the five named operations exposes a `GET /namespaces/:ns`-style fetchable resource, so there is no URI whose absence `404` would conventionally be describing — a caller isn't asking "does this namespace resource exist," they're asking "can this tuple write happen," and the honest answer to that is "no, and here's why" (a `TupleError` with a specific, actionable message), identical in kind to `undeclared_relation` or `subject_type_not_allowed`. Splitting one `TupleError` code into a different HTTP status class than its four siblings would also force every client to branch on status _and_ code instead of code alone, for a distinction this API's own shape doesn't actually support.
**Revisit if:** A future phase adds a real `GET /namespaces/:ns` (or similar) endpoint — at that point `404` becomes meaningful for that specific route (namespace fetch), though it still wouldn't retroactively apply to a tuple write's own error shape, which would stay exactly as reasoned above.

## D-054 — The `ADMIN_API_KEY` comparison uses `crypto.timingSafeEqual`, not `===`

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `src/api/auth.ts` compares the supplied bearer token against `env.ADMIN_API_KEY` with `node:crypto`'s `timingSafeEqual` (after a length check, which `timingSafeEqual` itself requires — unequal-length buffers throw rather than compare), never JavaScript's `===`/`string.length` short-circuiting comparison.
**Alternative rejected:** A plain `supplied === env.ADMIN_API_KEY` string comparison — simpler, and correct for every functional test in this phase's own suite (which can't distinguish the two by response _content_, only by response _timing_).
**Why it lost:** `===` on strings short-circuits at the first mismatched character, which means comparison time correlates with how many leading bytes of a guess happen to match the real key — a timing side channel an attacker with enough network samples can exploit to recover the key byte-by-byte without ever seeing a successful response. This is a single-admin-key HTTP API with a real, if narrow, network attack surface (anyone who can reach the deployed service can attempt this), so the standard mitigation (compare in an order-independent, data-length-only-dependent way) costs nothing here — `timingSafeEqual` is a Node built-in, not a new dependency — and directly closes a known class of vulnerability class that a purely functional test suite would never catch (every test that supplies a correct or incorrect key gets the right _answer_ either way; only a timing measurement would show the difference, which this project doesn't have infrastructure to test for, making prevention-by-construction the only real option here).
**Revisit if:** Never, for a secret-comparison path — this is close to a strict-dominance choice (no real downside) rather than a tradeoff to revisit.

## D-055 — `docs/github-governance.md`'s Steps 1-4 confirmed applied by direct owner confirmation, not by tool inspection

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** §9 Phase 8's exit criterion — "`docs/github-governance.md`'s steps are confirmed applied (or explicitly deferred with a stated reason) and the PR for this phase says which" — is resolved as **confirmed applied**: the repo owner worked through Steps 1-4 (branch protection for `main` including squash-only merges, the three required status checks, conversation resolution, no force-push/no deletion, applied to admins too; Dependabot auto-merge allowed; Dependabot security updates, secret scanning, and push protection enabled) directly in the GitHub UI and confirmed completion.
**Alternative rejected:** Confirm this criterion using only tool-based evidence gathered during this session.
**Why it lost:** A real search for a GitHub MCP tool capable of reading branch-protection-rule/ruleset details, the Dependabot auto-merge repository toggle, or Code security and analysis settings turned up nothing — this is a genuine, confirmed tooling gap (`ToolSearch` across "branch protection repository settings rules" and similar queries found no match), not a shortcut taken to avoid the work. One piece of this checklist _was_ independently, directly verified by this session's own tooling: "Require conversation resolution before merging" is genuinely active, proven by a real `405 Repository rule violations found` error encountered while attempting to merge PR #16 in Phase 7, before its unresolved review thread was resolved — first-party evidence, not a claim taken on faith. For the remaining admin-only settings (branch protection specifics, Dependabot auto-merge, Code security and analysis), §9 Phase 8's own exit criterion explicitly allows either tool-confirmed verification or "explicitly deferred with a stated reason" — this project chose to resolve it the stronger way instead: asking the repo owner directly, who then completed the steps and confirmed it, rather than deferring an unresolved criterion into a later phase.
**Revisit if:** A GitHub MCP tool capable of reading these settings becomes available in a later session — at that point, independently re-verify all of Steps 1-4 the same direct-tool-evidence way conversation-resolution was already confirmed, rather than continuing to rely on the one-time owner confirmation recorded here.

## D-056 — `@fastify/rate-limit` added to close a real CodeQL finding, per explicit user approval

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 8 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `@fastify/rate-limit` added as a real dependency (outside build spec §2's stated stack, so asked first per rule 6 — approved). Registered globally on the Fastify instance with a 100-request/minute default, overridden per-route: 300/minute on `GET /health` (real load-balancer polling needs headroom), 20/minute on the three `ADMIN_API_KEY`-gated write routes (defense-in-depth against key-guessing and write-flooding even from a caller who holds a valid key). A dedicated `rate_limited` `ApiErrorCode` (429) was added to `src/api/errors.ts` (same additive pattern as D-052's `invalid_request`) so a rate-limited response carries this API's one standard error envelope, never the plugin's own default body shape.
**Alternative rejected (build vs. buy):** A small in-house in-memory per-IP limiter — offered as an option specifically to avoid a new dependency. Rejected by explicit user choice: the standard, actively-maintained Fastify-official package is the better tradeoff here (correct edge cases — IPv6 subnetting, draft-spec headers, ban thresholds — that a from-scratch implementation would either skip or have to re-derive and re-test) for a security-relevant control that's cheap to get from a well-audited source.
**A real, non-obvious integration bug found and fixed while wiring this in, not by reading documentation alone:** registering the plugin via a fire-and-forget `void app.register(rateLimit, opts)` immediately followed by synchronous route declarations (the pattern every other registration/hook call in this file already uses) silently produced a rate limiter that never limited anything — confirmed live via a minimal reproduction outside this codebase entirely (a bare `Fastify()` instance, `@fastify/rate-limit`, one route, six requests, real sockets via `fetch`, not `.inject()`): zero `x-ratelimit-*` headers, zero `429`s, at any request count, with no error raised anywhere. Fastify only wires a plugin's `onRoute` hook (how `@fastify/rate-limit` attaches itself to each route) once that plugin's own async registration has actually resolved; declaring routes synchronously right after an un-awaited `.register()` call races that resolution and the routes are built before the hook exists to catch them — with the plugin's own `settings` (max/timeWindow) accepted silently, giving no signal anything was wrong. Fixed by making `buildServer` itself `async` and `await`-ing the one `app.register(rateLimit, ...)` call before any route is declared. A second, related bug surfaced immediately after fixing the first: `@fastify/rate-limit`'s `errorResponseBuilder` option is a value to `throw`, not a response body to return (its own default implementation returns a real `Error` with `.statusCode` set) — returning `rateLimitedError(...).body` directly from it, as if it worked like every other constructor in `src/api/errors.ts`, got the object thrown as a non-`Error` value and fell through to this file's own generic `>= 500` `internalError()` branch, misreporting a rate limit as a `500` instead of a `429`. Fixed by returning a real `Error` carrying a `retryAfterSeconds` marker, recognized and reshaped into `rateLimitedError`'s own envelope inside `setErrorHandler`.
**Independently verified, not trusted from the fix alone:** a dedicated `test/unit/api/rate-limit.test.ts` (written directly, not delegated — a small, focused addition responding to a CI finding discovered after both `test-author` Phase 8 passes had already completed) sends 21 real requests to a write-gated route and asserts the first 20 never return `429` while the 21st does, with the standard `rate_limited` envelope; a sibling test sends 20 requests to an unauthenticated read route and confirms none trip the (much higher) global default, proving the per-route override is real and not just a higher global number. Also confirmed against the real built CLI (`npm run build && node dist/cli/index.js serve`) against real local Postgres — real `x-ratelimit-limit: 300`/`x-ratelimit-remaining: 298` headers on a real `GET /health` request.
**Revisit if:** A future deployment runs multiple instances behind a shared load balancer — this plugin's default in-memory store is per-process, so the effective limit multiplies by instance count; `@fastify/rate-limit` supports a Redis-backed store for that case (`RedisStore`, already bundled in the package), not yet needed for this project's own stated single-instance, non-distributed scope (§1's own non-goals).

## D-057 — The Phase 9 example schema is one file, all four namespaces together, separate from Phase 1's own per-namespace CHECKPOINT fixtures

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `schema/example.authz` is new — one file declaring `org`, `group`, `folder`, and `document` together. Phase 1's own `schema/{document,folder,group,org}.authz` (each namespace alone, each deliberately compiling standalone per their own doc comments — see those files) are untouched, kept as-is as historical CHECKPOINT evidence.
**Alternative rejected:** Reuse or extend Phase 1's four separate files as the Phase 9 demo schema directly.
**Why it lost:** Phase 1's files were built to prove something narrower and different — that each namespace compiles in isolation, including the specific case (`folder.authz`) where a tuple-to-userset rule's target namespace is itself, self-contained, needing nothing else present. They're intentionally minimal (no `parent` relation shared consistently between `folder`/`document`, no exclusion, no intersection) — exactly right for what Phase 1 needed to show, wrong for §9 Phase 9's own ask ("a realistic tuple graph... a mix of direct and inherited grants"). Extending them in place would have meant either breaking Phase 1's own "compiles standalone" claim (once `document`'s `parent->edit` needs `folder` to exist in the same compilation unit) or silently changing what CHECKPOINT evidence those files represent. A single new file keeps both true at once: Phase 1's evidence stays exactly what it was when demonstrated, and Phase 9 gets a schema actually shaped for what it needs — one `authz schema publish schema/example.authz` publishing all four namespaces together, which also matters directly for the "under 10 minutes" exit criterion (one command, not four).
**Revisit if:** Never expected — these serve genuinely different purposes and neither substitutes for the other.

## D-058 — Every rewrite-rule kind, deliberately, in the demo schema — not just the ones §9 Phase 9 names by name

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `schema/example.authz` exercises union (every `view`/`edit` permission), exclusion (`org.view = member - banned`), intersection (`folder.sensitive_review = (viewer|edit) & sensitive_reviewer`), tuple-to-userset (`folder`/`document`'s `parent->edit`/`parent->view`), and nested-group-as-userset-subject (`group.member: user | group#member`, nested two levels in the seeded graph) — five real mechanics, not the one §9 Phase 9's own text names explicitly (two levels of group nesting).
**Alternative rejected:** A minimal schema satisfying only the one named requirement — union, tuple-to-userset, and the two-level group nesting, nothing else.
**Why it lost:** §9 Phase 9's exit criterion is about proving tuple-to-userset works, not an upper bound on what the demo may show — and a demo that only ever exercises two of the engine's four rewrite-rule kinds would leave `docs/RELATIONS.md`, the report-designer screens, and the README's own worked examples with nothing real to point at for exclusion or intersection, forcing either a second, disconnected example schema (drift risk — now two "the" example schemas) or hypothetical/invented illustrations for those two mechanics specifically, in a project whose whole discipline is never inventing an example when a real one is achievable. One schema, one seeded graph, every mechanic demonstrated with a real, verifiable outcome (see the check results the main agent captured directly against real Postgres before handing anything to `report-designer`) costs a handful of extra relations and tuples and removes that gap entirely.
**Revisit if:** Never expected for this repository's own demo; a client engagement's real schema (see `docs/DELIVERY.md`) should of course be shaped by their actual permission model, not by a desire to demonstrate every mechanic.

## D-059 — Demo identifiers use underscores, never hyphens — found live, not by reading the grammar first

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** Every object/namespace id in the seeded demo graph (`eng_docs`, `eng_backend_interns`, `finance_docs`, …) uses underscores. No hyphens anywhere.
**Why:** `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN` (`src/schema/dsl/types.ts`, Phase 1) is `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$` — lowercase letters, digits, and underscores only, no hyphens — and it governs every identifier in this system, not just schema names: namespace names, relation/permission names, and (per `src/store/tuples.ts`'s own `validateIdentifiers`) every tuple's object/subject id too. This was not caught by reading the grammar first; it was caught by actually running `scripts/seed-example.ts` against real Postgres with the original, more conventionally-styled hyphenated ids (`eng-docs`, `finance-docs`, …) and watching the very first nested-group tuple write fail with `subject id 'eng-backend' is not a valid identifier`. Every identifier in `schema/example.authz`, `scripts/seed-example.ts`, and every doc/screen that names one of these ids was written (or in the schema file's case, was already hyphen-free by luck) to match.
**Alternative rejected:** Relax `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN` to allow hyphens, so more conventionally-styled real-world ids (`eng-backend`, common in actual Slack/GitHub-style team naming) would work without translation.
**Why it lost:** This is exactly the kind of schema-wide grammar change build spec rule 0/§9 Phase 1 already settled and closed — reopening it in Phase 9, for cosmetic reasons, to accommodate one demo's naming preference, is the same scope-creep pattern already rejected once in this file (see D-041's sibling reasoning). The underscore-only rule has been load-bearing since Phase 1 (every relation/permission/namespace name in every schema this project has ever compiled already follows it) and changing it now would mean re-verifying every existing schema file, every compiled-config fixture in every test, and the DSL's own documented grammar in `.claude/commands/build-authz-service.md` §5 — for a demo that works perfectly well with underscored names instead.
**Revisit if:** A real client engagement's actual object ids are hyphenated (e.g. Slack channel ids) and translating them at ingestion time proves genuinely awkward — that's a real, separate question for `docs/DELIVERY.md`'s own scope, not something to retrofit into this repository's grammar for a demo's sake.

## D-060 — `authz soundness run` writes real, permanent rows into whatever database it's pointed at — including a stranger's own local demo database

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled (this entry's own "revisit if" trigger fired 2026-08-16 — see D-063)
**Update:** The `--dry-run` mode D-063 adds is exactly the trigger this entry's own "revisit if" named. The README's walkthrough now uses `--dry-run` by default (§ below still describes why the _underlying_ behavior — a real, non-dry-run `authz soundness run` genuinely does write permanent rows — is correct and unchanged; only the walkthrough's own advice changed, per D-063).
**Decision:** The README's own "try it yourself" walkthrough runs `authz soundness run` directly against the same local Postgres database the demo graph was just seeded into, and says so plainly (see the README's own "harmless, but expect to see them" note) rather than routing a stranger around the issue or hiding it.
**Why this needed a decision at all:** `src/soundness/runner.ts`'s `runSoundnessFuzz` calls the real `publishSchema`/`writeTuple` against the real `pool` it's given (confirmed by reading `runner.ts` directly, then reproducing live: a fresh, fully-wiped demo database, seeded, then one `authz soundness run` — `GET /health` afterward showed the four real demo namespaces plus several randomly-suffixed ones like `doc_l9dcbt` that were never part of the seeded graph). D-045 (Phase 7) already named this exact risk and solved it for CI specifically (`soundness.yml`'s own ephemeral `services:` Postgres, never the shared project database) — but Phase 7 had no "a stranger's own local demo database" scenario to consider yet, because that scenario didn't exist until this phase's own README walkthrough created it.
**Alternative rejected:** Instruct the reader to run the fuzz harness against a second, separate database from the one they seeded the demo graph into, keeping the demo database permanently pristine.
**Why it lost:** Splitting the walkthrough across two `DATABASE_URL`s adds real friction (a second `createdb`, a second `.env`-juggling step) to a flow whose own exit criterion is "under 10 minutes from a clean clone," for a cost that is genuinely cosmetic — extra rows in `namespace_configs`/`relation_tuples` a curious reader might notice if they go looking, never a correctness issue, never anything the check engine itself would answer differently because of. Naming the behavior plainly and moving on matches this project's own established discipline (rule 4: an answer a security reviewer wouldn't accept isn't one this project accepts from itself) applied here to documentation completeness rather than a design choice — the honest move is disclosure, not a workaround that trades real friction for a purely cosmetic gain.
**Revisit if:** A future phase adds a `--dry-run`/in-memory-only mode to `authz soundness run` for exactly this local-exploration use case, or a documented recommendation to use a disposable per-session database becomes this project's own convention generally (it isn't yet, elsewhere) — either would be a real reason to change the walkthrough's own advice, not just its wording.

## D-061 — `docs/DELIVERY.md` written in Phase 9, despite §9 Phase 9's own paragraph not naming it

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `docs/DELIVERY.md` (§13's content) is written as part of this phase, alongside `docs/RELATIONS.md`/`docs/CONSISTENCY.md`/the README rewrite.
**Alternative rejected:** Leave it unwritten, since §9 Phase 9's own paragraph lists exactly three deliverables ("`docs/RELATIONS.md`... `docs/CONSISTENCY.md`... README per §11") and never names `docs/DELIVERY.md`.
**Why it lost:** §3's own repo layout table lists `DELIVERY.md` alongside `RELATIONS.md`/`CONSISTENCY.md` as a doc "added per phase," and no other phase in §9 claims it — Phase 9 is the only remaining "docs, demo" phase this repository has, and §13's own content is fully specified and ready to use as-is. Leaving it permanently unwritten would mean either a phantom entry in the repo layout that never materializes, or an implicit tenth phase this build spec never actually names. The cost of writing it now is small (§13 already provides the complete content) and it directly serves the same "honest positioning, and it goes in the README" instruction (§1) the README rewrite's own honest-positioning section already links to it from.
**Revisit if:** Never expected — this is a scope-completion call, not a design tradeoff with a real alternative to reconsider later.

## D-062 — Four `test/isolation/` `.todo()`s stay open at Phase 9, with no further phase left to attribute them to

**Date:** 2026-08-15 · **Phase:** 9 · **Status:** settled (closed 2026-08-16)
**Resolved:** All four implemented for real by `test-author`, same day, on the same PR, per direct user instruction ("fix the open gap") rather than waiting for the "revisit if" trigger below. (1) proven via a DB-free capturing `Pool`/`PoolClient` double that records every `query()` call's exact SQL text and params across `publishSchema`/`writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`, asserting no `INJECTION_PAYLOAD_CORPUS` payload or legitimate field value ever appears as a literal substring in generated SQL — only behind a `$`-numbered placeholder. (2)-(3) implemented as genuine `fast-check` property tests, 2,000 runs each, against `compileSchema` (namespace names) and `writeTuple` (subject/object ids) respectively — not the identical oracle for both: writing test (2) surfaced a real, narrow gap between the _published_ grammar (`IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`/`MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH`) and what the parser _enforces_ for a namespace name specifically (`namespace`/`relation`/`permission` are reserved words, rejected despite matching the pattern) — folded correctly into the property's own oracle rather than filtered out, and `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`'s own doc comment (`src/schema/dsl/types.ts`) updated in the same pass to state this carve-out explicitly, since documentation that undersold real enforcement was itself a small, real accuracy gap worth closing while already there. (4) pins the exact boundary (63 chars accepted, 64 rejected, specifically for length, not character class) for both the namespace-name and subject/object-id paths. Independently verified by the main agent, not accepted on `test-author`'s report alone: read the full diff; ran the file directly (`vitest run --config vitest.config.ts`, 11/11 passed, zero `it.todo()` remaining anywhere in `test/isolation/`); ran the full `npm run verify` pipeline (221/221, up from 217/4-todo); performed an independent fail-check distinct from `test-author`'s own four (an off-by-one in `parser.ts`'s own length check, a different source location than any the subagent had already touched) and confirmed it fails for the right reason, then confirmed byte-identical restoration via `git diff`/`md5sum`.
**Original entry, for the record (superseded by the resolution above):**
**What's open:** `test/isolation/identifier-and-tuple-validation.fuzz.test.ts` carries four real, un-implemented `it.todo()`s: (1) "the compiled namespace config never contains an interpolated raw namespace or relation name in any generated SQL/DDL — only a parameter placeholder or a value that already passed validation"; (2) "for 2,000 random generated strings, a namespace name is accepted if and only if it matches the published identifier grammar, with no third outcome (crash, hang, silent truncation)"; (3) the same property for a subject/object id; (4) "an identifier at exactly the documented length limit is accepted, and one character over is rejected." §11's Definition of done requires every `it.todo()` in `test/isolation/` to be either un-skipped and passing, or attributed to a phase still explicitly open in `PROGRESS.md` — Phase 9 is the last phase this build spec names, so there is no later phase left to attribute these four to.
**Alternative rejected:** Silently leave them un-mentioned in Phase 9's own `PROGRESS.md` section, on the theory that they predate this phase and aren't this phase's fault.
**Why that lost:** This project's own standing rule (quoted directly inside `test/isolation/differential-soundness.fuzz.test.ts`'s own comments) is "don't leave a satisfiable `.todo()` stale" — the same discipline this project has applied at every prior phase boundary (see `test/isolation/README.md`'s own account of the tuple-validation `.todo()` gap being carried forward honestly, then later explicitly picked up and closed). Reaching the last named phase with four of these still open is a real, disclosed gap, not a design tradeoff with a defensible "why it lost" — the honest move is to say so plainly in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 9 section (open questions) rather than let the Definition-of-done checklist read as fully satisfied when it isn't quite.
**Why these four specifically, and why they weren't picked up now:** (1) needs a real assertion against `src/schema/publish.ts`'s actual generated SQL text, which no existing helper in this suite extracts; (2)-(4) are `fast-check`-driven property tests against the identifier grammar (2,000 random strings each, a Postgres-length-limit boundary case) that need their own generator work, not a quick fill-in — the same class of work Phase 2's own `.todo()`s were, which took a dedicated pass to close properly rather than being rushed into an already-large phase's final review. Opening a ninth build-spec phase to close four tests, this late, would be a larger scope change than the gap itself.
**Revisit if:** Any future work touches `test/isolation/identifier-and-tuple-validation.fuzz.test.ts`, `src/schema/publish.ts`'s SQL generation, or the identifier grammar itself — that's the natural moment to un-skip and implement these four for real, following the exact precedent already set by this project's earlier `.todo()` gap closures.

## D-063 — `authz soundness run --dry-run`: the same real fuzz cycle, with every row it created deleted before returning

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (D-060's own "revisit if" trigger fired) · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `SoundnessRunOptions.dryRun` (`src/soundness/runner.ts`), wired to `authz soundness run --dry-run`. When `true`, `runSoundnessFuzz` runs the exact same real cycle as always — real `publishSchema`, real `writeTuple` calls, real checks against both resolvers, a real (if briefly-lived) `soundness_runs` row — computes the exact same verdict, and returns the exact same `SoundnessRunResult` shape (including a real `id` from the row that briefly existed). Before returning, it deletes every row it created: the `soundness_runs` row, every tuple in the generated fixture (`deleteTuple`, already idempotent), and every `(namespace, version)` `publishSchema` published (`deletePublishedNamespaceVersion`, new — see below). `write_log` is deliberately left untouched. The non-dry-run path (default, unchanged) is provably identical to before this option existed — same statements, same persistence, same return shape.
**The one genuinely new piece of infrastructure this needed:** `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion(pool, namespace, version)` in `src/schema/publish.ts` — the first time anything in this codebase ever deletes a `namespace_configs` row. Every other part of this system treats a published namespace version as permanent, append-only history (no `publishOne` ever overwrites one; nothing else deletes one). This function is a narrow, deliberate exception, scoped to exactly one caller (this dry-run cleanup) and documented as such in its own doc comment — not exposed via the CLI or API surface, not a general "unpublish a namespace" feature. It's safe specifically because a dry run's published version is synthetic fuzz-fixture data, created and destroyed within the same function call, never observed as real schema history by anything in between (no other query, no other caller, ever sees it exist except transiently inside this one `runSoundnessFuzz` invocation).
**Alternative rejected (build vs. avoid):** Wrap the whole cycle in one Postgres transaction on a single client and `ROLLBACK` instead of `COMMIT` at the end — the cleanest-sounding approach, since a rollback needs no bespoke delete logic at all.
**Why it lost:** `publishSchema`/`writeTuple`/`productionCheck` each manage their own connection and their own `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` internally (`pool.connect()` per call) — making them composable into one caller-managed outer transaction would mean either changing their signatures to accept an already-connected client instead of a `Pool` (relying on Postgres's "nested `BEGIN` is a warning, not an error, and silently continues the outer transaction" behavior — fragile, undocumented-as-a-contract, and risky to build a real feature on top of), or duplicating their insert/validation logic in a parallel, dry-run-only code path (a drift risk: if `publishOne`'s SQL or `writeTuple`'s validation logic changes later, the duplicate would silently fall out of sync with what it's supposed to be dry-running). Explicit, targeted `DELETE`s of exactly what was tracked as created — reusing the already-proven `deleteTuple` and one new, narrowly-scoped function — touches none of the resolvers' or the store's "must be exactly right" internals (§2) and can't drift, since it calls the same real functions everything else calls, in the same order, then cleans up after.
**Why `write_log` is deliberately untouched:** `write_log` is this system's own honest, append-only ledger of every write that has ever really happened (§4/§6.3) — a dry run's tuple writes and the deletes that immediately undo them are real writes that really happened, in the same sense every other row in that table is. Removing them would make the ledger lie about the database's own history for the sake of a tidier row count in a table nobody browses directly the way they'd browse `namespace_configs`/`relation_tuples`. Left alone, deliberately, and said so in three separate doc comments (`SoundnessRunOptions.dryRun`, `cleanupDryRunArtifacts`, this entry) so nobody "fixes" it later on a hunch.
**Cleanup is best-effort, not transactional, and that's a deliberate, disclosed tradeoff:** every deletion category (the run row, every tuple, every namespace version) is attempted independently — one category failing doesn't block the others — and a cleanup failure is never allowed to mask or replace a genuine run failure already in flight (an `AggregateError` naming exactly what failed is thrown only when there's no prior error to protect). This means a process crash or connection loss mid-cleanup can, in principle, leave partial fixture rows behind — a real, accepted limitation, matching this project's own existing acceptance that nothing in `runSoundnessFuzz` is crash-atomic today either (the non-dry-run path has never guaranteed atomicity across the whole run).
**Independently verified, twice over, not accepted from the implementing subagent's report alone:** the main agent independently ran the exact same-seed dry-run-vs-real comparison against real local Postgres (`falseGrantCount`/`falseDenyCount`/`verdict`/`divergences` byte-identical between the two, `namespace_configs`/`relation_tuples`/`soundness_runs` counts unchanged after the dry run, `write_log` correctly advanced) and performed an original fail-check distinct from the subagent's own four — forced `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion` itself to always fail while leaving tuple/soundness_runs cleanup untouched, and confirmed the real database ended up exactly as predicted by the "each category cleans up independently" design: `relation_tuples`/`soundness_runs` correctly returned to zero, `namespace_configs` correctly retained exactly the rows whose deletion was forced to fail, and the thrown `AggregateError` named exactly those failures — then restored the file and confirmed byte-identical via `md5sum`. Also independently smoke-tested the real built CLI (`authz soundness run --dry-run`, `--dry-run --format markdown`) against real Postgres, confirming the `--format markdown`/`json` contract ("stdout is the report and nothing else," required by `.github/workflows/soundness.yml`'s PR-comment capture) is untouched by the dry-run note, which only appears in `--format text`.
**Revisit if:** A future deployment needs this to be crash-atomic (a process dying mid-cleanup genuinely matters for some new use case) — at that point, revisit whether the transaction-composability alternative above is worth its cost, now with a real, specific requirement driving it rather than a hypothetical one.

## D-064 — `/check`/`/expand` gated behind `ADMIN_API_KEY`, not left open to any caller

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo-audit finding, HIGH) · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `requireAdminAuth` (`src/api/server.ts`) now runs as the `preHandler` on `/check` and `/expand`, exactly as it already did on `/tuples` (POST/DELETE) and `/schema/publish`. Both routes get their own, more generous rate-limit budget (`gatedReadRateLimit`, 200/minute) than the write routes' 20/minute — a real integrated caller is expected to call `check` far more often than it writes — but the same admin key gate applies to both. `/schema/compile` and `/health` remain the only two unauthenticated routes, unchanged.
**Why this needed a decision at all:** these two routes were unauthenticated from the moment Phase 8 built them — the §9 exit criterion `checkAdminAuth` was written against ("an unauthenticated write attempt is rejected") only ever named the three write routes, and `/check`/`/expand` were built, tested, and shipped open, without "should a check/expand also require a key" ever being treated as a live question. The full-repo audit (2026-08-16) raised it as a HIGH finding: `performCheck` answers "does subject X have permission Y on object Z" for any caller, no key required — and `expand` reveals a permission's entire resolved membership tree (every direct subject, every nested userset) for any object/relation pair a caller names. Build spec rule 10 ("this system never says a permission is granted because it seems like it should be... every allow decision must be able to produce the path that justified it") is about proving a grant is real; it says nothing about who is allowed to _ask_. In a deployment where the tuple graph itself is sensitive — which subject can see which document, which group nests which other group — an open `/check`/`/expand` is an enumeration and information-disclosure surface: a caller with no credentials at all can map out real relationships and group membership one query at a time, without ever needing to write anything.
**Alternative rejected:** Leave `/check`/`/expand` unauthenticated, on the theory that a _read_ isn't the kind of operation build spec rule 10 and the Phase 8 exit criterion were written to guard, and that a real deployment would put its own auth layer in front of this service anyway — the same reasoning already used, correctly, for `/schema/compile` and `/health`.
**Why it lost:** `/schema/compile` and `/health` are safe to leave open because neither answers a question about _this deployment's actual tuple data_ — `/schema/compile` only compiles whatever source the caller itself supplies (nothing stored is read), and `/health`'s namespace list is schema metadata (names/versions), never a relationship. `/check`/`/expand` are categorically different: every response is derived from the real, live `relation_tuples` graph a real deployment has populated — exactly the sensitive data this whole project exists to gate access to. Trusting a deployment's outer infrastructure to add auth in front of this service is exactly the kind of implicit, unverified assumption rule 10 already rules out for grant decisions; applying the same discipline here, to who may ask a grant question at all, isn't a new standard, it's the existing one.
**Revisit if:** A deployment genuinely needs a caller class that can check/expand without holding the full admin key (e.g. a scoped, read-only service credential) — that's a real reason to add a second, narrower credential tier, not a reason to reopen leaving these routes unauthenticated.

## D-065 — auth must run before rate-limit counting, and `trustProxy: true` in `Fastify(...)`

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo-audit finding, HIGH) · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** Every gated route's `config.rateLimit` sets `hook: 'preHandler'` (`@fastify/rate-limit` v11's own option; default is `onRequest`), so the plugin's own `onRoute` hook appends its counting handler onto the route's already-declared `preHandler: requireAdminAuth` array instead of running as an earlier, separate `onRequest` hook ahead of it. `trustProxy: true` is set on the `Fastify(...)` constructor itself, so `request.ip` (the rate-limiter's default `keyGenerator` input) resolves from `X-Forwarded-For` rather than the immediate socket peer.
**Why this needed a decision at all:** the full-repo audit found that with the rate-limit plugin's default `onRequest` hook, `@fastify/rate-limit` counts and can reject a request _before_ Fastify ever reaches the route's own `preHandler` — every request against a gated route, authorized or not, consumed from the same budget purely by _arriving_. Confirmed directly by reading the installed plugin's own source (`node_modules/@fastify/rate-limit/index.js`'s `addRouteRateHook`), not assumed from its public docs: with the default hook, a flood of wrong-key (or no-key) requests could exhaust a write route's entire budget before a single one of them was ever compared against the real admin key — locking out the genuine admin along with the flood, an availability bug that defeats the very "defense-in-depth against key-guessing and write-flooding" `writeRateLimit`'s own doc comment says it exists for. Separately, `trustProxy` defaults to `false`, meaning `request.ip` would resolve to Railway's (or any reverse proxy's) own address for every distinct real caller alike — collapsing every caller behind the same proxy onto one shared rate-limit budget, so one caller could exhaust it for everyone else reachable through that proxy.
**Verified live, not just read from the plugin's docs:** wrote a dedicated regression test (`test/unit/api/server.test.ts`, "D-065: failed-auth requests never consume a gated route's rate-limit budget") sending 25 wrong-key requests — five more than `writeRateLimit`'s own `max: 20` — against `POST /tuples`, asserting every one returns 401 (never 429) and that `writeTuple` itself is never called, then confirming a request right after, carrying the correct key, still succeeds. Before the `hook: 'preHandler'` fix, request 21 in that sequence would already have been a `429`, not a `401` — the plugin's own default-hook counting has no notion of "this request never even attempted a key" and counts it regardless.
**Alternative rejected:** A global `app.addHook('onRequest', ...)` that checks the URL against a hardcoded list of gated routes and skips rate-limit counting for those specifically.
**Why it lost:** this is exactly the same fragile, easy-to-typo URL-matching pattern `requireAdminAuth`'s own doc comment already rejects in favor of route-level `preHandler` declarations — a new gated route added later, with no corresponding entry in a separately-maintained hook list, would silently get the wrong ordering, with no type error to catch it. `hook: 'preHandler'` keeps the ordering declared right at each route's own registration, next to the `preHandler: requireAdminAuth` it depends on — the same "declared next to what it governs" reasoning `requireAdminAuth`'s own doc comment already used once.
**Revisit if:** A deployment genuinely answers raw client sockets directly rather than sitting behind a reverse proxy (contradicting `serve.ts`'s own "reached from outside the process... a platform like Railway" assumption) — `trustProxy: true` would then let a client spoof its own `X-Forwarded-For` to evade its real rate-limit key, and should be revisited for that specific deployment shape.
**Update (2026-08-16, D-082):** The "Revisit if" clause above named the wrong trigger. A second full-repo audit found the spoofing risk doesn't require a deployment shape change at all — it fires in exactly the single-reverse-proxy shape this decision already assumes, because `trustProxy: true` means "trust every hop `X-Forwarded-For` claims," not "trust exactly the one real hop." A client can prepend its own fabricated address ahead of the real proxy-appended one and have `request.ip` resolve to the fabrication. See D-082 for the fix (`trustProxy: 1`) and live reproduction.

## D-066 — A dry-run cleanup failure never discards an already-computed `SoundnessRunResult`

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo-audit finding, HIGH) · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** In `runSoundnessFuzz` (`src/soundness/runner.ts`), the success-path call to `cleanupIfDryRun()` — the best-effort teardown `SoundnessRunOptions.dryRun` runs just before returning — is now wrapped in its own `try`/`catch`. A cleanup failure there is logged via `console.error` (naming the seed and the verdict that was about to be returned) and the function still returns the real, already-computed `result`. Before this fix, that same call was a bare `await cleanupIfDryRun();`: a thrown cleanup failure fell straight into `runSoundnessFuzz`'s own outer `catch` block, indistinguishable there from a genuine "no verdict was ever computed" failure.
**Why this needed a decision at all:** the full-repo audit traced what that indistinguishability actually does to a caller. `src/cli/commands/soundness.ts`'s own `catch` maps _any_ thrown error from `runSoundnessFuzz` to exit code 3 and a generic `Postgres: ...` message — the same code `src/report/exitCodes.ts`'s own doc comment reserves specifically for "infrastructure failure... surfaced before any verdict exists to map." A dry run whose fuzz cycle succeeded — including one that found a real, critical `false_grant` (verdict `unsound`, meant to be exit code 1 and block a PR) — would, if the cleanup step immediately afterward hit an unrelated transient failure (a dropped connection, a lock timeout), silently downgrade that already-computed, already-correct verdict to a bare "infrastructure failure" and lose it entirely: no markdown report printed, no exit code 1, nothing pointing at the real finding. This is a strictly worse outcome than the cleanup failure alone — a genuine soundness violation reported as a shrug-worthy infra hiccup, exactly the kind of masked signal build spec rule 10's "no implicit grants, no 'probably fine'" discipline exists to prevent, applied here to _reporting_ a violation rather than to _detecting_ one.
**Alternative rejected:** Leave the bare `await cleanupIfDryRun();` as-is, on the theory that a cleanup failure is rare enough (best-effort, independently-attempted-per-category, per D-063) not to warrant special-casing, and that exit code 3 is at least a _visible_ failure a human would investigate rather than a silent pass.
**Why it lost:** "visible but wrong" is still wrong, and worse than useless here — a human seeing exit code 3 on a soundness run has no way to tell "the database was unreachable, nothing was ever proven" apart from "a real security-relevant verdict was computed and then lost by a filing-cabinet fire on the way out," and would reasonably treat the two identically (rerun and move on), the second of which risks quietly dropping a critical finding until some later run reproduces it, if the same transient cleanup condition doesn't recur every time. `runSoundnessFuzz`'s own doc comment already draws this exact line for its `catch` block's _second_ cleanup-failure case ("a failure here must never replace `err`... whether `err` is a genuine run failure... or itself came from the cleanup call on the success path above, `err` is always the one thing this function is already about to throw") — this fix applies the identical "never let cleanup's own outcome overwrite the thing that actually matters" reasoning to the success path, which the original code simply hadn't yet been made to follow.
**Verified live:** a dedicated DB-free unit test (`test/unit/soundness/runner-dry-run-cleanup-failure.test.ts`) mocks every I/O dependency `runSoundnessFuzz` has (via `vi.spyOn`, the same established pattern `test/unit/api/server.test.ts`/`test/unit/cli/soundness.test.ts` already use) to force a real `runSoundnessFuzz(pool, { dryRun: true })` cycle to succeed and then have `deleteTuple` reject during cleanup — confirms the call resolves (never rejects) with the real, correctly-shaped result and that the failure is logged via `console.error`, not swallowed silently. Fail-checked directly: reverted the `try`/`catch` back to a bare `await cleanupIfDryRun();` and confirmed the test fails for the right reason (the simulated cleanup error propagates uncaught), then restored and confirmed byte-identical via `md5sum`.
**Revisit if:** Never expected — this closes a real gap in an existing design (D-063's own success-path teardown), not a tradeoff with a live alternative to reconsider later.

## D-067 — Two independent unbounded-native-recursion DoS paths in the schema DSL, closed with two different fixes for two different reasons

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo audit finding) · **Status:** settled
**What was wrong:** A full-repo audit found two independent, unauthenticated denial-of-service paths reachable from `POST /schema/compile` (`src/api/server.ts` — unauthenticated by design, but recursion-depth DoS was never itself reasoned through when that call was made). Both were confirmed live before any fix, not assumed from the audit's own description: (1) `src/schema/dsl/parser.ts`'s `parseAtom`/`parseTerm`/`parseExpression` (mutually recursive, one native call-stack frame per level of `(` nesting in a permission expression, no depth counter) threw a raw `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` at 3,000 nested parens (a ~6KB source string) in this environment — squarely inside the audit's own reported 3,000-5,000 range. (2) `src/schema/dsl/compiler.ts`'s `checkCircularPermissions` walked its permission-dependency graph via a second, entirely separate native recursion (`dfs`, one frame per `permission pN = pN+1` chain edge, no `(` nesting anywhere), and overflowed independently somewhere between 5,000 and 10,000 permissions in this environment (the exact crash point varied a little between otherwise-similar repro scripts, as native call-stack limits generally do — but every measurement was well inside the audit's own reported 8,500-9,600 range or below it, never above) (a captured stack trace confirmed the crash originates entirely inside `dfs`'s own self-recursive call — old line 336 — with `path.push` at old line 329 as the frame that actually tripped the limit, never once touching `parser.ts`) — proving directly that a fix scoped only to Bug A's paren-nesting would not have closed this second path, exactly as the audit warned.
**The fix, Bug A (`src/schema/dsl/parser.ts`, `src/schema/dsl/types.ts`, `src/schema/dsl/errors.ts`):** `ParserState` gained a `parenDepth` field, incremented/checked in `parseAtom` on each `(` and decremented on the matching `)`. Past `MAX_EXPRESSION_NESTING_DEPTH` (`types.ts`, alongside `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`/`MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH`) the parser throws the existing `SchemaParseError` machinery with a new `SchemaErrorCode`, `'expression_nesting_too_deep'` (`errors.ts`) — a clean, located rejection, never a raw `RangeError`. **100** was chosen as the ceiling: every real schema this project has ever written (`schema/example.authz`, every worked example in `.claude/commands/build-authz-service.md` §5) nests at most 1-2 levels deep, so 100 is generous by roughly two orders of magnitude while remaining trivially cheap on the real call stack (100 nesting levels costs on the order of a few hundred stack frames, nowhere near Node's default limit) — well inside the audit's own suggested 100-200 range, at the conservative end since nothing in this DSL's grammar has a legitimate use for anything close to even 100.
**The fix, Bug B (`src/schema/dsl/compiler.ts`):** `checkCircularPermissions`'s `dfs` was rewritten from native recursion to an explicit iterative worklist stack (`dfsFrom`, with a `DfsFrame { name, depsList, depIndex }` per in-progress call) — sidestepping the native-recursion depth problem entirely, per standard practice for cycle detection, rather than capping chain length artificially (there is no principled reason to bound how long a legitimate, acyclic permission chain may be, unlike `(` nesting).
