**#26 — verified live**: two throwaway probe files (one importing from outside `src/schema/`, one from a hypothetical sibling file inside `src/schema/`) both correctly triggered the lint error; the full repo, including the legitimate `runner.ts` caller and the legitimate test spy, lints clean both before and after. Independently re-confirmed by the main agent with a fresh probe file after the fact.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check .` all clean (confirmed twice — once by the fix's own author, once independently by the main agent against the combined working tree). `npx vitest run`: 317/317 passing at the point this entry was written (before the remaining in-flight test-gap findings' own coverage lands — see PROGRESS.md for current status).

**Revisit if:** A second legitimate caller for `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion` is ever genuinely needed — add it to the `no-restricted-imports` rule's exemption alongside `src/soundness/runner.ts` at that point, with the same reasoning this entry documents for why the contract narrowed to one caller in the first place.

## D-081 — The remaining nine test-gap findings from the full-repo audit, closed with real coverage — including two cases where the finding's own suggested mechanism didn't work and a genuine alternative had to be found

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo audit findings #10, #11, #17, #20, #21, #31–#34, all test-gap, MEDIUM/LOW) · **Status:** settled

Test-only — `git diff --stat src/` is empty for this entry; every change lives in `test/`. Grouped into one entry since all nine are coverage additions closing a documented gap, not independent behavior changes — listed individually for traceability. Every new/changed assertion was verified to actually fail for the right reason via a targeted, reverted source mutation before being trusted (this project's own standing discipline, applied per-finding below).

- **#10** (`cross-resolver-agreement.integration.test.ts`): added a fixture mixing tuple-to-userset _and_ nested-group membership in one chain (the file already had a substantial depth-budget-parity section from D-069/D-070 — the finding's own "deepest fixture is three levels" premise was stale; the real remaining gap was a _combined_-mechanism chain, not merely a deeper one). Boundary confirmed empirically against real Postgres (`maxDepth: 3` denies, `4` allows) before trusting it; reintroducing either D-069 bug independently breaks one half of the new pair.
- **#11** (`production-resolution-path.integration.test.ts`): a new fixture where the permission itself is shallow (one rewrite-rule hop) but the underlying relation is reached through three levels of nested-group tuples — confirms `checks.depth` folds in the real SQL-side recursive-CTE depth (`3`), not just the TypeScript-level walk depth (`1`), closing the exact gap the finding named (the prior test's grant was a direct tuple, SQL frontier depth 0, satisfiable without the fold logic at all).
- **#17** (`test/unit/schema/publish.integration.test.ts`, new): `publishSchema`'s all-or-nothing transaction claim, forced red by a `CHECK` constraint added to a dedicated test container (not the suggested pre-inserted colliding-version row — see below) — asserts a second namespace's forced insert failure rolls back the first namespace's already-executed insert too, with a control test proving the assertion isn't trivially satisfied by a resolver that persists nothing regardless.
- **#20** (`test/unit/cli/check.test.ts`, new, + `check.integration.test.ts`): a denied result still writes a `checks` row; a malformed subject/object ref, an invalid `--at-token`, and an unreachable database each exit correctly — mirroring `expand.ts`'s own DB-free/integration test split (the finding's framing that `expand.integration.test.ts` "covers multiple branches" didn't hold on inspection — that coverage is actually split across `expand.test.ts` + `expand.integration.test.ts`, which is the real pattern this mirrors).
- **#21** (`rewrite-rules.test.ts` + `schema-validation.test.ts`): a mixed-operator expression (`a & b | c`) pins the exact resulting AST (`union(intersection(a,b), c)`), confirming `&` binds tighter than `|`/`-` (D-011); a strict/soft pair (D-012) confirms a tuple-to-userset target namespace absent from the compilation unit is always rejected, while a plain relation's `#relation` subject-type namespace absent from it is tolerated.
- **#31** (`test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts`, new): `runMigrations` called twice back-to-back against a fresh container — the second call applies nothing (`applied: []`), no duplicate `schema_migrations` rows, and the full table/column fingerprint is unchanged.
- **#32** (`tuple-store.integration.test.ts`): mirrors `writeTuple`'s existing malformed-identifier injection-corpus test for `deleteTuple`, identical corpus, identical unreachable-pool mechanism.
- **#33** (`reference-resolver.depth-budget.test.ts`): a call with `maxDepth` genuinely omitted (not `{}`, not the constant spelled out — the argument itself absent) resolves a 14-hop chain; a control chain one hop past `DEFAULT_REFERENCE_MAX_DEPTH` is still denied with the option omitted, pinning the fallback to the exact documented constant rather than "some large-enough number."
- **#34** (`schema-validation.test.ts`): the whitespace-only-source test now asserts `code === 'empty_source'` and explicitly `code !== 'no_namespaces_declared'`, not just `ok === false` — the distinction the test's own name already promised but never checked.

**Where a finding's own suggested fix mechanism didn't actually work, and what was used instead:**

- **#17** suggested pre-inserting a colliding `(namespace, version)` row to force a mid-loop failure. This cannot work: `publishOne` computes `version` as `coalesce(max(version), 0) + 1` for the namespace being published, which by construction can never already exist as a row, however many rows are pre-inserted first — a pre-inserted row only pushes the computed next version higher, never onto an existing one. The only way to force a genuine collision from outside `publishOne` would be a real write timed to land in the exact window between its own `SELECT` and `INSERT` — a race against a **specific line of application code**, not a deterministic test setup, and one that D-080's own `pg_advisory_xact_lock` fix (landed earlier the same day) now closes for any two calls that both go through `publishOne` anyway. A `CHECK` constraint forcing a real Postgres error from the second insert exercises the identical code path (`catch` → `ROLLBACK` → rethrow) deterministically instead — the property under test (a mid-loop Postgres error rolls back everything already inserted in the same transaction) is unchanged; only the mechanism forcing that error is. Worth noting explicitly: this is a _good_ outcome, not a workaround papering over a gap — it means the concurrent-publish fix is robust enough that the original finding's own suggested race can no longer be constructed the way it described.
- **#20**'s own citation described `expand.integration.test.ts` as already covering "a denied result, a malformed ref, an invalid token, an unreachable database" as a pattern to mirror. On inspection, `expand.integration.test.ts` has exactly one test too — the real multi-branch coverage for `expand` is split across `expand.test.ts` (DB-free, CLI-argument-shaped cases) and `expand.integration.test.ts` (the one case that genuinely needs real published schema data). `check`'s own new coverage mirrors that actual split (a new `check.test.ts` for the DB-free cases, the existing `check.integration.test.ts` extended for the one case that needs real data), not the citation's literal description of it.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check .` all clean — confirmed independently by the main agent against the full combined working tree, not just accepted from the fix's own author. `npx vitest run` (fast suite): 328/328 passing. All new/modified `*.integration.test.ts` files re-verified against real Postgres — by the fix's own author via this project's established LOCALVERIFY technique (Docker unavailable in this sandbox), and independently spot-checked again by the main agent (`publish.integration.test.ts`'s own two tests re-run via a fresh LOCALVERIFY copy against real local Postgres, 2/2 passing, copy deleted, never committed) rather than trusted from the subagent's report alone.

**Revisit if:** A future change to `publishOne`'s own locking strategy (D-080) removes the `pg_advisory_xact_lock` this entry's own #17 reasoning depends on — re-confirm whether the originally-suggested pre-inserted-collision mechanism becomes viable again at that point, or whether the `CHECK`-constraint approach here should simply stay as the permanent mechanism regardless (it exercises the identical code path either way, so there's no urgency to switch back).

## D-082 — `trustProxy: 1`, not `trustProxy: true` — D-065's fix trusted an unbounded proxy chain instead of the one real hop

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, finding #3, HIGH) · **Status:** settled
**Decision:** `Fastify({ ..., trustProxy: 1, ... })` in `buildServer` (`src/api/server.ts`) — `1`, not `true`. `1` tells Fastify's `X-Forwarded-For` resolution to trust exactly one hop counted from the right (the address the deployment's own single reverse proxy actually appended), and to ignore anything ahead of it — including a value a client itself prepends.
**Why this needed a decision at all:** D-065 set `trustProxy: true` to fix a real problem (every caller behind the same proxy sharing one rate-limit budget), but `true` means "trust every hop the header claims," not "trust exactly the one real hop this deployment has." Reproduced live against the actual installed Fastify/`@fastify/proxy-addr`: a request carrying `X-Forwarded-For: 6.6.6.6, 9.9.9.9` resolves `request.ip` to `'6.6.6.6'` — the attacker's own fabricated first entry — under `trustProxy: true`, and correctly to `'9.9.9.9'` — the value the one real proxy actually appended — under `trustProxy: 1`. Since `@fastify/rate-limit`'s default `keyGenerator` keys on `request.ip`, and no route here overrides it, `trustProxy: true` let any caller obtain a fresh rate-limit budget on every single request simply by sending a new fabricated header value each time — defeating every budget in this file at once: the global 100/min default, `/health`'s 300/min (added by D-056 specifically to close a CodeQL unauthenticated-DoS finding), `gatedReadRateLimit`'s 200/min on `/check`/`/expand`, and `writeRateLimit`'s 20/min defense-in-depth against `ADMIN_API_KEY`-guessing. D-065's own "Revisit if" clause named the wrong trigger (see its "Update:" note above) — this doesn't require a different deployment shape to fire; it fires in the exact single-reverse-proxy shape (Railway) D-065 already assumed as safe.
**Verified live, not just read from Fastify's docs:** (1) a standalone repro script constructing two `Fastify` instances, one per `trustProxy` value, both fed the identical spoofed multi-hop header via `.inject()`, confirming the resolution difference above. (2) A new regression test (`test/unit/api/server.test.ts`, "finding #3: a spoofed X-Forwarded-For prefix cannot obtain a fresh rate-limit budget") sending 105 requests to an unmatched path, each carrying a DIFFERENT fabricated leading address ahead of the same real proxy hop — confirmed to fail (never reaches 429 across all 105) against the pre-fix `trustProxy: true`, and to pass (hits 429 well before request 105, all fabricated addresses correctly collapsing onto one shared budget) against `trustProxy: 1` — by temporarily reverting the config, re-running, and reverting back, not just trusted from reasoning about the mechanism.
**Alternative rejected:** A custom `keyGenerator` that ignores `X-Forwarded-For` entirely and keys on the raw socket peer address instead.
**Why it lost:** that's equivalent to `trustProxy: false` — every caller behind Railway's proxy would collapse back onto Railway's own address as one shared budget, reopening the exact problem D-065 existed to fix. `trustProxy: 1` is the narrower, correct fix: keep trusting the proxy's own appended value, just not anything a client could inject ahead of it.
**Revisit if:** This deployment ever sits behind more than one reverse-proxy hop (e.g. a CDN in front of Railway's own proxy) — `trustProxy: 1` would then resolve to the CDN's address, not the true client, and the trusted-hop count would need to become `2` (or the specific list of trusted proxy CIDRs Fastify's `trustProxy` also accepts) to match the real chain depth.

## D-083 — `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` close a real token-watermark race with a _global_ transaction-scoped advisory lock — `write_log.token`'s allocation order now provably matches its commit order

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, HIGH) · **Status:** settled

**What was wrong:** `assertTokenObserved` (`src/store/tokens.ts`) treats `select max(token) from write_log >= T` as proof that every write with `token <= T` has committed. `write_log.token` is a Postgres `bigint generated always as identity` column — its sequence value is allocated at INSERT-statement-execution time, non-transactionally, so allocation order carries no guaranteed relationship to commit order on its own. `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` (`src/store/tuples.ts`) ran a plain `BEGIN...COMMIT` with nothing serializing them — unlike `publishOne`'s analogous race (D-080), which its own `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext($1))` already closed. Concretely: transaction A (a revoke, `deleteTuple`, allocates token 100) slow to commit (a lock wait, a GC pause, a disk stall); transaction B (an unrelated concurrent write, allocates token 101) commits first, fast. A caller who legitimately received token 101 from B and pins a check to it passes `assertTokenObserved(101)` (`max(token) = 101` is visible) while A's revoke (token 100 ≤ 101) has not committed yet — the exact violation of `docs/CONSISTENCY.md`'s stated invariant, "a check pinned to token T never returns a result that ignores a write with token ≤ T." This does not affect the primary documented use case (pinning to your own just-received write token, trivially safe by construction), but it does affect the broader guarantee the docs state applies to _any_ observed token.

**Why this wasn't caught earlier:** `docs/CONSISTENCY.md` states "a genuinely impossible situation on a single Postgres instance with synchronous commits" for the case `assertTokenObserved` fails its own comparison — that claim was about the _comparison_ never seeing an inconsistent `write_log` state (true, and unaffected by this finding), not about whether the token watermark itself could be satisfied while an earlier write was still mid-flight. Those are two different properties, and only the first one had ever actually been exercised or proven — the second was asserted, never tested, until this fix.

**The fix:** `acquireWriteLogLock` (`src/store/tuples.ts`) — `select pg_advisory_xact_lock($1, $2)` as the very first statement inside `writeTuple`'s and `deleteTuple`'s transaction, immediately after `BEGIN`, before either their own `insert`/`delete` against `relation_tuples` or their call to `insertWriteLog`. Unlike `publishOne`'s namespace-scoped `hashtext($1)` lock, this cannot be scoped any narrower than global: `write_log.token` is a single sequence shared by every tuple write/delete in the entire database, regardless of namespace, so any two concurrent writes anywhere — even on completely unrelated namespaces/objects — can exhibit the race above; a namespace-scoped lock would only close it for two writes sharing a namespace, leaving the general case (an unrelated concurrent write, exactly what the finding describes) wide open. The lock key uses the **two-integer-argument** form (`pg_advisory_xact_lock(classid, objid)`), not the single-bigint form `publish.ts` uses — Postgres documents the two forms as entirely separate lock keyspaces that never overlap, so this is a structural non-collision guarantee, not "a number nowhere near `hashtext()`'s typical output." `classid` is the fixed, greppable, human-legible ASCII value of `wlog` (`0x776c6f67` = `2003595111`), exported as `WRITE_LOG_LOCK_CLASSID`; `objid` is a fixed `0` (`WRITE_LOG_LOCK_OBJID`) — there is exactly one thing this lock ever serializes, so there is no second dimension to key by. `pg_advisory_xact_lock` releases automatically at `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`, matching `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`'s existing transaction wrapping with no separate unlock call needed — the same reasoning D-080 gives for its own lock.

**The honest cost:** every `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` call in the entire system, for any namespace, now serializes against every other one — a real throughput ceiling under concurrent write load this store did not have before (previously only two writes racing for the exact same `relation_tuples` row contended at the database level; now any two concurrent tuple writes/deletes anywhere contend on this one lock). This is a deliberate trade of write throughput for a soundness guarantee `docs/CONSISTENCY.md` already claimed unconditionally.

**Verified live against real Postgres, not just reasoned about:** a standalone repro script opened two independent `pg.Client` connections and deliberately inverted commit order relative to token-allocation order — client A began a transaction, inserted its `write_log` row (allocating the _lower_ token), and withheld its `COMMIT`; client B began, inserted (allocating the _higher_ token), and committed immediately. **Before the fix:** `max(token)` already covered B's token while A's still-lower-numbered row remained genuinely uncommitted and invisible to a plain read (`is A's write_log row visible? false`, `assertTokenObserved(pool, tokenB)` would PASS) — the race reproduced exactly as described. **After the fix**, repeating the identical scenario through the real `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` functions (a raw client acquiring the real, exported lock and holding it uncommitted, racing a concurrent real `writeTuple` call on an unrelated object): the real `writeTuple` call was observably still-pending 500ms later (`bSettled === false`) — genuinely blocked, not merely lucky timing — and only resolved once the raw client's transaction committed; its resulting token was strictly higher, `assertTokenObserved` resolved cleanly, and the raw client's write was already visible. A second, symmetric test proved the lock is shared across operation _and_ table (a held delete-mirroring transaction blocks a concurrent real `writeTuple`), not merely per-statement or per-table. Both new regression tests were confirmed to fail for the right reason (`bSettled` incorrectly `true`, proving the race was not actually closed) when `acquireWriteLogLock`'s two call sites were temporarily commented out and the tests re-run, then restored.

**Two alternatives considered and rejected**, mirroring D-080's own reasoning for the analogous per-namespace case: `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the just-inserted `write_log` row doesn't close the race — the row it would lock is created by the very insert whose ordering is in question, so a second transaction could still insert its own row and race to commit before the first releases anything (no shared row exists to serialize _concurrent inserts_ against, only already-existing rows). Catching a would-be ordering violation after the fact and retrying isn't viable either — unlike a unique-constraint violation (D-080's case), there is no constraint Postgres itself would ever reject here; the wrong ordering is silently valid data, only observable by a consistency check that has already returned an answer, too late to retry.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` on every touched file all clean. `npx vitest run` (fast suite) and `npx vitest run --config vitest.integration.config.ts` (full integration suite, via this project's established LOCALVERIFY-against-real-local-Postgres technique — Docker unavailable in this sandbox) both fully green, including the two new regression tests in `test/unit/store/tuple-store.integration.test.ts`. `npx tsx src/cli/index.ts soundness run --dry-run` re-run against real Postgres afterward to confirm the differential-fuzz harness (which performs real tuple writes under the hood) still completes cleanly with the lock in place.

**Revisit if:** This lock becomes an actual measured bottleneck under real write concurrency (e.g. sustained multi-writer load where `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` throughput, not database CPU/IO, is the binding constraint) — at that point, consider whether a narrower mechanism (e.g. a single dedicated serializer process/queue for `write_log` inserts specifically, or accepting a documented, narrower consistency window for tokens pinned to a write that isn't the caller's own) could preserve the same guarantee at lower cost. Until then, correctness — a claim `docs/CONSISTENCY.md` already made unconditionally — outweighs the throughput this costs.

## D-084 — `false_grant` rendering gets a real size budget, not a fixed entry cap; `post-soundness-comment.mjs` gets an independent second layer so an oversized body can never crash the poster unhandled

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (full-repo audit, HIGH) · **Status:** settled

**A note on the entry this updates:** the audit finding that prompted this fix described the "every `false_grant` renders uncapped, by deliberate design" behavior as something `docs/DECISIONS.md` had its own entry for, expecting an `Update:` note here the way D-060/D-067 carry one. On inspection, no such entry exists — the decision was real (see `src/report/markdown.ts`'s own `RenderSoundnessMarkdownOptions` doc comment, and the `false_grant always renders in full, regardless of count` test in `test/unit/report/markdown.test.ts`) but was only ever recorded inline in code, never given its own `DECISIONS.md` entry the way D-043/D-044 (the sibling Phase 7 rendering decisions, same date) were. That is itself a small, real documentation gap, disclosed here rather than papered over with a forced `Update:` note on an entry that was never actually about this. This entry is the first `DECISIONS.md` record of the sizing question.

**What was wrong:** `renderSoundnessMarkdown` capped `false_deny` divergences at `maxRenderedFalseDeny` (default 20) but rendered every `false_grant` unconditionally, in full — correct in spirit (a `false_grant` must never be summarized away, per the report-designer brief and §8) but with no ceiling of any kind. Empirically confirmed against the real, compiled renderer (not reasoned about): 500 synthetic `false_grant` divergences, each a minimal single-hop `directGrant` path, rendered to **243,074 characters** — nearly 4x GitHub's own real PR-comment-body limit (~65,536 characters). `scripts/post-soundness-comment.mjs` had no length check anywhere except an empty-body guard (D-068); its `checkedFetch` throws unhandled on GitHub's `422` rejection of an oversized body, crashing the "Post or update the soundness PR comment" workflow step before it posted anything. `.github/workflows/soundness.yml`'s own "Fail the job" step has no `if: always()`, so it's skipped too — the job still ends up red (no false-negative merge), but the actual damage is worse: on a PR's first soundness run, no comment posts at all; on a PR with an older, clean-looking tracked comment already on it, that stale comment is left untouched and visibly wrong while the real, severe report never displays. CI's default fuzz budget is 5,000 queries (`SOUNDNESS_FUZZ_QUERIES`); a genuine regression flipping a commonly-hit relation to always-allow can plausibly produce well over 150-200 `false_grant`s in one run, comfortably past where this crash reproduces.

**The fix, two independent layers:**

1. **`src/report/markdown.ts`** — `renderSoundnessMarkdown` now tracks the running rendered body's length (via a small `joinedLength` helper, O(n) over the loop rather than re-joining and re-measuring on every iteration) while rendering the `false_grant` section, against a new `maxCommentChars` option (default `DEFAULT_MAX_COMMENT_CHARS = 60_000` — deliberately well under GitHub's real ~65,536-character limit, leaving real headroom for the `false_deny` section and everything else that renders afterward). The moment rendering the next `false_grant` entry would cross that budget, the loop stops — but never silently: an unmissable, bold `**TRUNCATED — N more FALSE_GRANT divergences not shown in this comment (M total).**` line always follows, together with a paragraph stating plainly that this is not a reduced-severity result, that the true count is the one already stated in the headline, and pointing at the workflow run's own "Run soundness fuzz" step log (which already `cat`s the complete, untruncated `soundness-report.md` verbatim — the real mechanism this workflow has, not a hypothetical artifact upload it doesn't) for the full detail. This is deliberately **not** the same mechanism as `false_deny`'s own `maxRenderedFalseDeny` entry-count cap: a `false_grant` is truncated only by real, measured document size, never by an arbitrary count picked in advance — the two options (`maxRenderedFalseDeny`, `maxCommentChars`) stay independent, governing independent sections, on purpose.
2. **`scripts/post-soundness-comment.mjs`** — a second, independent safety net that does not trust the renderer's own budget math to be bug-free forever. Before either `fetch` call, the real report's byte length (`Buffer.byteLength(body, 'utf8')`) is checked against `GITHUB_COMMENT_BODY_BYTE_LIMIT` (65,536, GitHub's own documented ceiling). If it's over — whatever the reason — this script never sends the oversized body: `buildOversizedFallbackBody` extracts the real H2 headline line and the real `Reproduce: ...` line straight out of the oversized report verbatim (both are always the first two rendered elements after `SOUNDNESS_REPORT_MARKER` in `renderSoundnessMarkdown`'s own output) and posts a short, honest, marker-prefixed comment instead — the real, measured verdict and counts, never a vaguer "something went wrong," plus the same pointer at the workflow run's own log. Starting with the marker preserves the "update in place, never stack" contract for this fallback comment too.

**Verified empirically, not just reasoned about, against the real, compiled `renderSoundnessMarkdown`:**

- 500 `false_grant` divergences, `maxCommentChars: Infinity` (simulating the pre-fix, uncapped behavior): **243,074 characters** — confirms the crash risk is real, not theoretical.
- The same 500 divergences, real default options (no override): **60,648 characters** — under GitHub's 65,536-character limit — with 124 of the 500 rendered in full and the true `(500 total)` count stated in the truncation notice.
- A clean `SOUND` run (0/0): unchanged, 340 characters, identical shape to the pre-fix renderer (this code path never touches the new loop).
- A small `false_deny`-only run (5 divergences, default `maxRenderedFalseDeny`): unchanged — all 5 render, no truncation, `false_deny`'s own cap-20 mechanism untouched.
- 3 small `false_grant` divergences (well under any realistic budget): all 3 still render in full, no truncation notice — confirms no regression to the "always renders in full" behavior at realistic scale.
- `scripts/post-soundness-comment.mjs`, run directly (fabricated `GITHUB_TOKEN`/`GITHUB_REPOSITORY`/`GITHUB_EVENT_PATH`, no real network credentials) against the 243,074-character oversized report: the size guard correctly logged `"...is 248090 bytes, over GitHub's 65536-character comment limit — posting a short summary instead..."` and proceeded to the real network call (failing only on GitHub's own `401 Bad credentials` for the fake token — confirmed the guard itself never throws, and is reached and passed cleanly). The same script run against a normal small report never logged that message and went straight to the network call, confirming no behavior change on the common path. Run again against a literally empty report: the pre-existing D-068 empty-body guard still fires first and unchanged.

**Alternative rejected:** Truncate `false_grant` the same casual way `false_deny` already is (a fixed entry-count cap, e.g. "first 20"). Rejected explicitly — a `false_grant` is this project's most severe possible finding (§6.5); silently capping it at a fixed count the same way a non-blocking `false_deny` is capped would be its own regression, exactly the failure mode the report-designer brief warns against ("a resolution path is never summarized away... each one shows its actual chain"). A size budget with an unmissable, honest notice preserves that principle — every `false_grant` up to the real space available still gets its full resolution path, and the ones that don't are never quietly dropped.

**Revisit if:** GitHub's own comment-body limit changes, or a future UI (§8's real screens, Phase 9) replaces the markdown PR comment with something that has no such ceiling — at that point `maxCommentChars`/`GITHUB_COMMENT_BODY_BYTE_LIMIT` may no longer need to exist at all, or could grow substantially.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit` clean on every file this fix touches (one pre-existing, unrelated error in `test/unit/cli/soundness-format.test.ts` from concurrent, unrelated work in this same working tree — not introduced or touched by this fix). `npx eslint .` clean on every file this fix touches (one pre-existing, unrelated error in `src/api/server.ts` from the same concurrent work — likewise untouched by this fix). `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file. `npx vitest run test/unit/report/markdown.test.ts`: 28/28 passing, including four new tests for the size-budget behavior (a small-override-budget case, the real 500-divergence/default-budget case pinned against GitHub's literal 65,536-character limit, a register/severity check on the truncation notice itself, and a no-false-positive check at small scale).

## D-085 — `expand()` gets the same namespace-config cache the production resolver already has

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, finding #5, MEDIUM) · **Status:** settled

**What was wrong:** `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`'s `WalkContext` carries a `schemaCache: Map<string, NamespaceConfig | null>` built specifically to avoid re-querying `namespace_configs` for a namespace already looked up earlier in the same walk (its own doc comment cites "folder's parent->view revisiting folder itself at every hop"). `src/audit/expand.ts`'s own `WalkContext` had no equivalent — `expandName` called `getLatestNamespaceConfig` unconditionally on every visited node. `expand()`'s walk is deliberately sequential (`mapSequential`, not `Promise.all` — required to keep the shared `visiting` cycle-guard set correct), so every extra round trip sits directly on the critical path, and `/expand` is a reachable, `ADMIN_API_KEY`-gated, 200 req/min API route — a real, avoidable cost on a hot admin route, never documented anywhere as an accepted tradeoff.

**The fix:** `expand.ts`'s `WalkContext` gained the identical `schemaCache` field and a `getConfig(ctx, ns)` helper, mirroring `resolver.ts`'s own naming and behavior exactly (checked once, cached, rebuilt fresh on every `expand()` call — never shared across calls, so it cannot become a correctness-relevant cache per build spec §6.1). `expandName` now calls `getConfig(ctx, object.ns)` instead of `getLatestNamespaceConfig` directly.

**Verified live against real Postgres:** a 10-level `parent->view` chain (permission satisfied only through nested parent tuples, never a direct grant) issued **44** `pool.query` calls before this fix and **23** after — confirmed by instrumenting `pool.query` directly around a real `expand()` call, comparing the same fixture against the pre-fix code (via `git stash`) and the fix. The reduction isn't the full 20-to-1 the finding's own example implied (the tuple-fetch queries themselves are unaffected — only the namespace-config lookups this cache targets are eliminated), but it's a real, measured, roughly-halved query count for this shape, not a speculative improvement.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .` clean. `test/unit/audit/expand.integration.test.ts` (5/5) re-run against real Postgres via this project's established LOCALVERIFY technique — unchanged output, confirming the cache is purely a latency optimization with no observable behavior difference.

**Revisit if:** `expand()`'s output ever needs to reflect a schema change made mid-walk (it doesn't today, and shouldn't — a single `expand()` call is meant to reflect one consistent snapshot of the schema, exactly like a single `productionCheck` call already does with its own identical cache).

## D-086 — `authz check --path` — the README's own headline example now actually produces the diagram it claims to

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, finding #8, MEDIUM) · **Status:** settled

**What was wrong:** `README.md` presents a 5-hop arrow diagram and states `authz check user:dana edit document:eng_handbook` "returns this exact path," and its own "try it yourself" walkthrough tells a first-time reader to run precisely that command. But `check()` (`src/cli/commands/check.ts`) only ever printed `ALLOWED`/`DENIED` — there was no flag to print the resolution path. The path itself was always real and already computed/stored (`performCheck` writes it to `checks.resolution_path`; the API's JSON response includes it as `path`) — it just was never what this specific CLI command printed, a first-impression documentation failure for a project whose entire value proposition is a visible resolution path.

**The fix:** a new `--path` flag on `authz check`, backed by `renderResolutionPath` (`src/cli/commands/check.ts`) — a small recursive renderer over `ResolutionStep` (§6.7's positive-witness tree) that reproduces the README's own indented-arrow-chain style exactly. Chosen over the alternative (rewriting the README/`docs/RELATIONS.md` down to describe what the CLI used to do) because it's the smaller, more honest fix: the data was already there, the CLI just never surfaced it, and this project's whole identity is built around "every allow shows its work" — narrowing the documented claim instead of building the two-line CLI wiring to actually satisfy it would have been the wrong tradeoff.

**A real bug caught during live verification, not shipped:** the first version of `renderResolutionPath` printed a `tupleToUserset` hop's trailing label using `step.relation` (the tuple relation _followed_, e.g. `"parent"`) instead of the outer permission name actually being satisfied — producing `document:eng_handbook#parent` against the real seeded example graph, where the README's own diagram says `document:eng_handbook#edit`. Root cause: most `ResolutionStep` variants (`union`/`intersection`/`exclusion`/`tupleToUserset`) do not carry the name of the permission being satisfied at their own node — only `directGrant`/`usersetMembership` do, via a real storable relation. Fixed by threading that name down explicitly as a function parameter (`name`, inherited unchanged through `union`/`intersection`/`exclusion`; the recursive descent into a `tupleToUserset`'s `member` switches it to `step.computedUserset`, since that's the name being proven on `step.through` — not `step.relation`, and not necessarily equal to `name` either, even though a `parent->view` self-referential-inheritance idiom often makes them look the same).

**Verified live against the real seeded example graph** (`schema/example.authz`, `scripts/seed-example.ts`): `authz check user:dana edit document:eng_handbook --path` output matched the README's own quoted 5-hop diagram exactly, structurally identical line for line. Also verified against a real DENIED case (prints an explicit "no resolution path" note, never a blank/silent no-op), a real intersection case (`sensitive_review`, both required branches rendered), and a real exclusion case (`org.view = member - banned`, the base grant rendered under a "granted, and not excluded" header) — all four `ResolutionStep` shape families exercised against real data, not just the one linear chain.

**Test coverage, two layers:** `test/unit/cli/check-path.test.ts` (new, DB-free) hand-builds `ResolutionStep` trees with `name`/`relation`/`computedUserset` deliberately set to three different strings, pinning the exact bug caught above so it can't recur even if a future schema no longer happens to exercise that distinction. `test/unit/cli/check.integration.test.ts` (extended) proves the same flag end-to-end against a real, uniquely-named namespace and real Postgres, asserting the printed output hop-for-hop. A real bug was caught here too during LOCALVERIFY: the first version of the integration test read `logSpy.mock.calls` _after_ calling `logSpy.mockRestore()` — `mockRestore()` does everything `mockReset()` does (which clears recorded calls) before restoring the original implementation, so every assertion silently saw an empty array. Fixed by capturing the calls before restoring.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file. `test/unit/cli/check-path.test.ts`: 6/6 passing (DB-free). `test/unit/cli/check.integration.test.ts`: 3/3 passing against real local Postgres via LOCALVERIFY.

**Revisit if:** A future `ResolutionStep` variant is added to §6.7's shape — `renderResolutionPath`'s exhaustive `switch` (no `default` case) will fail to compile until the new variant is handled, by construction.

## D-087 — Five more findings from the second full-repo audit, closed with real coverage: cycle-detection blind spots, a stale rate-limit test, an untested `--dry-run`×`--format` contract, D-013's actual rejected alternative, and quieter test logs

**Date:** 2026-08-16 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, findings #4/#6/#7/#12/#14) · **Status:** settled

Test-only except finding #14's small `BuildServerOptions` addition to `src/api/server.ts` (an optional, defaulted parameter — `serve.ts`, the one production caller, is untouched, so production logging behavior is unchanged by construction). Grouped into one entry since all five are coverage/test-infra additions closing documented gaps, not independent behavior changes — listed individually for traceability. Every new/changed assertion was verified to actually fail for the right reason via a targeted, reverted source mutation before being trusted (this project's own standing discipline).

- **#4** (`test/unit/resolve/reference-resolver.graph-shape.test.ts`): every cyclic tuple graph in the committed suite used the identical 2-node userset-subject-nesting shape — no test constructed a cycle through `tupleToUserset` or a direct single-tuple self-loop, both mechanisms sharing the same `visiting`-set guard but never independently exercised. Two new tests added; both confirmed to fail (`RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`) when the guard was temporarily disabled in `src/resolve/reference/resolver.ts`, then restored clean.
- **#6** (`test/unit/api/rate-limit.test.ts`): the describe block claiming `/check` is "governed by the global 100/minute default" was stale post-D-064/D-065 — unauthenticated `POST /check` now returns 401 before ever reaching the counted rate-limit path, so the test's only assertion (`not.toBe(429)`) passed trivially while testing nothing real. Retargeted at `/schema/compile` (confirmed live to be the one route still genuinely governed by the bare global default) and added a new test authenticating and driving `/check` to its real 200/minute budget. Both confirmed to fail for the right reason when the relevant `max` was temporarily lowered, then restored.
- **#7** (`test/unit/cli/soundness-format.test.ts`): `soundness.ts`'s own doc comment states `--dry-run` never affects `--format markdown`/`json` output, but no committed test passed `dryRun: true` while exercising those format branches. Two new tests assert byte-identical output between `dryRun: false`/`true` for both formats, independently anchored against the real renderer's own output rather than mere self-consistency. Confirmed to fail when a fake dry-run marker was temporarily injected into each branch.
- **#12** (`test/unit/schema/schema-validation.test.ts`): D-013 requires a permission with a live grounding branch alongside a dead cyclic branch to be rejected as a whole — the committed suite's self-referential-permission tests never combined the two. New test compiles `permission x = owner | y; permission y = x` and asserts both are flagged via the structured `SchemaError.member` field. **A drafting mistake caught and fixed during verification, not shipped:** the first version asserted on `message.includes('x')`, which passed even under a simulated "per-branch liveness" implementation (D-013's own explicitly-rejected alternative) — because `y`'s own error message text embeds the cycle path `(x -> y -> x)`, so it contains the substring `'x'` regardless of whether `x` itself was ever actually flagged. Switched to the precise `member` field; the same fail-check then correctly failed, proving the test now actually distinguishes the two implementations.
- **#14** (`src/api/server.ts` + 3 test files): Fastify/pino request logging (`{ level: env.LOG_LEVEL }`, unconditional) flooded Vitest stdout — rate-limit/404-flooding tests alone fire well over a hundred requests each. `buildServer` gained an optional `BuildServerOptions.logger` parameter (default `undefined`, preserving `{ level: env.LOG_LEVEL }` exactly when omitted); every test file calling `buildServer(pool)` now passes `{ logger: false }`. Verified live: `rate-limit.test.ts` now produces zero JSON log lines (previously flooded); production logging confirmed unaffected by running `authz serve` against real local Postgres and observing normal pino output. `test/unit/api/server.integration.test.ts`'s copy of this change could not be run live in this sandbox (no working Docker/testcontainers runtime) — verified by `tsc`/`eslint`/`prettier` and code review only, a lower bar than the other two files, disclosed rather than silently assumed equivalent.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .` clean. `npx prettier --check` clean on all changed files. `npx vitest run` (fast suite): 353/353 passing (includes this entry's additions plus every other concurrent fix landed the same day).

**Revisit if:** `test/unit/api/server.integration.test.ts`'s finding-14 change should be live-verified against real Postgres the next time Docker is available in this environment, to close the one disclosed gap above.

## D-088 — Deployed to Railway; a real, reproducible latency benchmark; the README states what this actually is in its own first line

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (portfolio-readiness pass, three findings from external review) · **Status:** settled

**Deployment.** `authz-api` (Railway service `c8e8621b-d892-492a-9d02-276d97a9e25f`, project "Upwork Portfolio", environment "production"), tracking `main`, `DATABASE_URL` wired to the existing `Postgres-RBA` service via Railway's own `${{Postgres-RBA.DATABASE_URL}}` variable reference (private networking — never the public TCP proxy) — the same dedicated database `docs/DELIVERY.md`'s own task list already named for this project, never touched by any of this repo's own local/CI verification, which has always run against an ephemeral or local Postgres instead. Needed a `start` script first (`package.json` had none) — `"start": "node dist/cli/index.js doctor && node dist/cli/index.js serve"`, the standard signal Railway's Nixpacks builder auto-detects for a plain Node app. Chaining `doctor` before `serve` was a deliberate choice, not an afterthought: `authz serve` itself never applies migrations (only `authz doctor` does), so without this, the first deploy against a genuinely fresh database would start a server pointed at an unmigrated schema and fail every real request instead of failing the deploy itself, loudly, up front. `doctor`'s own non-zero exit on an unreachable database means `&&` correctly stops `serve` from ever starting against a broken connection — verified live, locally, before ever touching Railway (`npm run build && npm start` against real local Postgres: doctor reports `4/4 applied`, server listens, `/health` returns real `200`).

**Verified live against the real deployment, not assumed from "status: SUCCESS" alone:** this sandbox's outbound egress policy blocks direct connections to `*.up.railway.app` (confirmed via the agent proxy's own recorded failure: `connect_rejected`, "gateway answered 403 to CONNECT" — a policy denial, not a transient failure, and per this environment's own standing instruction, not something to retry or route around) and blocks raw TCP to the database's public proxy port entirely, so this repository's own agent could not personally curl the live URL the way a human reader can. What _is_ directly verified, via Railway's own deployment-log API (not the browser-facing dashboard, and not trusted from a bare "SUCCESS" status field alone): the container started, `authz doctor` ran against production and printed `Postgres: reachable — database "railway", server 18.6`, `Migrations: 4/4 applied`, `doctor: OK`, and Fastify then logged `Server listening at http://127.0.0.1:8080` (Railway's own injected `PORT`) with no error afterward. That is real evidence the service is correctly wired end-to-end against the real, dedicated database — external reachability from outside Railway's own network is the one link in the chain this repository's own tooling could not close itself, and is disclosed as such rather than asserted.

**Benchmark.** `scripts/benchmark-check-depth.ts` (`npm run benchmark`) — real `performCheck` latency at permission-chain depths 1/3/5/10, 50 runs each, against real Postgres; results published in the README's new "Latency" section. Deliberately measures the check engine's own graph walk plus its Postgres round trips, never an HTTP round trip against `authz serve` — network transit time is a property of where a caller is physically calling from relative to wherever this is deployed, not a property of this engine, so publishing an HTTP-inclusive number as "the" latency would misrepresent what actually varies and what doesn't. Written as a checked-in, permanent, reproducible script (mirroring `scripts/seed-example.ts`'s own established pattern — a `.ts` file run via `tsx`, no build step, `src/` imports of the real domain functions) specifically so the README's own numbers are a claim anyone who clones this repo can check themselves, not a one-off run nobody else can reproduce — the same discipline every soundness/correctness claim in this project has always been held to (§0 rule 9), applied here to a performance claim instead. Deletes every tuple it writes on exit; deliberately does **not** delete its own published namespace version — `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion` (`src/schema/publish.ts`) is ESLint-restricted to exactly one caller (`src/soundness/runner.ts`'s dry-run cleanup) for a real, documented reason: every other piece of this system treats a published namespace version as permanent, append-only history, never a synthetic fixture to erase. A benchmark script that reached for that function anyway to tidy up after itself would have been quietly working around the exact invariant that restriction exists to protect — caught and corrected before landing, not shipped and found later.

**README naming.** Added a one-line tagline directly under the H1 ("A fine-grained authorization service, Zanzibar-style...") — `package.json`'s own `description` field already used almost this exact phrasing (`"Fine-grained, relationship-based authorization service..."`); the README's own first line simply never matched it. "Fine-grained authorization" is the phrase that actually shows up in job postings and recruiter search, "Relationship-Based Authorization" is this project's own precise, correct name for what it is — stating both, instead of relying on the title alone, costs one line.

**Where this came from:** all three findings originated in an external review (a separate conversation, not this repository's own audit tooling) that got two things right (deployment status, published latency numbers were both genuinely missing) and two things wrong about this specific repository's actual state — it suggested differential/soundness testing "if it isn't in yet" (it's the project's entire centerpiece, Phases 3-5, with its own CI badge) and suggested starting a decisions log "while it's fresh" (`docs/DECISIONS.md` already had 88 entries at the time). Recorded here because the corrected assessment, not just the three accepted actions, is itself worth having on record — see this same date's chat transcript for the full back-and-forth.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file. `npm run build && npm start` verified live against real local Postgres before ever touching Railway. `npm run benchmark` run against real local Postgres, output pasted directly into the README (not hand-edited afterward). Railway deployment verified via the platform's own deployment-log API as described above.

**Revisit if:** This sandbox's egress policy ever allows `*.up.railway.app` (or the specific deployed domain), at which point re-verify external reachability directly rather than relying on deployment logs alone — that's a strictly stronger check than what was available here and should replace it, not just supplement it.

**Update (2026-08-17): external reachability confirmed, and the live instance seeded.** The user independently curled the live URL from outside this sandbox — `{"status":"ok","database":{"reachable":true},...}` — closing the one gap this entry's own "Revisit if" named, without needing this sandbox's egress policy to change. Also seeded the deployed instance with the real demo graph (`scripts/seed-example.ts`) via Railway's `preDeployCommand` (runs once, before the persistent server process starts, without replacing it) — confirmed via the platform's own deployment-log API: `published org v1`/`group v1`/`folder v1`/`document v1`, all 22 tuple writes, `seeded 22 tuples across 4 namespaces`. `preDeployCommand` was cleared back to empty immediately after, so it doesn't re-publish a new namespace version on every future deploy. Two things caught live during this, not assumed: (1) the first redeploy attempt (`railway_redeploy(deploymentId: ...)`, replaying a specific prior deployment) silently did **not** run the newly-set `preDeployCommand` at all — its build plan showed no pre-deploy step, and the runtime logs jumped straight from container start to the normal `doctor && serve` output with no seed activity in between. Root cause: that operation replays a deployment's own snapshotted config from when it was first built, not the service's current settings — `serviceInstanceRedeploy(serviceId, environmentId)` (current-config, not a specific deployment snapshot) is the one that actually picks up a settings change, confirmed by its build plan and runtime logs showing the seed script's real output on the very next attempt. (2) The user's own first re-check hit `GET /` (the bare domain, no path) and got the same `not_found` envelope every other unmatched route gets — expected per D-072, not a regression, confirmed directly from the request's own logged `req`/`res` attributes (`{"method":"GET","url":"/",...}` → `{"statusCode":404}`) rather than guessed at.

## D-089 — A real Windows user ran "Try it yourself": docker-compose, an isolated Railway dev database, and a genuine cross-platform bug in `authz doctor`

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (live user walkthrough, not internal review) · **Status:** settled

**Context.** Every prior verification of the README's own "under 10 minutes, from a clean clone" flow — including D-045's task #44 — ran on this sandbox's Linux environment. This is the first time a real person actually walked through it, on Windows (Git Bash / MINGW64), and it surfaced two real gaps neither internal review nor the external-review pass in D-088 caught.

**Onboarding gap: no scaffolding for "any reachable Postgres 16+."** `cp .env.example .env` alone leaves `DATABASE_URL` pointing at that file's own placeholder connection string — not a real database. `authz doctor` correctly reported `Postgres: unreachable`, but there was no checked-in way to get a real one quickly, and no documented fix path. Shipped in #34 (merged before this entry): `docker-compose.yml` (Postgres 16, credentials matched to `.env.example`'s placeholder — `cp .env.example .env && docker compose up -d` needs zero further edits) and a new README "Troubleshooting" section. Separately, an isolated Railway Postgres service (`authz-dev-postgres`, its own volume, its own TCP proxy, its own credentials — deliberately not the same database backing the live `authz-api` demo from D-088, so seeding/soundness-fuzzing it can never touch what `/health` reports on) was provisioned for this specific user as a second option.

**The real bug: `Migrations: 0/0 applied`, and `doctor: OK`, on a database that had never been migrated.** Once the user pointed `DATABASE_URL` at the new Railway database, `authz doctor` reported `Postgres: reachable` — correct — followed by `Migrations: 0/0 applied` and `doctor: OK` — both wrong, on a genuinely fresh database with zero migrations ever applied. Root cause: `src/cli/commands/doctor.ts` computed `MIGRATIONS_DIR` as `new URL('../../store/migrations', import.meta.url).pathname`. A `file://` URL's `.pathname` on a Windows path keeps the leading `/` in front of the drive letter (`file:///C:/Users/...` -> `/C:/Users/...`) — not a valid Windows filesystem path. `existsSync` on that bogus path returns `false`, and `discoverMigrations` treats a missing directory as zero migrations, not an error (deliberately, per its own doc comment, for the legitimate case of a fresh clone before Phase 2's first migration ever existed) — so the failure was completely silent: no exception, exit code 0, `doctor: OK` printed, and every later command (`seed:example`, `check`, `expand`, `soundness run`) would have failed against tables that were never created. `src/config/env.ts` already got this exact conversion right (`fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)`, not `.pathname`) — `doctor.ts` just didn't match it. Fixed by mirroring `env.ts`'s own pattern.

**Why this wasn't caught sooner.** `.pathname` and `fileURLToPath` produce the same result on POSIX paths (no drive letter to mishandle) — every verification of this flow, including the CI matrix and every local/sandbox run in this project's own history, has run on Linux or macOS. This is a Windows-only defect that would affect any Windows user of the CLI, built or run from source alike (the bug is in the TypeScript source, so it compiles into `dist/` unchanged) — not a one-off. `test/unit/cli/doctor.test.ts` pins the actual mechanism directly (`fileURLToPath(url, { windows: true })` — Node 20+, this project requires Node >=22 — forces the Windows code path deterministically on any host, including this Linux CI runner) rather than asserting `doctor.ts`'s end-to-end behavior only on the one platform already known to work.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check`, `npm run test` (new regression test passing) all clean. Could not verify the fix against a real Windows machine directly — the mechanism-level test above is the closest substitute available in this environment; the user's own next `authz doctor` run against the Railway dev database is the real-world confirmation.

**Revisit if:** Any other place in this codebase computes a filesystem path from `import.meta.url` — checked at the time of this entry (`grep -rn "import.meta.url" src/`) and `env.ts`/`doctor.ts` were the only two; `doctor.ts` was the only one using the broken pattern.

## D-090 — `authz soundness run --progress <n>` — a real user asked for this, mid-troubleshooting

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (live user walkthrough, continuation of D-089) · **Status:** settled

**Context.** Immediately after D-089's fix, the same user ran the 5,000-query `soundness run --dry-run` from the README's own walkthrough against the Railway dev database. It sat with zero output for long enough to look identical to a hang — `soundness run` prints nothing at all until the entire run finishes, by design (`src/cli/commands/soundness.ts`'s `printText`/`renderSoundnessMarkdown`/`renderSoundnessJsonString` are each called exactly once, after `runSoundnessFuzz` resolves). Separately, an actual lock-contention issue from an earlier interrupted run (Ctrl+C not fully killing the process on Windows) compounded the confusion before being ruled out via the Postgres server's own logs (`unexpected EOF on client connection with an open transaction` — Postgres had already rolled it back cleanly). Once that was ruled out and a smaller run confirmed the harness itself was working, the user asked directly: "can you make it so it gives me a count every x amount."

**What shipped.** `--progress <n>`: a `checked X/Y queries` line on **stderr** every `n` completed queries, plus always once more on the final batch regardless of whether `n` evenly divides the total. Three pieces:

- `src/report/progress.ts` — `createProgressReporter(every, write)`, the cadence decision (when to actually print, given a stream of `(completed, total)` calls that may not land on round numbers). Pure, `write` injected rather than hardcoded to `process.stderr.write` — matches this project's own established "keep I/O at the edges" preference (`discoverMigrations`, `buildDivergenceRecord`) specifically so the logic is unit-testable with no real terminal.
- `src/soundness/runner.ts` — `SoundnessRunOptions.onProgress`, called by `checkAllQueries` once per real concurrency-sized batch (never more often), with `completed` always `<= total` and the final call always `completed === total`. `runner.ts` has no opinion on _how_ progress is displayed — it just calls the function it's handed, exactly like every other optional hook in this file.
- `src/cli/commands/soundness.ts` — validates `--progress` the same way `--queries` is already validated (positive integer or exit code 2), builds the real `process.stderr.write`-backed reporter, and threads it through only when the flag was actually given (so the default path — no `onProgress` at all — is byte-for-byte unchanged from before this option existed).

**Why always stderr, never stdout, in every `--format`.** `markdown`/`json` output's own existing contract (this file's own top-of-file doc comment, unchanged by this addition) is "stdout is the report and nothing else" — `.github/workflows/soundness.yml` captures stdout verbatim as the literal PR-comment body. Progress output landing in that stream even once would corrupt every CI-posted soundness report. Stderr has no such contract to break, and a terminal user sees both streams interleaved anyway.

**Verification.** `test/unit/report/progress.test.ts` — the cadence logic directly, no I/O: reports on schedule, always reports the final call even off-schedule, never double-reports when the final call already lands exactly on a milestone. `test/unit/cli/soundness.test.ts` — `--progress` validation (malformed -> exit 2, matching `--queries`'s own precedent) and that a valid value reaches `runSoundnessFuzz` as a real function, DB-free (`vi.spyOn`, this file's own established pattern). `test/unit/soundness/progress.integration.test.ts` — real Postgres, real `runSoundnessFuzz` call: `onProgress` actually fires, `completed` values are strictly increasing, every call's `total` matches the run's real query count, and the final call's `completed` equals it — run against this sandbox's own local Postgres (LOCALVERIFY-swapped for the file's committed `PostgreSqlContainer` setup, per this project's own established substitution pattern when Docker isn't available locally) before being restored to the committed form. `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check`, and the full unit suite all clean.

**Revisit if:** A future caller wants progress during the schema-publish or tuple-write phases too, not just the checks phase — today's `onProgress` only covers `checkAllQueries`, which is the one phase actually slow enough in practice (per-query resolver round trips) to need it; publish and tuple writes are comparatively fast even over a slow connection, and instrumenting them wasn't asked for or observed to be a problem.

## D-091 — Third full-repo audit: two mechanical fixes landed directly (CI comment-race, the D-089 path bug in 15 test files)

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (third full-repo audit, findings #5 and #7) · **Status:** settled

**Finding #5 — `soundness.yml` had no `concurrency:` group.** Two runs on the same PR (a normal fixup push or rebase a couple minutes apart, each run taking several minutes end to end) could both reach `scripts/post-soundness-comment.mjs`'s create-vs-update decision from the same stale comment-list snapshot and both POST — two stacked, possibly contradictory-verdict comments (each run gets its own random fuzz seed), directly contradicting the workflow's own top-of-file "never stacking" claim. `src/report/prComment.ts`'s `staleCommentIds` doc comment already names this exact race as a scenario its self-healing cleanup is meant to address, but that cleanup only runs on some future single run — nothing closed the race at the source. Fixed: `concurrency: { group: soundness-${{ github.event.pull_request.number }}, cancel-in-progress: true }` — a new push cancels any still-running check for that PR before it can race the comment step, which is strictly better than the existing reactive cleanup and also stops wasted compute on runs that are about to be superseded anyway.

**Finding #7 — the exact D-089 Windows path bug, unfixed in 15 test files.** D-089 fixed `src/cli/commands/doctor.ts`'s `MIGRATIONS_DIR` computation (`new URL(...).pathname`, which leaves an invalid leading slash in front of a Windows drive letter) but its own "Revisit if" clause only grepped `src/` — never `test/`. Every `*.integration.test.ts` file in the suite computes its own `MIGRATIONS_DIR` the same way `doctor.ts` used to, and one file (`test/unit/demo/example-schema.integration.test.ts`) has two more path constants (`SCHEMA_PATH`, `SEED_SCRIPT_PATH`) built the identical broken way. On Windows, `discoverMigrations` would silently report zero migrations for every one of these files' own fresh testcontainers Postgres, and the entire integration suite would fail with "relation does not exist" errors rather than a clear signal about what actually went wrong. Doesn't affect CI today (`ci.yml` runs `ubuntu-latest` only) or any shipped behavior, but D-089's own account establishes this project has real Windows contributors running these files. Fixed mechanically across all 15 affected files (`fileURLToPath(new URL(...))`, matching `env.ts`'s and the now-fixed `doctor.ts`'s own pattern) via a small one-off script applying the identical regex substitution to every file, rather than 15 individual hand-edits that could each introduce a subtly different mistake — spot-checked against the transformed source directly, then verified end-to-end by LOCALVERIFY-swapping one representative file (`test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts`) to a direct local-Postgres connection and confirming its 3 tests still pass, before restoring it to its committed `PostgreSqlContainer` form. (`test/unit/cli/doctor.test.ts` was deliberately excluded from this sweep — its own `.pathname` usage is D-089's regression test _demonstrating_ the bug, not an instance of it; `test/unit/soundness/progress.integration.test.ts` was already correct, per D-090's own account, and needed no change.)

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file (including `.github/workflows/soundness.yml`, whose YAML has no lint step of its own in this project but was hand-reviewed for indentation/syntax). `migrate.integration.test.ts` LOCALVERIFY-run against real local Postgres as described above. `soundness.yml`'s own `concurrency:` block could not be triggered/observed live in this sandbox (no way to run GitHub Actions itself here) — reviewed against GitHub's own documented `concurrency` semantics instead, matching this project's established practice of disclosing what could and couldn't be verified directly (see D-088's own "Verified live... not assumed" section for the same discipline applied to a different unverifiable-here claim).

**Revisit if:** Any future `*.integration.test.ts` file is added by copying an existing one that predates this fix — check it uses `fileURLToPath`, not `.pathname`, the same way D-089's own "Revisit if" should have been read to cover `test/` from the start.

## D-092 — Third full-repo audit, resolver/differential-fuzz/audit-trail cluster: one-check-one-snapshot transactions, an exponential SQL blowup, and five smaller correctness/coverage fixes

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (third full-repo audit, findings #1/#2/#6/#8/#15/#16/#17) · **Status:** settled

**Findings #1 and #2 — `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`, both centered on `sqlRelationMembershipWithWitness`/`fetchReachableFrontier`, fixed together since the second finding's own SQL sits inside the transaction the first finding adds.** Finding #1 (HIGH): every read `productionCheck` issued — the schema lookup, `fetchReachableFrontier`, `fetchTuplesOnFrontier`, `listTupleSubjects` — was its own independent, autocommit `pool.query()` call, with zero shared transaction or snapshot, despite `docs/CONSISTENCY.md` already claiming "the check's own read transaction first confirms `write_log` has advanced ... before it reads a single `relation_tuples` row." A concurrent write landing between two of those queries could let a check stitch a resolution path together from two facts (an edge and a grant) that never coexisted at any single real point in the database's history — a phantom witness in the audit trail. Fixed: `productionCheck` now acquires one `pg.PoolClient`, opens `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ READ ONLY`, and every `relation_tuples`/`write_log` read for that check runs on that one client inside that one transaction (`sqlRelationMembershipWithWitness`, `fetchReachableFrontier`, `fetchTuplesOnFrontier`, and `listTupleSubjects` all now take a `PoolClient`, not `Pool`). `REPEATABLE READ`, not `SERIALIZABLE`: the whole walk is read-only, so it needs snapshot isolation, not `SERIALIZABLE`'s extra write-conflict detection, which would add serialization-failure retries this codebase has no retry logic for. `atToken` pinning composes with this by re-verifying the floor check (`write_log` has advanced to at least `atToken`) as the literal first statement of the new transaction, on the same client — a new module-private `assertTokenObservedOnSnapshot`, not a reused call to `src/store/tokens.ts`'s `assertTokenObserved` (that function takes a `Pool`, and a `PoolClient` cannot structurally satisfy that type; widening its signature would touch a file outside this fix's assigned scope for this audit round — see "Revisit if"). Running the floor check as the transaction's _first_ statement matters concretely: `REPEATABLE READ`'s snapshot is taken at the first query, not at `BEGIN`, so anchoring it there is what guarantees every later read in the check sees at least as much as `atToken` requires, rather than hoping a pool-level pre-check run on a possibly-different connection still describes whatever snapshot this transaction happens to get. One disclosed, deliberate gap: `getConfig`'s `namespace_configs` lookups stay on the plain `pool`, outside the new transaction — `getLatestNamespaceConfig` (`src/schema/publish.ts`) takes a `Pool`, and widening it is also outside this fix's file scope. `WalkContext.schemaCache` (unchanged) already means the same namespace is fetched at most once per check, so the dangerous case (one namespace observed at two different published versions within one check) was already structurally impossible; what's left is the strictly weaker case of two _different_ namespaces in one check each reading as of a very slightly different moment during a concurrent schema publish — narrower, and not what this finding's own counterexample described.

Finding #2 (HIGH): `fetchReachableFrontier`'s recursive CTE had no per-iteration dedup — only a per-path cycle guard and a depth cap — so a node reached via K different upstream paths in one iteration had every one of its outgoing edges independently re-expanded K times in the next, ordinary `WITH RECURSIVE` semantics. A legitimately acyclic "diamond of diamonds" reconvergent group hierarchy (the normal shape of a real nested-group tree, expected per D-021) produces `b^d` rows for only `b·d` distinct nodes, well inside the default `CHECK_MAX_DEPTH` of 25 — confirmed directly against real Postgres before the fix: a 12-level, branching-3 chain of reconvergent diamonds did not return within a 20-second timeout on the unfixed query; the identical query with the fix returns in under 100ms. Fixed: the recursive term's `select` became `select distinct on (subject_ns, subject_id, subject_relation) ...` — confirmed against real Postgres 16 that `DISTINCT ON` is allowed directly in a recursive term (only `ORDER BY`/`LIMIT`/`OFFSET`/aggregates/window functions are rejected there, and none of those are needed here). The delicate part, argued and then independently verified: could collapsing same-iteration duplicates down to one representative path cause the _existing_ cycle guard to incorrectly block a child identity that a _discarded_ duplicate's path wouldn't have blocked, silently losing real reachability (a `false_deny`, not a `false_grant`, but still a genuine correctness regression)? The argument: any identity that could ever appear in a chosen representative's own `path` array must, by construction, already have been added to the frontier as its own row at an earlier iteration — appearing in someone's ancestor list is only possible by having been discovered first — so that identity's own children were already (or will still be) explored via its own frontier entry, independent of what a different node's dedup-selected representative does later. Verified empirically, not just argued: a throwaway differential fuzz script (not committed) generated 3,000 random cyclic/reconvergent graphs (6–25 nodes, out-degree 0–5) and compared the _set_ of reachable `(ns,id,relation)` identities returned by the query with and without `distinct on` — zero mismatches. The dedup is per-iteration, not global across every iteration a node could ever reappear at (a true global "visited" set isn't expressible in a standard-conforming recursive CTE without violating its "the recursive table may be referenced only once" restriction) — a node rediscovered at a much later, unrelated depth can still appear more than once in the raw output, which the pre-existing client-side `dedupeFrontier` already collapsed for correctness before this fix and still does. One accepted, disclosed side effect: because `DISTINCT ON` with no `ORDER BY` picks an unspecified representative among same-iteration duplicates, `checks.depth`'s reported high-water mark for a node with multiple same-iteration parents may not be the _shortest_ available path to it — still a real path through real tuples, never fabricated, just not guaranteed minimal. A deliberate trade of a small amount of diagnostic precision for eliminating an exponential blowup, not a correctness concern (per the reachability argument above).

**Finding #6 (MEDIUM) — `src/resolve/reference/resolver.ts`'s `maxDepth` accepted `NaN`, silently disabling the depth ceiling.** `x > NaN` is always `false` in JavaScript, so `depth > ctx.maxDepth` with `maxDepth: NaN` never fires, leaving branch-local cycle detection (which, by design per D-021, does not fire on a genuinely acyclic-but-deep chain) as the only termination guard — a long enough acyclic chain with `{ maxDepth: NaN }` would blow the real JS call stack, contradicting this file's own documented contract ("Never throws ... never an exception, never a hang"). The exact bug class D-074 already found and fixed once for `assertTokenObserved`. Fixed: `referenceCheck` now validates `Number.isFinite(maxDepth) && maxDepth >= 0` before any recursion starts, throwing a plain `Error` (not the `TypeError` the finding's own suggested code used — this project has no other use of the built-in error subclasses anywhere in `src/`, and the finding's own wording explicitly permitted matching "this project's own established error-throwing convention" instead). Verified live: with the guard temporarily commented out, a 20,000-node acyclic chain called with `{ maxDepth: NaN }` threw exactly the predicted native `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`; restored, the same call now throws the new, clear `Error` immediately, with zero recursion.

**Finding #8 (MEDIUM) — `test/unit/audit/expand.integration.test.ts` had a cyclic-termination test, but only for `expandRelation`'s userset-expansion call site, never for the textually and structurally distinct `expandName` call inside `expandRewrite`'s `tupleToUserset` branch — the same class of gap D-087 already found and fixed once, but for a different file.** Added a second cyclic-termination test driving the cycle through `permission view = viewer | parent->view` with `parent` tuples forming `folder:a -> folder:b -> folder:a` — a plain-relation cycle with no userset-subject nesting anywhere in it, so a pass can only be explained by the `tupleToUserset` branch's own use of the `visiting` guard actually working. Verified the same way this file's own pre-existing cyclic test already was: `visiting.has(key)` temporarily removed, the new test genuinely timed out (15s, confirmed live against real Postgres, not a stack overflow), guard restored and the test suite confirmed byte-identical and passing again.

**Finding #15 (LOW) — only one of two asymmetric dry-run cleanup failure combinations had ever been verified against a real database.** Combination (a) (namespace-cleanup fails, tuple-cleanup succeeds) was already verified live (D-060/PROGRESS.md). Combination (b) (tuple-cleanup fails, namespace-cleanup succeeds — orphaned `relation_tuples` rows for a namespace with no published config left, invisible to `GET /health`) had only ever been exercised by a fully-mocked unit test whose own doc comment explicitly disclaims proving real deletion behavior. `deleteTuple` is idempotent (deleting an already-absent row is a successful no-op, not an error), so deleting a fixture tuple out from under it via a second connection would not force a genuine failure — instead, a real Postgres `BEFORE DELETE` trigger on `relation_tuples`, installed and torn down for the duration of one new test in `dry-run-cleanup.integration.test.ts`, rejects the delete of exactly one specific tuple a given seed is known (via `generateFixture`'s own deterministic reproducibility) to generate. The test then asserts on the _actual_ row state left in `relation_tuples` afterward: exactly the one poisoned tuple survives, under its exact key, while every other fixture tuple and every namespace-config row is gone — a deterministic, database-level fault injection with zero changes to `runner.ts` or any other application source file.

**Finding #16 (LOW) — a comment in `runSoundnessFuzz`'s outer `catch` block claimed `err` might have "itself come from the cleanup call on the success path above," which cannot happen under normal execution** (that path's own `try`/`catch` already logs and swallows its own failure rather than throwing). Reworded to state plainly that the success-path cleanup call already swallows its own errors and cannot ordinarily be the source of `err` here (the only theoretical exception being `console.error` itself throwing synchronously) — comment-only, no behavior change.

**Finding #17 (LOW) — `checks.depth`'s own high-water-mark contract (D-038: "a union with three failing children before a fourth succeeds did real work at each of their depths, not just the last one") had no test distinguishing "reports the failing branches' own depth" from "reports only the winning branch's depth."** Added `permission p = ra | rb | rc | rd` to `production-resolution-path.integration.test.ts`, where `ra`/`rb`/`rc` are each a real, three-level nested-group chain (the same shape and empirically-confirmed depth-3 cost as this file's own pre-existing nested-group fixture) ending in a grant to someone other than the checked subject, and `rd` is a single shallow direct grant to the checked subject, declared last so the union's evaluate-in-order, return-on-first-success loop genuinely evaluates and fully explores `ra`/`rb`/`rc` before ever reaching `rd`. `result.depth` is asserted to be `3`, not the shallow depth `rd` alone would produce.

**Verification.** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file. `npm run test` (410 tests, 36 files) green. Every `*.integration.test.ts` file touched or added to (`production-check-behavior.integration.test.ts`, `cross-resolver-agreement.integration.test.ts`, `production-resolution-path.integration.test.ts`, `expand.integration.test.ts`, `dry-run-cleanup.integration.test.ts`) was LOCALVERIFY-run against this sandbox's real local Postgres (temporarily swapping `PostgreSqlContainer` for a direct `Pool` against a throwaway database, per this task's own standing accommodation for a Docker-less sandbox) and restored to its committed `PostgreSqlContainer` form immediately after; every throwaway database was dropped. The full 5,000-query differential soundness fuzz run (`test/isolation/differential-soundness.fuzz.integration.test.ts`) was also LOCALVERIFY-run against the fixed production resolver: zero `false_grant`, verdict `sound`, ~11s wall-clock. Fuzz-harness power was re-confirmed against this specific (substantially rewritten) resolver, not assumed to still hold from an earlier round: a deliberate "intersection implemented as union" break (short-circuit-allow on the first true branch instead of requiring all) was introduced in `evalRewrite`'s `intersection` case, and the identical 5,000-query run caught it — 33 `false_grant`s — well within the standard budget; the break was then reverted and the clean run re-confirmed.

For finding #1 specifically: the regression test (`production-check-behavior.integration.test.ts`) is fully deterministic, not timing-dependent, despite the property being about a genuine race — `productionCheck`'s own internal `await`s run back-to-back with no externally-controllable pause between them, so there is no reliable way to schedule a concurrent commit to land in the microseconds-wide window between two of its own queries by racing against it directly (confirmed by trying; every naive attempt either always won or always lost the race depending on machine load, never both).
