Instead, the test uses a real Postgres `LOCK TABLE namespace_configs IN ACCESS EXCLUSIVE MODE` (confirmed directly, live, to block a plain `SELECT` against that table from another connection while leaving a concurrent `INSERT` into the _different_, unlocked `relation_tuples` table completely free) to create a wide, fully controlled pause at the check's config-lookup query — which, for a pinned check, always runs strictly after the floor-check query that anchors the `REPEATABLE READ` snapshot. This lets the test deterministically commit a real grant _while the check is blocked mid-transaction_, then confirm the check still resolves `denied`, using the real `productionCheck` function end to end, not a hand-reproduction of its SQL. What this does **not** and cannot prove: the exact real-world timing distribution of how often the original bug would have manifested in production (a function of actual concurrent write load, which this sandbox has no way to characterize) — only that the mechanism is real, reproducible on demand, and closed by the fix. The deliberate-revert check (plain `BEGIN` in place of `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ READ ONLY`) confirmed the identical test fails (`allowed: true` instead of `false`) against the pre-fix behavior, live, before being restored.

**Revisit if:** `src/store/tokens.ts`'s `assertTokenObserved`/`currentToken` ever get their `pool: Pool` parameter widened to a smaller structural type (e.g. `Pick<Pool, 'query'>`, matching `src/schema/publish.ts`'s own `publishOne`'s existing `client: { query: Pool['query'] }` precedent) for some unrelated reason — at that point, `assertTokenObservedOnSnapshot` in `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts` should be deleted and replaced with a direct call to the real `assertTokenObserved(client, atToken)`, closing the small, disclosed duplication between the two functions' "is this token observed" logic (the two are not fully redundant today only because of file-scope boundaries specific to this audit round, not because sharing them would be architecturally wrong — `src/store/tokens.ts` is store infrastructure this file already treats as a sanctioned import, per its own top-of-file doc comment, not part of the reference-vs-production resolver isolation rule §6.2 protects). Similarly, if `src/schema/publish.ts`'s `getLatestNamespaceConfig` is ever widened to accept a `PoolClient`, `WalkContext.pool` and the separate `getConfig` code path in `resolver.ts` should be revisited to fold schema-config reads into the same `REPEATABLE READ` transaction as everything else, closing the narrower, disclosed residual gap finding #1's own fix left open (see that finding's own paragraph above). Finding #2's per-iteration dedup: if a future fuzz run or real-world graph shape is ever found where a node is legitimately rediscovered at many different, unrelated depths (not the "diamond of diamonds" shape this fix targets), the per-iteration-only scope of the dedup should be revisited — today's fix does not attempt true global, cross-iteration dedup, and says so.

**Update:** the structural barrier this entry's own "Revisit if" paragraph named has since been removed — `src/store/tokens.ts`'s `assertTokenObserved` and `currentToken` and `src/schema/publish.ts`'s `getLatestNamespaceConfig` all now take `QueryExecutor`, not `Pool` (D-097), and a real `pg.PoolClient` satisfies `QueryExecutor` structurally, so a `PoolClient` can now be passed where these functions previously required a plain `Pool`. This does **not** change the practical decision this entry made — leaving `getConfig`'s schema-config reads on the plain pool, outside the check's own `REPEATABLE READ` transaction — because that decision was never solely resting on the now-removed structural barrier: this entry's own Finding #1 paragraph already gave a second, independent reason (`WalkContext.schemaCache` fetches a given namespace's config at most once per check, so the one dangerous case — the same namespace observed at two different published versions within one check — was already structurally impossible regardless of what type `getConfig` accepted). That schemaCache-based reasoning is what later work — D-107, from the same later audit round as D-106 — restates as the current justification for the identical "getConfig deliberately stays on the plain pool" shape in `expand.ts`, the other file (beyond `resolver.ts`, covered above) where this same tradeoff applies.

## D-093 — Third full-repo audit, API-surface/CLI/config-validation/migration-locking cluster: eight findings closed

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (third full-repo audit, findings #3/#4/#9/#10/#11/#12/#13/#14) · **Status:** settled

**Findings #3/#13 — `/check` and `/expand`'s request bodies accepted any non-empty string as a namespace, id, or relation, with no identifier-grammar enforcement, unlike every tuple write.** `src/api/server.ts`'s `entityRefSchema` and both routes' bare `relation` field were plain `z.string().min(1)` — `src/store/tuples.ts`'s `validateIdentifiers` (which every `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` call already goes through) enforces `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`/`MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH` from `src/schema/dsl/types.ts`, but `performCheck`/`expand` never route through it. Not an auth bypass — `getConfig` still gates on `object.ns` matching a real _published_ namespace first, and a published name is already grammar-clean by construction — but a malformed `id` (e.g. containing `:` or `#`) can make `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`'s `parseFrontierKeyString` silently mis-split a reconstructed audit-trail path, and every such value that clears the namespace gate lands permanently in the `checks` audit table's plain `text` columns, which carry no `CHECK` constraint. Fixed by reusing `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`/`MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH` in a new `identifierField()` helper, applied to `entityRefSchema`'s `ns`/`id` and both routes' `relation`. `test/unit/api/server.test.ts` gained a companion case per route (`ns: 'has:colon'`) asserting the new 400, next to the existing structurally-malformed-body block.

**Finding #4 — `authz doctor` conflated "Postgres unreachable" with "a migration failed to apply," the identical shape D-073 already fixed for `/health`.** One try/catch wrapped both the connectivity probe and `runMigrations`; a real migration-content failure (a broken `.sql` file, a conflicting concurrent run) printed the exact "Postgres: unreachable ... Check DATABASE_URL in .env" framing right after a "Postgres: reachable" line had already printed — self-contradictory, and it pointed an operator at the one thing (DATABASE_URL) already proven fine. Split into two independent try/catches mirroring D-073's own pattern: the connectivity probe keeps its original "unreachable" framing; a second, independent catch around `runMigrations` reports `Migrations: failed to apply — <message>`, never calling the database unreachable or naming DATABASE_URL. `test/unit/cli/doctor.test.ts` gained two DB-free regression tests (`getPool`/`closePool`/`runMigrations` mocked via `vi.spyOn`, this project's established pattern): a mocked `runMigrations` rejection produces the new, correctly-attributed message with the "reachable" line still printed, and a mocked connectivity failure still produces the original, unchanged message with `runMigrations` never even attempted.

**Findings #12/#9 — every defaulted `EnvSchema` field except the three `optionalString()` ones failed outright on a blank `.env` placeholder instead of falling through to its default, and `EnvSchema`/`optionalString` had zero dedicated tests anywhere in the repo.** `optionalString()`'s whole purpose (D-008) is preprocessing `'' -> undefined` so a blank `.env` line means "use the default" — applied only to `DATABASE_URL`/`SOUNDNESS_FUZZ_SEED`/`ADMIN_API_KEY`. `PORT`/`NODE_ENV`/`LOG_LEVEL`/`CHECK_MAX_DEPTH`/`CHECK_CACHE_TTL_MS`/`SOUNDNESS_FUZZ_QUERIES`/`MAX_CONCURRENCY` (one more than the finding's own six named fields — `CHECK_CACHE_TTL_MS` carries the identical bug, just not currently live since `Number('')` happens to equal its own default of `0`) used plain `.default(...)` with no such preprocessing: `z.coerce.number()` on `''` is `0`, not `NaN`, so a blank numeric field cleared to `0` and then failed whatever positivity check the field itself carries, never reaching `.default(...)`; a blank enum field failed because `''` isn't a declared value. Latent, not live — `.env.example` never ships any of these fields blank today. Fixed by factoring the `'' -> undefined` preprocessing into a shared `blankToUndefined` helper and building `optionalNumber()`/`optionalEnum()` on top of it, alongside `optionalString()` (which now also takes an optional `minLength`/message, used by the `ADMIN_API_KEY` fix below); every defaulted field now goes through one of the three. A first draft of `optionalEnum` typed its generic parameter as a string tuple (`z.ZodEnum<[string, ...string[]]>`), which doesn't match zod v4's actual `ZodEnum<T extends EnumLike>` shape (a mapped record, not a tuple — zod v3's shape, checked directly against this project's real installed v4.4.3 rather than assumed) — the mismatch silently produced a broadened, wrong inferred type for `env.LOG_LEVEL` that only surfaced as a real `tsc` failure three call sites away, in `src/api/server.ts`'s own `{ level: env.LOG_LEVEL }`; caught by running `tsc` before trusting the fix, not by inspection, and corrected to a plain `z.ZodType<string>` constraint. New `test/unit/config/env.test.ts` (previously zero dedicated tests — confirmed via `grep -rn "EnvSchema|optionalString" test/` before writing anything) exercises `EnvSchema.safeParse()` directly against a fully-populated valid object, an entirely empty object (documenting the real premise, checked directly rather than assumed from the finding's own wording, that nothing in this schema is actually "required" — everything falls through to a default or to `undefined`, by D-008's own deliberate design), the `''`-blank shape for every `optionalString()` field and every now-fixed defaulted field, an invalid `NODE_ENV`/`LOG_LEVEL`, and a non-numeric/zero/negative/non-integer `PORT` — 21 tests, all against the corrected schema.

**Finding #10 — a flood of failed-`ADMIN_API_KEY` requests against any gated route was, by design of D-065's own fix, counted by no rate limiter at all.** D-065 correctly moved rate-limit counting to run _after_ `requireAdminAuth` so a flood of wrong-key requests could never exhaust the budget a legitimate key-holder needs — the necessary but never-weighed consequence is that `@fastify/rate-limit` gives a route with its own `config.rateLimit` _only_ that route's limiter, never a global fallback underneath it, and Fastify's preHandler chain stops the instant `requireAdminAuth` rejects, so `writeRateLimit`/`gatedReadRateLimit`'s own counting hook never runs for a failed-auth request — confirmed by this project's own existing D-065 regression test (repeated wrong-key requests all return 401, never 429). Fixed with a second, independent, much coarser limiter (`authFloodGuard`, 1000 requests/minute/IP) positioned _before_ `requireAdminAuth` in every gated route's own `preHandler` array. Deliberately hand-rolled rather than a second `app.rateLimit(...)` call from the same `@fastify/rate-limit` registration: read directly from that plugin's own source before ruling it out, not assumed — every limiter check born from one `app.register(rateLimit, ...)` call shares one `rateLimitRan` flag per request, set by whichever check runs first; a second `app.rateLimit(...)` limiter ahead of `requireAdminAuth` would set that flag on every request and silently disable the existing per-route limiters entirely, not add a second layer alongside them. `ADMIN_API_KEY` also gained a `.min(32)` in `EnvSchema` (via `optionalString`'s new second parameter, finding #12 above) with a specific message, so a trivially short key fails fast at startup instead of being silently accepted.

**A real bug caught live while verifying finding #10, not shipped:** the first version of `authFloodGuard`'s wiring used one `const gatedPreHandlers = [authFloodGuard, requireAdminAuth]` array shared across all five gated routes' own `preHandler` option. `@fastify/rate-limit`'s `onRoute` hook appends its counting handler onto a route's `preHandler` array via a plain in-place `Array.prototype.push` — with one shared array object, all five routes' own rate-limit handlers accumulated onto the _one_ array every route's `preHandler` option actually pointed to, and (because of the same `rateLimitRan` sharing described above) only the first of those five accumulated checks ever counted anything; the other four silently no-op'd on every request regardless of route. This broke the _existing_, pre-finding-#10 `writeRateLimit` budget outright — `test/unit/api/rate-limit.test.ts`'s own pre-existing 21-request test failed (`expected 400 to be 429`) the moment this draft landed, which is what caught it. Fixed by turning `gatedPreHandlers` into a function that returns a fresh array on every call, so each route's `preHandler` option is its own distinct array object. Full test suite re-run clean afterward (403/403).

**Finding #11 — `authz check --at-token ''` (or whitespace-only) was silently treated as the legitimate token `0` instead of rejected.** `Number('')` and `Number('   ')` both coerce to `0`, which passes both `Number.isInteger` and `atToken < 0` — unlike `soundness run --queries=""`, which rejects blank input via its own `<= 0` check (a trick that doesn't carry over here, since `0` genuinely is a valid `--at-token`). Fixed with an explicit `options.atToken.trim() === ''` guard before the existing numeric checks, exiting 2 the same way the existing malformed-token path already does — also brings `check.ts` in line with `checkBodySchema`'s own `z.number().int().nonnegative().optional()`, which already rejects a string-typed empty value for the equivalent HTTP operation. `test/unit/cli/check.test.ts` gained two cases (`''` and `'   '`) next to the existing not-a-number/negative cases.

**Finding #14 — `runMigrations` read `schema_migrations`, computed what's pending, and applied it with nothing serializing that sequence against a second, genuinely concurrent call — the identical "read pending state, then write, unserialized" shape D-080 fixed for `publishOne` and D-083 fixed for `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`, never extended to migrations.** Two instances cold-starting together during a rolling deploy could both read the same "pending" snapshot and both attempt to apply it. Closed with a third, distinct `pg_advisory_lock` key pair — `MIGRATIONS_LOCK_CLASSID`/`MIGRATIONS_LOCK_OBJID` (ASCII `'migr'` = `0x6d696772`, `objid` fixed `0`, matching `WRITE_LOG_LOCK_CLASSID`'s own naming convention and, being the two-integer-argument form, structurally non-colliding with `publishOne`'s single-bigint `hashtext()` keyspace regardless of the value chosen) — held for the entire read-then-apply sequence. **Session-scoped (`pg_advisory_lock`/`pg_advisory_unlock`), not transaction-scoped like D-080's and D-083's own locks — a deliberate, load-bearing difference.** Those two lock a single operation that is itself one transaction start to finish, so "released automatically at COMMIT/ROLLBACK" fits exactly; `runMigrations` cannot be one transaction, because `MIGRATIONS_TABLE_DDL` needs to commit and become visible immediately (see below) and — this function's own existing, load-bearing contract — each migration is applied in its own transaction, on its own separate pooled connection, specifically so an earlier migration's success survives a later one's failure. A `pg_advisory_xact_lock` tied to one connection's one transaction cannot stay meaningfully held across a loop whose real work commits on _different_ connections one migration at a time. **Two real bugs caught live during this fix's own concurrent-call verification, neither shipped:** (1) a first draft left `MIGRATIONS_TABLE_DDL` running unlocked, up front, exactly as before — two genuinely concurrent first-ever calls against a fresh database reproduced a real `pg_type_typname_nsp_index` unique-constraint violation from Postgres's own `pg_catalog`, 100% reproducible; moving the DDL inside the lock (before the read, not just before the apply loop) closed it. (2) Moving that DDL inside a `pg_advisory_xact_lock`-wrapped explicit transaction (an intermediate draft, before settling on session-scoped) broke even a single, non-concurrent call outright — the DDL stayed uncommitted and invisible to the per-migration connections' own separate transactions, which then failed with `relation "schema_migrations" does not exist` on their very first `insert`. The final, session-scoped design (DDL runs as its own immediately-committed statement; per-migration transactions proceed exactly as before; the lock is released in `finally`, its own failure never masking whatever the `try` block already threw — the same "cleanup's own outcome must never overwrite the real error" reasoning D-066 already applies to `runSoundnessFuzz`'s cleanup step) avoids both. A new `test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts` describe block, its own dedicated fresh container (a database that genuinely has zero migrations applied yet at the moment both calls fire), fires two real `runMigrations` calls via `Promise.all` and asserts one applies every migration, the other applies none, and `schema_migrations` ends with exactly one row per migration — mirroring `tuple-store.integration.test.ts`'s own real-race testing idiom. LOCALVERIFY-confirmed the race reproduces with the fix reverted (100% across repeated runs) and is closed with it restored, before the committed test was written.

**Verification:** `npx prettier --check`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm run lint` all clean on every touched file. `npm run test`: 403/403 passing, including all new/modified tests above. Every `*.integration.test.ts` change (`test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts`) LOCALVERIFY-run against this sandbox's real local Postgres (`postgres://authz:authz@127.0.0.1:5432/`, throwaway databases created via the superuser role and dropped immediately after each run) before being restored to its committed `PostgreSqlContainer` form — confirmed via `grep -n "LOCALVERIFY\|authz_lv"` on the restored file that no swap artifact was left behind. The real `authz doctor` CLI was also run end-to-end against a throwaway database, once for a clean success and once against a deliberately broken migration file, confirming the finding #4 message split live, not only through mocks.

**Revisit if:** Finding #10's `authFloodGuard` state is a plain in-memory `Map` local to each `buildServer()` call — correct and unbounded-in-practice for a single-instance deployment (this project's only current one, D-088), but would need a shared store (Redis, matching `@fastify/rate-limit`'s own optional `RedisStore`) if this API is ever horizontally scaled behind a load balancer, the same condition that would already force revisiting `writeRateLimit`/`gatedReadRateLimit`'s own per-instance budgets. Finding #14's session-scoped lock assumes `runMigrations` is always called to completion on a connection it fully controls; if a future caller ever needs to cancel a call mid-flight (not something any caller does today), confirm the lock still releases correctly rather than leaking on a pooled connection that gets abandoned instead of properly closed.

## D-094 — Third full-repo audit continued: canonical same-operator flattening across parens, a real republish/write-path test, and the malformed-schema fixture actually compiled for the first time

**Date:** 2026-08-17 · **Phase:** post-9 (third full-repo audit, findings #18/#19/#20) · **Status:** settled

**Finding #20 — `(a|b)|c` and `a|(b|c)` compiled to two different tree shapes for the exact same rewrite rule.** `src/schema/dsl/parser.ts`'s `parseTerm`/`parseExpression` flatten a same-operator chain into one flat n-ary node, but only checked `left.kind` against the operator being applied, never the freshly parsed `right`. Reproduced directly against the real compiler (`tsx`, both `|` and `&`) before changing anything, per this task's own instruction: `(a | b) | c` already came out flat (`{union: [a, b, c]}` — `left` is the parenthesized union, so the existing left-only check caught it), but `a | (b | c)` came out nested (`{union: [a, {union: [b, c]}]}` — `right` is the parenthesized union, which the left-only check never looks at). D-011 only settled cross-operator precedence (`&` binding tighter than `|`/`-`); it says nothing about same-operator flattening across a paren boundary, so this wasn't an already-accepted tradeoff, just an asymmetry nobody had exercised from both sides at once. **Fix chosen: flatten symmetrically on both sides** (`flattenChildren`, a small shared helper `parseTerm`/`parseExpression` both call, checks `left.kind`/`right.kind` against the target kind and merges children accordingly) — not the alternative of tracking a "came from parens" flag and refusing to flatten across any paren boundary at all. `&`/`|` are genuinely associative, so `(a|b)|c`, `a|(b|c)`, and the unparenthesized `a|b|c` all describe the identical set of subjects; a flat n-ary node is already this parser's own existing default shape for an unparenthesized chain, and both resolvers already walk `children` as an arbitrary-length array with no ordering dependency (union/intersection are the two rewrite-rule kinds this project's own architecture already treats as order-insensitive) — so full flattening costs nothing downstream and makes the compiled AST canonical for logically equivalent source, which is exactly what `rewrite-rules.test.ts`'s own exact-shape `toEqual` checks and the D-036/D-037 resolution-path audit trail both implicitly want. The paren-boundary-tracking alternative would have required threading new parse-time state with no corresponding field in the stable `RewriteRule` shape (`types.ts`'s own "deliberately plain, JSON-safe data" contract), just to make two logically identical schemas compile to two different trees on purpose — more code for a worse invariant. A mixed-kind group (`a & (b | c) & d`) is untouched either way: `right.kind` is `'union'`, not `'intersection'`, so the merge check simply doesn't fire, and the parenthesized union stays a distinct nested child, correctly. Six new tests in `test/unit/schema/rewrite-rules.test.ts` pin this down for both operators: `(a|b)|c`, `a|(b|c)`, and the unparenthesized `a|b|c` all now produce the byte-identical flat 3-child union (same for `&`/intersection), and a dedicated test confirms a differently-kinded parenthesized group is never absorbed into the outer chain.

**Finding #18 — `getLatestNamespaceConfig` (the function that actually gates every real tuple write) had no test proving it picks up a namespace's _second_ published version.** `grep -rln "publishSchema" test/` confirmed every existing integration test publishes a namespace exactly once; the one test that republishes to a second version (`test/unit/api/server.integration.test.ts`) only asserts `listLatestNamespaceVersions` (a sibling, `/health`-facing read) reports the new version, never that a write against the new version's own new relation actually succeeds. Not a suspected bug — `publishOne`'s SQL is a plain `insert`, and `getLatestNamespaceConfig`'s own query is `order by version desc limit 1`, both about as simple as this codebase gets — but a real, specific gap on a behavior that matters: nothing had ever proven the write path's schema lookup isn't reading some cached or stale version. Added to `test/unit/store/tuple-store.integration.test.ts` (the file whose own top-of-file doc comment already claimed to exercise `getLatestNamespaceConfig`, and whose sibling `describe` block, "a tuple write is validated against the latest published schema for its namespace," is the exact natural home — the claim wasn't true until this test actually imported and called it): publish namespace N v1 with only an `owner` relation; write a tuple against `owner`, confirm it succeeds; write a tuple against `collaborator` (a relation that doesn't exist yet) and confirm it's rejected with `undeclared_relation` — the control half, proving a later successful write against that same relation name can't be mistaken for a write path that never validated relation names at all; republish the same namespace as v2, adding `collaborator`; write a tuple against `collaborator` and confirm it now succeeds; call `getLatestNamespaceConfig` directly and confirm its `relations` map now contains `collaborator`; and, per this finding's own "optionally" suggestion, call `productionCheck` against the new v2-only relation's permission and confirm it resolves `allowed: true` — proving the production resolver's own `getLatestNamespaceConfig` call (`src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`) picked up v2 too, not just the write path.

**Finding #19 — `schema/malformed-example.authz` was cited by name — in its own header comment and `docs/RELATIONS.md:46` — as "a worked example of a rejection," but nothing in the repo ever actually compiled it.** `grep -rn "malformed-example\.authz" --include="*.ts" .` returned zero matches before this fix. Read the fixture's own committed content first (per this task's instruction) rather than guessing: two permissions on its one `document` namespace each reference an undeclared relation — `permission view = viewer | editor | owner` (line 10; `viewer` was never declared, only `owner`/`editor` were) and `permission edit = editor | admin` (line 11; `admin` was never declared) — so the expected result is `compileSchema` rejecting with two `undeclared_reference` errors, at lines 10 and 11 respectively, confirmed directly against the real compiler via `tsx` before writing the test. Added to `test/unit/schema/schema-validation.test.ts` (the existing home for every other schema-compiler rejection test): reads the fixture straight off disk via `readFileSync`/`fileURLToPath` (matching this project's own established pattern in `test/unit/demo/example-schema.integration.test.ts` and `publish.integration.test.ts` for reading real committed source rather than retyping it) — never a copy of its source pasted into the test, so a future hand-edit to the fixture can't silently drift out of sync with what this test actually asserts — and asserts both located errors by `member`/`line`, plus that every error in the result formats as `line N: ...` per this task's own "never a generic parse error" non-negotiable.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check` clean on every file touched (`src/schema/dsl/parser.ts`, `test/unit/schema/rewrite-rules.test.ts`, `test/unit/schema/schema-validation.test.ts`, `test/unit/store/tuple-store.integration.test.ts`, this file). `npm run test` (401 tests, 36 files) green, including the new DB-free tests. Finding #18's new test LOCALVERIFY-run against this sandbox's real local Postgres (`postgres://authz:authz@127.0.0.1:5432/`, two throwaway databases — the file's main fixture pool and its separately-containered `raceContainer`-backed lock-contention block both needed swapping, since the file starts two independent Postgres containers, not one) — all 18 tests in the file passed, `test/unit/store/tuple-store.integration.test.ts` restored to its committed `PostgreSqlContainer` form immediately after, both throwaway databases dropped.

**Revisit if:** Finding #20 — a future rewrite-rule kind is added that's also associative (none of `exclusion`/`tupleToUserset` are, so this doesn't apply to either today) and wants the same canonical-flattening treatment; extend `flattenChildren`'s call sites deliberately, don't assume it generalizes for free. Finding #18 — a future schema-versioning feature (pinning a write to an older, non-latest version, say) changes what "the write path" is even supposed to look up; this test's own "v1 then v2, same namespace" shape should be revisited alongside whatever replaces `getLatestNamespaceConfig`'s "always latest" contract.

## D-095 — Deterministic simulation testing runs against a fake tuple-store adapter, never inside real Postgres

**Date:** 2026-08-20 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST scoping — design-panel review, not yet built) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** deterministic simulation testing for this project's storage/write path — `productionCheck`, `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`, `runMigrations`, `publishSchema` — swaps the tuple-store seam for an in-memory implementation that models Postgres's documented transaction/lock/MVCC semantics (`REPEATABLE READ` visibility anchored at a transaction's first query, the three distinct advisory-lock keyspaces and lifetimes this project already relies on, `fetchReachableFrontier`'s recursive-CTE reachability semantics) closely enough to inject partial writes, a crash between a commit landing and the caller observing it, reordered or duplicated operations, and seeded concurrent interleaving — and asserts this project's own consistency invariants hold across all of it. Full design in `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`, chosen by a three-way design panel (abstract interface seam vs. SQL-pattern-matching fake driver vs. a narrow hybrid extending `publishOne`'s existing `{ query: Pool['query'] }` precedent) scored independently by two judges against fidelity, blast radius, long-term maintainability, fault-class coverage, and fit with this project's own conventions; the narrow hybrid won on both scorecards.

**Alternative rejected:** running the same fault-injection scheme against a real, disposable Postgres instance — e.g. `pg_terminate_backend`, killing and restarting a container mid-write, or a proxy that drops/delays/duplicates wire traffic.

**Why it lost:** Postgres is not itself deterministic or crash-injectable from outside its own process. This project doesn't own the database engine the way TigerBeetle or FoundationDB own their whole storage stack — there is no way to make a real Postgres instance replay an identical seed byte-for-byte, and claiming otherwise would be the kind of overclaim this project's own soundness-report language already has a working discipline against making ("a measured result of this run's fuzz budget, not a claim of general security"). Simulating at the storage seam is honest about what actually gets proven: this project's own application-level concurrency and consistency logic under adversarial scheduling — not Postgres's internal crash-recovery correctness, which is Postgres's job to prove, not this project's.

**Revisit if:** a future need arises to validate a Postgres-specific failure mode this fake cannot model by construction (WAL corruption, replication-lag consistency, real disk-full behavior) — at that point real-Postgres fault injection becomes a second, narrower, explicitly-scoped effort layered on top of this one, not a replacement for it.

---

## D-096 — Every `pg.Pool` in this project now listens for `'error'` — the real cause of `test-integration`'s intermittent "Unhandled Errors" failures

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (CI reliability) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** every `new Pool(...)` construction in this repo — the one real production pool (`src/store/client.ts`'s `getPool`) and all 21 pools across the 16 `*.integration.test.ts` files — now attaches a `pool.on('error', (err) => { console.error(...) })` listener immediately after construction.

`pg`'s own documentation states the pool emits `'error'` on behalf of any _idle_ client that hits a background, connection-level error (the server restarting, a network blip, a container being stopped while a pooled connection is technically still open) — and Node's default behavior for an `EventEmitter`'s unhandled `'error'` event is to throw, crashing the entire process over a single idle connection's transient error. `grep -rn "\.on('error'" src/ test/` returned zero matches before this fix, on either the real production pool or any test file's pool — this project had never actually guarded against `pg`'s own documented gotcha, anywhere.

This is the real, confirmed root cause of the intermittent `test-integration` CI failures first seen on PR #39 and again on the push-triggered `main` run for the D-095 docs commit (`d87da55`, a 100%-documentation diff — proof by construction that the failure could not have been a real regression in any code, since no code changed) — both showed the identical signature: every real test passing (110/110, then 116/116), followed by a Vitest "Uncaught Exception" section reporting `error: terminating connection due to administrator command` (Postgres error code `57P01`, sent to a client when its backend is terminated — exactly what happens to any client still checked into a pool when that pool's own Testcontainers Postgres container is stopped in `afterAll`). Previously diagnosed only as "a container-teardown race, re-running fixes it" (true, but incomplete — a re-run only ever _avoided_ the race window, it never closed it). With this fix, the exact same race can still occur, but the resulting `'error'` event now has a listener: it's logged, not thrown, and the test run's real pass/fail result is finally determined only by real assertions.

This is not only a CI-reliability fix — the identical, previously-unguarded gap exists in `src/store/client.ts`'s real production pool. Before this fix, a live production incident as mundane as Postgres restarting during a routine maintenance window, or a network blip resetting one idle connection, would have crashed the entire `authz-api` Node process, not merely failed the one in-flight request that happened to be using that connection. The test-file fix and the production fix are the same code, applied everywhere `new Pool(...)` appears — found via the CI flake, but load-bearing well beyond it.

**Alternative rejected:** treating this purely as a CI-infrastructure problem — reducing `test-integration`'s parallelism, adding a delay between `pool.end()` and `container.stop()` in every file's `afterAll`, or configuring Vitest to not fail the run on an "Unhandled Errors" section.

**Why it lost:** every one of those would have narrowed the race's _timing window_ without fixing what actually crashes the process when the window is hit — the real defect is the missing listener, not the race itself (the race is an accepted, harmless-once-guarded reality of stopping a container that might still have an idle pooled connection). Reducing parallelism or adding delays would have made the failure rarer without eliminating it, and would have done nothing at all for the identical, more consequential gap in production's own pool. Suppressing Vitest's "Unhandled Errors" reporting wholesale would also have hidden a genuinely different, real bug surfacing through the exact same mechanism in the future.

**Revisit if:** a future `pg.Pool` construction is added anywhere in this repo without this same listener — there is currently no lint rule enforcing it (a `no-new-pool-without-error-listener`-shaped custom ESLint rule, mirroring this project's own existing `no-restricted-imports` precedent for `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion` in D-081, would close that gap mechanically rather than relying on this entry being remembered).

---

## D-097 — DST Phase D0 lands: the storage seam is genuinely swappable, and it already found a real bug

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST D0, `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** `src/store/tuples.ts`'s `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`/`listTuplesByObject`/`listTuplesBySubject`, `src/store/tokens.ts`'s `currentToken`/`assertTokenObserved`, and `src/schema/publish.ts`'s `getLatestNamespaceConfig` are narrowed from concrete `pg.Pool`/`pg.PoolClient` to two new shared structural types (`src/store/query-executor.ts`'s `QueryExecutor`/`ConnectionSource`) — extending `publishOne`'s own pre-existing `{ query: Pool['query'] }` precedent rather than reinventing it. A real, working in-memory fake (`src/store/dst/`) now satisfies these types: a connection/transaction engine with a per-connection uncommitted write buffer and commit-sequence tagging, a trivial exact-match SQL-shape executor for the nine shapes `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple`/`currentToken`/`getLatestNamespaceConfig` actually issue, and one real fault: a crash injectable at any statement inside an open transaction. `test/unit/store/dst/tuple-store.dst.test.ts` runs the real, unmodified `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` against it — no Postgres, no Testcontainers, no Docker — and proves the flagship claim: a crash injected between the tuple insert and the write-log insert leaves neither row behind, and a fresh, uncrashed retry still succeeds. Fail-checked live, not assumed: the crash mechanism disabled and confirmed the three crash-dependent tests genuinely fail (not just pass by construction), then restored.

**A real bug, found and fixed along the way, not hypothetical.** Building a fake connection that stays genuinely dead after a crash — matching real Postgres, where a terminated connection can't run anything else either, including its own `ROLLBACK` — immediately exposed that `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` (`src/store/tuples.ts`) and `publishSchema` (`src/schema/publish.ts`) all shared the same latent defect: `catch (err) { await client.query('ROLLBACK'); throw err; }`, with no inner try/catch around the `ROLLBACK` call itself. If that `ROLLBACK` throws — which it does, on a connection that has genuinely died — its failure silently replaces `err`, and the caller sees "connection terminated" instead of whatever actually caused the original failure. Confirmed live both ways: reverted the fix and watched the wrong error surface (`DST fake connection: query issued on a connection that has already crashed`, not the real cause), then restored and confirmed the correct one surfaces. Fixed with the same "cleanup's own outcome must never overwrite the thing that actually matters" pattern `src/store/migrate.ts`'s `runMigrations` already established for its own advisory-unlock cleanup: the `ROLLBACK` call now runs inside its own try/catch, swallowing a rollback failure rather than letting it mask `err`. `src/store/migrate.ts`'s own per-migration apply loop has the identical shape and was **not** touched here — outside this task's file scope, disclosed rather than silently fixed in passing; see "Revisit if."

**Alternative rejected (for the seam type):** reusing `Pool['query']` verbatim as `QueryExecutor.query`'s own type, matching `publishOne`'s literal existing annotation.

**Why it lost:** `Pool['query']` is `pg`'s own heavily overloaded signature (a `Submittable` stream, a `QueryArrayConfig`, a `QueryConfig`, or plain text+values) — nothing in this codebase ever calls it any way but the last, simplest one. Requiring the fake's own `.query()` implementation to satisfy all four overloads just to type-check would have meant either a much larger, mostly-dead implementation or unsafe casts papering over the mismatch. A hand-written, narrower signature (`query<Row>(text: string, params?): Promise<{rows: Row[]; rowCount: number | null}>`) covers everything this codebase actually calls, and a real `Pool`/`PoolClient` still satisfies it structurally (one of its own overloads already has that exact shape) — so every existing caller keeps working with zero changes, confirmed by `npx tsc --noEmit` passing clean across the whole repo with no caller-site edits anywhere.

**Alternative rejected (for D0's own scope):** also promoting `getLatestNamespaceConfig`'s sibling functions in `src/schema/publish.ts` — `publishSchema`, `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion`, `listLatestNamespaceVersions` — to the same narrowed type while already in the file.

**Why it lost:** `getLatestNamespaceConfig` is narrowed because `writeTuple`'s own schema validation calls it unconditionally, before ever opening a transaction — a genuine, necessary dependency, not scope creep. `publishSchema` depends on `publishOne`'s own namespace-scoped advisory lock, which is explicitly D1's job (`docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s phase plan); narrowing its type now, ahead of the fake actually being able to honor that lock's real semantics, would create a type that compiles but that D0's own fake cannot yet correctly satisfy for that function's real behavior — a type promise this phase can't keep. Left untouched, with the dependency boundary stated plainly here rather than silently expanded.

**Revisit if:** D1 lands (advisory locks) — at that point `publishSchema`/`runMigrations`'s own `Pool`/`PoolClient` parameters get the identical narrowing treatment, and `src/store/migrate.ts`'s own identical rollback-masking gap (named above, not fixed here) should be closed in the same pass, using the exact same pattern. D2 lands (snapshot transactions) — `src/store/dst/state.ts`'s `commitSeq` field, populated correctly since D0 but unused by any read, becomes load-bearing for real snapshot filtering.

---

## D-098 — DST Phase D1 lands: advisory locks are a real, blocking, Promise-based engine, and both remaining Pool-narrowings close

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST D1, `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** a new module, `src/store/dst/locks.ts`, implements `withAdvisoryLock`'s in-memory half — a real FIFO wait-queue mutex, keyed by opaque string, shared across every `FakeConnectionImpl` on one `FakeStoreState`. `acquireLock` returns a `Promise` that resolves immediately if a key is free, or only once genuinely granted otherwise — never a boolean flag or a timer — so two connections driven concurrently via `Promise.all([...])` interleave at that `await` exactly like two real Postgres backends, one blocked behind the other's held lock, with no seeded scheduler needed for this (D4's own job). Two lifetime models share the one engine: `scope: 'xact'` locks release in a blanket sweep at `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` (`releaseLocksForConnection(..., 'xact')`); `scope: 'session'` locks release only via an explicit matching `pg_advisory_unlock` or the holding connection dying outright (`releaseLocksForConnection(..., 'all')`) — never at that connection's own `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`, and never at an ordinary `.release()` back to a pool, matching Postgres's own documented behavior. `src/store/dst/connection.ts` special-cases all four real lock SQL texts this codebase issues — `tuples.ts`'s global write-log lock, `publish.ts`'s per-namespace `hashtext($1)` lock, and `migrate.ts`'s session lock plus its own explicit unlock — directly, before a query ever reaches `shapes.ts`'s `lookupShape`, since locking needs connection identity and genuine blocking that an ordinary synchronous `ShapeHandler` can't express; D0's `advisoryLockHandler` no-op stopgap is removed. `query()` is genuinely `async` now (D0's version deliberately wasn't, having nothing to await).

`src/schema/publish.ts`'s `publishSchema` and `src/store/migrate.ts`'s `runMigrations` are narrowed from concrete `pg.Pool` to `ConnectionSource` — the exact move D-097 deferred to this phase. `migrate.ts`'s `MIGRATIONS_LOCK_CLASSID`/`MIGRATIONS_LOCK_OBJID` are now exported, following `tuples.ts`'s own `WRITE_LOG_LOCK_CLASSID` precedent, so a test can acquire the _real_ production lock key directly. `migrate.ts`'s per-migration apply loop's own rollback-masking gap — named but explicitly not fixed in D-097 ("outside this task's file scope") — is closed here with the identical try/catch-around-`ROLLBACK` pattern already used in `tuples.ts`/`publish.ts`.

`test/unit/store/dst/advisory-lock.dst.test.ts` (20 tests) exercises this end to end, no Postgres: a seeded sweep (`it.each`, 8 seeds, each varying bystander count, write/delete mix, and which side races) generalizing the real D-083 regression test's own hand-orchestrated scenario (`test/unit/store/tuple-store.integration.test.ts`) — every bystander `writeTuple`/`deleteTuple` call proven to genuinely not settle (`flushMicrotasks`, 20 drained microtask ticks — no wall-clock `sleep` needed, unlike the real-Postgres version, since nothing here depends on real I/O timing) until the lock holder commits, every bystander token then strictly exceeds the holder's, no token collides, no write lost; a namespace-hash-lock test proving per-namespace scoping (a concurrent acquire for a _different_ namespace never blocks, the _same_ namespace's does); a session-lock test proving the migrations lock survives an unrelated `COMMIT` on its own holding connection and releases only on explicit unlock; and D1's own named exit-criterion test, the session-lock-crash test — a connection holding the session lock crashes, and a previously-blocked waiter on a second connection is granted the moment it dies. Fail-checked live: `acquireLock` temporarily forced to always grant immediately regardless of held state; 11 of 12 blocking-dependent tests failed with clear, specific assertion errors (the one test that didn't require blocking to pass unaffected, as expected); restored via `cp`, confirmed byte-identical via `diff`, all 20 reconfirmed green.

**Alternative rejected:** giving each of the three real lock call sites its own bespoke in-memory tracking (three separate ad hoc "is X locked" booleans/sets scattered across `shapes.ts` handlers), rather than one shared, generic engine.

**Why it lost:** `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md` itself already commits to `withAdvisoryLock` as "one generalized helper, three real call sites" specifically because Postgres documents the two-integer and single-bigint-hash advisory-lock forms as structurally non-colliding keyspaces this project's own correctness already depends on (D-080, D-083, and `migrate.ts`'s own `MIGRATIONS_LOCK_CLASSID` doc comment all reason about this explicitly) — three independent, ad hoc mechanisms would each need to separately reimplement genuine blocking (not just mutual exclusion) and would offer no structural guarantee that a two-int-form key and a hash-form key can never collide, the same guarantee three bespoke `Map<string, boolean>`s would have to get right by convention rather than by construction. One engine, three callers each supplying their own key and scope, makes that non-collision and the two lifetime models' correctness a property of one reviewed module instead of three.

**Alternative rejected (queueing discipline):** no explicit queue at all — a blocked `acquireLock` call retries on a short interval (polling) until the key becomes free.

**Why it lost:** polling is nondeterministic in exactly the way this whole effort exists to avoid (D-095) — two waiters polling at the "same" interval have no principled resolution for which one wins a given free-up, and a real bug that only reproduces under one specific grant order could vanish or reappear across identical runs depending on polling-loop scheduling noise the test author never controls. A FIFO queue makes grant order a deterministic function of arrival order alone, with no timing dependency at all.

**Revisit if:** D2 lands (snapshot transactions) and needs `withSnapshotTransaction`'s own `REPEATABLE READ` visibility model — nothing here changes for that; D4 lands (the seeded scheduler) and wants to explore lock-acquisition ordering itself as part of its interleaving space, not just each call site's own internal statement order — at that point `acquireLock`'s immediate-grant-when-free fast path may need to become schedulable too, rather than always resolving synchronously.

---

## D-099 — DST Phase D2 lands: `REPEATABLE READ` snapshot visibility is real, `productionCheck` runs through the fake unmodified, and the D-092 phantom-witness regression is closed under deterministic control

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST D2, `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** `src/store/dst/shapes.ts`'s `ShapeContext` gains `visibleAsOf: number | undefined` — `undefined` means "see everything currently committed" (D0/D1's own unchanged behavior for every ordinary connection), a real number means "only rows committed at or before this `commitSeq`." Every existing read handler (`listByObjectHandler`, `listBySubjectHandler`, `maxTokenHandler`) now honors it via one shared `isVisible` helper, purely additively — D0/D1's own 28 tests stay green, untouched. `src/store/dst/connection.ts` recognizes the exact literal text `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`'s `productionCheck` issues, `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ READ ONLY`, as a third transaction mode (`TxState.Snapshot`, distinct from `Open`'s write-buffering): `snapshotSeq` is captured — `state.nextCommitSeq - 1` — the first time a real (non-transaction-control) statement runs after entering `Snapshot` mode, never at `BEGIN` itself, replicating Postgres's own documented anchor-at-first-query semantics exactly. Enforced by construction, not just documented: `bufferOp` throws if a write is ever attempted inside `Snapshot` mode, mirroring `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own "Two grafts" discipline of enforcing an ordering invariant structurally rather than leaving it an assumption.

Three new SQL shapes are registered, matching `resolver.ts`'s real literal query text exactly: `listTupleSubjects` (the tuple-to-userset hop), `fetchTuplesOnFrontier` (the batched `unnest` join, snapshot-filtered), and `fetchReachableFrontier` — deliberately **not** a real multi-level recursive BFS. `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own phased plan makes the real, differentially-verified-against-Postgres frontier BFS (`fetchReachableFrontierVia`) D3's own named deliverable, parallelizable with D2 but not part of it. D2's own handler answers correctly for exactly the part of the real query's semantics that needs no recursion — the seed row, always present in the real CTE's own `UNION ALL` regardless of whether any recursion happens — and **throws**, rather than silently under-reporting reachability, the moment a real, snapshot-visible userset-subject edge exists on the `(object, relation)` it was asked about **and** the caller's own remaining depth budget (`maxDepth`, the real query's own `$4`) is high enough that real recursion could actually have reached it — matching the real recursive term's own `m.depth < $4` guard at the seed row's own `depth = 0` exactly, not just approximating it (misrecognition is strictly worse than non-recognition — `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own permanent rule, applied here to a semantic gap, not just an unrecognized SQL string). This is sufficient for D2's own flagship regression, which is specifically about a _plain_ grant tuple, not a userset edge — see "Why this scope is enough" below.

`src/resolve/production/resolver.ts` is narrowed from concrete `pg.Pool`/`pg.PoolClient` to `ConnectionSource`/`QueryExecutor` throughout (`WalkContext.pool`/`.client`, `productionCheck`, and every private helper that takes `client`) — the same non-breaking move D0/D1 already applied elsewhere; `npx tsc --noEmit` passes clean across the whole repo with zero caller-site changes anywhere (`authz check`, `POST /check`, `src/audit/checks.ts` all still pass a real `Pool`, which still satisfies the narrower type structurally). **This narrowing is explicitly orthogonal to D-022**, which forbids the _reference_ resolver (`src/resolve/reference/resolver.ts`) sharing code with the _production_ one — parameterizing which physical driver answers `resolver.ts`'s own storage calls touches neither side of that isolation boundary: nothing in `resolver.ts` imports from or shares code with `src/resolve/reference/`, before or after this change, and D-022's own rule was never about how this file talks to Postgres.

**A real, previously-actionable cleanup opportunity found and handled while touching every line of this file, not executed blindly.** D-092's own "Revisit if" predicted this exact moment: "if `assertTokenObserved`... [is] ever widened to a smaller structural type... `assertTokenObservedOnSnapshot`... should be deleted and replaced with a direct call." That widening happened in D0, before D2 ever started — so the _structural_ reason `assertTokenObservedOnSnapshot` couldn't just call `assertTokenObserved(client, atToken)` directly is already gone. It is kept anyway, by deliberate choice now rather than type-system necessity: its own error message names _this check's own transaction snapshot_ specifically, distinguishing a rare, expected-possible snapshot-anchoring race from the pool-level pre-check `productionCheck` already ran moments earlier — collapsing the two would silently lose that distinction for a future debugger. The doc comment is rewritten to state this precisely rather than leave the now-false "structurally not assignable" reasoning in place; the identical stale-reasoning problem in `productionCheck`'s own doc comment (the "getConfig stays outside the transaction" gap, whose original _structural_ half of its two-part justification was also closed by D0) is corrected the same way.

**The pause mechanism — `armNextConnectionPause`, `src/store/dst/source.ts`/`connection.ts` — is DST's own answer to a real limitation the pg-backed regression test lives with.** `docs/DECISIONS.md` D-092's own real regression test (`test/unit/resolve/production/production-check-behavior.integration.test.ts`) had no controllable pause point inside `productionCheck`'s own back-to-back `await`s, and had to resort to a real `LOCK TABLE namespace_configs IN ACCESS EXCLUSIVE MODE` against an _unrelated_ table just to manufacture a wide, deterministic race window. The fake needs no such trick: `armNextConnectionPause(afterStatements)` arms a one-shot pause — after `afterStatements` successful statements on the next connection opened, its next call genuinely suspends (a real pending `Promise`, exactly like a blocked D1 lock acquisition) until the returned `resume()` callback is called. A real design bug was caught and fixed before this shipped, not assumed away: an early draft stored the pause's resolver in a single shared field on the connection source, which a second `armNextConnectionPause` call (before the first pause was ever resumed) would silently overwrite, making `resume()` release the _wrong_ connection's gate. Fixed by making each arming fully self-contained — the gate and its resolver are built and captured entirely inside `armNextConnectionPause`'s own closure, never a shared mutable field — caught by re-reading the design before it was tested, not by a failing test (no test yet exercised two concurrently-armed pauses at once — a real, if narrower, gap this fix closes structurally rather than leaving for a future flaky test to surface).

**Why this scope is enough to reproduce D-092's own flagship regression faithfully.** That bug's real counterexample is specifically about a _plain_ grant tuple (no userset nesting) becoming visible to one of `fetchReachableFrontier`/`fetchTuplesOnFrontier`'s reads but not the other, across a concurrent commit landing between them. A plain grant is exactly what `fetchTuplesOnFrontier` reads off the seed row `fetchReachableFrontier`'s D2 handler always returns — no multi-level recursion is needed to reproduce or close that specific race. `test/unit/store/dst/production-check.dst.test.ts`'s flagship test proves productionCheck's real statement sequence for a token-pinned check on a simple relation is exactly `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ READ ONLY` → the floor-check (which anchors the snapshot) → `fetchReachableFrontier` → `fetchTuplesOnFrontier` → `COMMIT` (`getConfig`'s own `namespace_configs` lookup runs on a _different_ connection, never touching this count — matching `resolver.ts`'s own disclosed, deliberate scoping) — then pauses at both statement 2 (right after the anchor, before the frontier fetch) and statement 3 (between the frontier fetch and the tuple-on-frontier fetch, the exact query pair D-092's own counterexample was about), commits a real conflicting grant on a separate connection while paused, and proves the check's own result never observes it, even though its own later statements literally execute after the grant — while a fresh, unpinned check issued afterward does see it, confirming the grant genuinely landed. Generalized across 6 further seeds varying object/subject identifiers and which of the two pause points fires. Four more tests prove the frontier handler's own scope-limit guard is real and precisely scoped: a schema with a genuine, reachable userset-subject tuple throws the named "D3's own job" error rather than silently under-reporting; the identical schema with no such tuple actually written resolves normally; a genuine userset-subject tuple that a `maxDepth: 0` budget could never actually reach resolves a clean denial rather than throwing (the `maxDepth` fix below); and a real `parent->view` tuple-to-userset check exercises `listTupleSubjects` for the first time (the coverage fix below).

**Two real, distinct findings caught by an independent adversarial review pass before this shipped, not assumed correct from the first draft.** Following this project's own ultracode-mode discipline for substantive work, three independent reviewer agents examined the diff from different angles (snapshot-anchoring correctness, D-092 reproduction fidelity against the real code, scope-limit/edge-case correctness), and every finding was then adversarially re-verified by a separate agent tasked with trying to refute it — 3 findings survived, reducing to 2 distinct root causes:

1. **`fetchReachableFrontierHandler` ignored the real query's own `maxDepth` (`$4`) parameter entirely**, throwing its "D3's own job" error whenever any snapshot-visible userset-subject edge existed on the queried `(object, relation)` — even when the caller's own remaining depth budget was `0`, the exact case where real Postgres's own recursive term (`m.depth < $4`, false at the seed row's `depth = 0` whenever `$4 <= 0`) structurally cannot recurse either, making the seed-only answer this handler already computes the true, complete one. Live-reproduced two ways (`maxDepth: 0` against a relation directly, and via `resolve()`'s own `remainingDepth = Math.max(0, ctx.maxDepth - depth)` computation) before being fixed: the throw condition now requires `maxDepth > 0` too, mirroring the real guard exactly. A new test (`a-userset-edge-that-a-zero-remaining-depth-budget-could-never-actually-reach-does-not-trigger-the-guard`) pins this down; fail-checked live (the `maxDepth > 0` condition temporarily forced to `true`, the new test failed with the exact predicted throw, restored and reconfirmed).
2. **`listTupleSubjects` — one of the three new D2 shapes — was registered correctly but never actually invoked by any DST test**, since neither of the file's existing schema fixtures contained a real `tupleToUserset` rewrite rule (DSL syntax `parent->view`). This silently broke the recognizer-coverage safety argument (`docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s "Two grafts" section: "a mismatch throws 'no shape registered', a test catches it") for this one shape specifically — a future edit to either side's query text could diverge with nothing to catch it, exactly the failure class that discipline exists to prevent, left undisclosed. Closed by adding `TUPLE_TO_USERSET_SCHEMA_SOURCE` (the identical `parent->view` shape this repo's own `schema/example.authz` uses) and two tests genuinely exercising the tuple-to-userset path end to end through `productionCheck`. Fail-checked live (the registered SQL text temporarily corrupted, both new tests failed with the real "no shape registered" error, restored and reconfirmed).

Fail-checked live, this project's established discipline, at both layers: `isVisible` temporarily forced to always return `true` regardless of `visibleAsOf`; all 8 snapshot-dependent tests failed with the exact predicted flip (`result.allowed` from `false` to `true` — the phantom witness reappears), the tests that don't depend on snapshot filtering stayed green, unaffected, as expected; restored via `cp`, confirmed byte-identical via `diff`. Combined with the two review-driven fixes above and their own fail-checks, all 15 tests in this file (35 total across D0/D1/D2's DST suites) reconfirmed green.

**Alternative rejected:** building `fetchReachableFrontierVia`'s real, multi-level recursive BFS now, as part of D2, rather than deferring it to D3.

**Why it lost:** `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own phased plan explicitly separates the two concerns — D2 is snapshot _visibility_, D3 is frontier _recursion_, "parallelizable... depends only on D0" — precisely because they're independent enough not to need sequencing, and folding D3's own work into D2 opportunistically (since the file was already open) would repeat exactly the scope-creep D0 already declined once (leaving `publishSchema` unnarrowed rather than "while already in the file," per D-097). A real recursive BFS needs its own from-scratch algorithm and its own differential-equivalence proof against real Postgres (3,000+ random cyclic/reconvergent graphs, matching D-092's own precedent) — building it hastily as a D2 side effect, without that proof, would ship an unverified mechanism under the same trust the rest of this project extends only to things it has actually checked.

**Revisit if:** D3 lands (recursive frontier) — `fetchReachableFrontierHandler`'s throw-on-userset-edge guard should be replaced by real recursion at that point, not kept as a permanent limitation; the same differential-equivalence discipline `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md` describes for the pg-backed query should extend to proving the _fake's_ frontier BFS matches it too. D4 lands (the seeded scheduler) — the two hand-picked pause points (statements 2 and 3) this phase's own test uses should become one instance of the scheduler's own general interleaving-point selection, not a permanently bespoke mechanism; `armNextConnectionPause` may become redundant once the scheduler can suspend any connection at any statement generically.

---

## D-100 — DST Phase D3 lands: a real, multi-level recursive-frontier BFS, proven equivalent to Postgres by a seeded differential sweep

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST D3, `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** `src/store/dst/frontier.ts`'s new `fetchReachableFrontierVia` is a from-scratch, in-memory BFS that replicates `src/resolve/production/resolver.ts`'s real `fetchReachableFrontier` recursive CTE exactly, not approximately, on three properties: (1) iterative, working-table semantics — each round expands only the previous round's own newly-produced rows, never re-scanning earlier rows, matching standard `WITH RECURSIVE` evaluation; (2) per-iteration (never global) `DISTINCT ON`-equivalent dedup — two edges discovered in the same round toward the identical `(subject_ns, subject_id, subject_relation)` identity collapse to one representative, with this implementation's own "first edge wins" tie-break standing in for Postgres's own unspecified one, safe per D-092's own reachability argument (restated in full in `frontier.ts`'s own doc comment) that the choice of representative can never change the reached-identity _set_; (3) a per-_row_ path cycle guard, checking only the expanding row's own root-to-here ancestry, never a global visited set — matching the real query's own `not (identity = any(m.path))` exactly, deliberately not the _stronger_ global-cycle-detection an in-memory implementation could trivially afford, since diverging here would answer a different reachability question than the real query asks. `src/store/dst/shapes.ts`'s `fetchReachableFrontierHandler` now calls this real BFS, replacing D2's own deliberately narrow seed-row-only stopgap and its "throws, this needs D3" guard entirely (`docs/DECISIONS.md` D-099) — the fake now genuinely answers every case, not just the no-recursion-needed one.

**Proof, not assumption — two layers.** First, `test/unit/store/dst/frontier.dst.test.ts` (10 tests, DB-free, run and passing locally): hand-computed expected outputs for a linear chain, an empty graph, the `maxDepth` boundary (0 and mid-chain truncation — the exact case D2's own review found a bug in, D-099), a real cycle, a self-loop, cross-iteration re-discovery of the same identity (proving dedup is per-round, not global), the classic reconvergent diamond, D-092's own named hardest case (the 12-level branching-3 reconvergent-diamond chain — 37 nodes, no blowup), and snapshot-visibility composition with D2. Fail-checked live: the cycle guard temporarily disabled, the cycle test failed exactly as predicted (an alternating `a`/`b` sequence growing to the `maxDepth` bound instead of terminating at 3 rows via zero-new-rows), restored and reconfirmed. Second, `test/unit/resolve/production/frontier-equivalence.integration.test.ts` (new): 300 random userset-subject-edge graphs (6–25 nodes, out-degree 0–5, self-loops and cycles and reconvergence all possible by construction, `maxDepth` also randomized 0–12 to exercise that boundary differentially) generated via `src/soundness/generators.ts`'s own seeded `fast-check`/`pure-rand` machinery (`SeededRng`/`hashSeedToInt31`, now exported from that file for exactly this reuse — never a new PRNG, matching `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own explicit instruction), run against both the real `fetchReachableFrontier` (now exported from `resolver.ts`, alongside `dedupeFrontier`/`FrontierRow`, matching `src/store/tuples.ts`'s own `WRITE_LOG_LOCK_CLASSID` precedent for exactly this reason — avoiding a hand-copied SQL string that could silently drift out of sync) against a real Postgres testcontainer and the in-memory BFS on an identical fixture, comparing the _set_ of reached identities and, over the _deduped_ (min-depth-per-identity) rows, the max depth reached — never raw per-row paths, and never the raw/undeduped max depth either, since D-092's own reachability argument only establishes invariance for the reached-identity set and each identity's own minimum depth, not for the raw traversal-wide maximum (see the review finding below for why that distinction matters) — plus a direct, dedicated replay of D-092's own hardest known case, additionally reconfirming the real query still completes in well under a second (the exact regression D-092's own `DISTINCT ON` fix closed).

**Honestly disclosed: this integration test could not be run in this sandbox before shipping.** No Docker daemon and no reachable local Postgres exist in this environment (confirmed directly — `docker ps` fails to connect, and the `.env`-configured `DATABASE_URL` refuses connection) — the same constraint every `*.integration.test.ts` file in this repository already lives with, but load-bearing here in a way it wasn't for D0–D2: this is the _first_ execution this differential-equivalence suite will ever get, in CI, not a re-confirmation of something already proven locally. Given that, this phase leaned harder on this project's own adversarial-review discipline than D0–D2 did: an independent four-dimension review (algorithm fidelity against the real recursive CTE's own documented semantics; the never-run integration test's own SQL and generator correctness; wiring/regression risk to D0–D2's already-shipped behavior; whether the DB-free unit tests' own hand-computed expectations might themselves be wrong) ran before this shipped, each finding adversarially re-verified by a separate refuting agent.

**One real, well-hidden bug found, independently rediscovered by all four review dimensions, plus a related doc-comment overclaim — not assumed correct from the first draft.** The differential-equivalence sweep's own `maxDepthReached` assertion compared the **raw, undeduped** max depth reached on each side (`rows.reduce((max, row) => Math.max(max, row.depth), 0)`) for exact equality. This is not actually invariant under `DISTINCT ON`'s documented-unspecified tie-break: D-092's own reachability argument only establishes that a differing per-iteration tie-break can never change the reached-identity _set_ or each identity's own _minimum_ depth — it says nothing about the raw traversal-wide maximum. Concrete counterexample, hand-traced against the real, unmodified `fetchReachableFrontierVia` (not merely argued): the graph `root→A`, `root→B`, `A→C`, `B→C`, `C→A` (a diamond at `C` with a back-edge into one of its two parent branches). If the per-round dedup happens to pick `A`'s edge as `C`'s winning representative (`C`'s own path becomes `[root,A,C]`), `C`'s own `C→A` edge is blocked by the cycle guard (`A` already in that path) and the walk terminates at raw max depth 2. If it instead picks `B`'s edge (`C`'s path becomes `[root,B,C]`), `C→A` is **not** blocked (`A` isn't in that path), so `A` is legitimately rediscovered one round later at depth 3 before finally terminating — raw max depth 3. Same input graph, same correct algorithm, only the arbitrary tie-break differs; the reached-identity set (`{root,A,B,C}`) is identical either way. Because real Postgres's own query-plan-driven `DISTINCT ON` (no `ORDER BY`) tie-break has no reason to correlate with this implementation's own "first edge discovered wins" tie-break, and the random graph generator explicitly, deliberately produces reconvergent-diamond-with-back-edge shapes like this one across its 300 draws, this assertion was a **real, likely-to-eventually-fire CI flake** that would have misleadingly implicated a correct `fetchReachableFrontierVia` rather than the test's own flawed invariant assumption — caught here before this suite's actual first execution in CI, exactly the scenario the heavier four-dimension review was invested in catching. A related, medium-severity doc-comment overclaim in `frontier.ts` (the sentence claiming a differing tie-break "can only affect which specific path/depth gets reported for a node with multiple same-round parents") was corrected alongside it — that framing understated the effect: it can also change the raw, traversal-wide max depth, via exactly the same-round-winner → cycle-guard → extra-rediscovery-round mechanism above.

**Fix:** `maxDepthReached` in `frontier-equivalence.integration.test.ts` now takes a `ReadonlyMap<string, {depth}>` and is called on `dedupeFrontier`'s own output (the same deduped, min-depth-per-identity map already used for the identity-set comparison) on both sides, never on the raw row arrays — the quantity D-092's own reachability argument actually establishes as invariant. `frontier.ts`'s own doc comment and this file's earlier paragraph above are both corrected to state the narrower, accurate claim: the reached-identity set and each identity's own minimum depth are invariant under a differing tie-break; the raw, undeduped maximum is not, and must never be compared directly between two independent implementations. The companion 12-level branching-3 diamond replay test is unaffected in substance — that graph is a strictly layered DAG with no back-edges, so no tie-break-dependent divergence was ever possible there either way — but was switched to the deduped comparison too, for consistency rather than out of necessity. **Fail-check:** the DB-free half of this fix (`dedupeFrontier` collapsing to a genuine per-identity minimum, and the corrected assertion comparing that rather than the raw arrays) was confirmed by re-running `frontier.dst.test.ts`'s existing 10 tests, still green, plus manual re-derivation of the two-tie-break counterexample above directly against the unmodified `fetchReachableFrontierVia` output (both orderings hand-traced to the depths stated above, confirming the bug was real before the fix and that the deduped max — 2 in both orderings — is what the fixed assertion now actually compares). The SQL-dependent half (confirming the fixed assertion passes against real Postgres) cannot be locally re-run in this sandbox for the same reason the original bug went undetected pre-review; it is deferred to this suite's first real CI execution, same as every other part of this integration test.

**Alternative rejected:** trusting the DB-free unit test suite alone as sufficient proof, and treating the real-Postgres differential test as optional/best-effort given it can't be run pre-merge in this environment.

**Why it lost:** the DB-free tests can only ever check this implementation against _itself_ — hand-computed expectations the same author derived, for graph shapes the same author chose. They cannot catch a shared blind spot (a real property of Postgres's own `WITH RECURSIVE`/`DISTINCT ON` semantics neither the implementation nor its hand-derived tests correctly modeled) or a subtle divergence only a genuinely random, adversarial input distribution would surface — exactly the gap `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own design already anticipated by requiring this specific proof shape for D3, not leaving it to D0–D2's own "prove it end-to-end through the real production function" pattern alone. Skipping or downgrading it because this sandbox can't run it would mean shipping D3's own central correctness claim on strictly weaker evidence than every other DST phase's exit criterion demanded, for a reason (sandbox limitation) that has nothing to do with whether the claim is actually true.

**Revisit if:** D4 lands (the seeded scheduler) — this suite's own random-graph generation is a natural candidate to eventually drive from the same scheduler infrastructure rather than its own bespoke `SeededRng` usage, if that unification is ever worth the churn. D5 lands (CI tiering) — 300 graphs was sized for an ordinary "every PR" job with no nightly/PR split yet; once that split exists, the nightly tier is the natural place to run a substantially larger sweep (thousands, matching D-092's own one-time 3,000-graph verification scale) without slowing down every PR's own feedback loop.

---

## D-101 — DST Phase D4 lands: one seeded, reusable scheduler replaces D0–D3's own ad hoc PRNGs and pause choreography, and an adversarial review found the replacement was itself broken on arrival

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (DST D4, `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`) · **Status:** settled

**Decision:** `src/store/dst/scheduler.ts` centralizes two mechanisms D0–D3 each hand-rolled independently: `dstRngFromSeed` (the one canonical seed → `SeededRng` construction, reusing D3's own exported `SeededRng`/`hashSeedToInt31` — never a new PRNG, per `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s explicit instruction) replaces the byte-identical local `mulberry32` copies in `advisory-lock.dst.test.ts` and `production-check.dst.test.ts` (D1/D2), and `raceUnderPause` replaces the hand-written "arm a pause, start the operation under test, confirm it's genuinely suspended, run something concurrent, resume, observe the result" choreography `production-check.dst.test.ts`'s own flagship `it.each([2, 3])` test wrote out in full. Ported onto the new module: `advisory-lock.dst.test.ts`'s 8-seed lock-contention sweep (D1), `production-check.dst.test.ts`'s flagship pause-point test and its 6-seed generalization (D2) — satisfying `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own D4 exit criterion, "at least one earlier phase's test ported onto the shared scheduler with identical pass/fail behavior." A new seeded crash-point sweep was also added to `tuple-store.dst.test.ts` (D0), closing an actual, previously-unclosed gap between `docs/DST-PROPOSAL.md`'s own stated design ("swept across every possible crash point, across many seeds") and what D0 shipped (one hand-picked crash point, no seed variation) — this is new coverage, not a port, and is named as such rather than folded into the "pure refactor" framing the rest of this phase otherwise earns.

**An honest accounting of what "identical pass/fail behavior" actually means here.** The ported tests' own assertions and invariants are unchanged — every one still passes, at the same logical properties it always checked. What changed is _which specific scenarios get drawn_: `dstRngFromSeed` is a structurally different generator from the ad hoc `mulberry32` it replaces (different algorithm, different draw semantics), so the same seed number now produces a different bystander count, a different write-vs-delete mix, a different pause point than it used to. This was flagged directly by an adversarial review as a place the first draft's own doc comment overclaimed — "every test ported onto this module keeps its own exact assertions and pass/fail behavior" reads, uncorrected, as "nothing observable changed," which is false; corrected to state plainly that the draws themselves change while the properties under test do not.

**The central finding: the pause-confirmation mechanism this phase built was itself silently broken for the exact tests it was built to serve, and passed anyway.** Following this project's own ultracode-mode discipline, two independent reviewer agents examined this diff before it shipped — mechanism fidelity (with explicit instructions to verify every claim by actually breaking the code and running the suite, not just reading it) and doc-comment accuracy against the real source and the real pre-D4 tree. The mechanism review's method: temporarily reduce `armNextConnectionPause` to a complete no-op — nothing ever suspends, ever — and run the ported D2 suite. Result: **`test/unit/store/dst/production-check.dst.test.ts` passed 16 of 16 tests anyway**, including all 8 D-092 phantom-witness race tests `raceUnderPause`'s own "confirm it's genuinely suspended" check exists specifically to protect.

Root cause, measured directly: the first draft's `raceUnderPause` confirmed suspension by flushing a _fixed_ 20-hop microtask budget (`flushMicrotasks`, inherited unmodified from the code it replaced) and checking whether the held operation had already settled — a guess, not a fact, about how long "probably still paused" takes. Measured against the real, unmodified code: a token-pinned `productionCheck` call — the exact shape all 8 race tests use — needs **25** microtask hops to settle when it is _not_ paused at all (a plain unpinned check needs 18; `writeTuple` needs 15, which is why the ported D1 lock-contention sweep's own equivalent check has real power and the D2 port's did not). `settled` was therefore always `false` at the 20-hop checkpoint regardless of whether the pause had fired, so the structural throw this phase's own doc comment called "stronger than a soft per-test assertion" could never actually fire for the tests it mattered most for. This is not a new fault-detection gap D4 introduced from nothing — the equivalent hand-rolled checks in the pre-D4 code were built on the identical 20-hop `flushMicrotasks` guess and were equally vacuous for these tests before this phase ever touched them — but centralizing the mechanism and _advertising_ it as newly structural, without actually closing the gap, would have shipped a false sense of rigor strictly worse than the honestly-soft assertion it replaced.

**Fix — no guessing, a real signal.** `source.ts`'s `armNextConnectionPause` now returns `fired`, a `Promise` that resolves the instant the pause genuinely triggers on whichever connection consumes the arming (`connection.ts`'s pause-check branch calls a new `notifyPauseFired` callback synchronously, before ever awaiting the pause gate itself). `raceUnderPause` races the held operation's own completion against `fired` instead of a fixed microtask budget: if `fired` wins, the pause is a verified fact, not an inference, and `concurrentOp` runs next (which, by this same sequencing, can never itself consume an arming meant for `heldOp` — a related finding the redesign moots by construction, since `concurrentOp` is never invoked before `fired` has already won).
