After reverting to `^22.20.1` and running `npm install`, `npm ls @types/node --all` confirms `22.20.1` resolved for the direct devDependency and every relevant transitive consumer; the one pre-existing exception in the tree is unrelated to this fix — `@types/ssh2@1.15.5` (pulled in by `@testcontainers/postgresql` → `testcontainers` → `@types/dockerode`, not by `ssh-remote-port-forward`, whose own separate `@types/ssh2@0.5.52` entry carries no nested `@types/node` at all) pins its own `@types/node@^18.11.18`, resolving to a nested `18.19.130` several layers deep — present before this fix, present after it, and outside D-079's original scope too (confirmed directly via `npm ls @types/node --all` / `npm ls @types/ssh2 --all` against `git show HEAD:package-lock.json`, not inferred).

**Fail-check, adapted for a version-pin fix:** there is no single assertion that flips red/green here — D-079 itself already found the drift "currently benign" (`tsc --noEmit` stays clean under either pin, since nothing in `src/` uses a Node-26-only type), and re-confirming that finding is part of what this fix re-establishes, not a weakness in it. Demonstrated the actual regression mechanism directly instead: reverted to the drifted `^26.2.0`, reran `npm install`, and confirmed `npx tsc --noEmit` stayed clean even in the broken state — proving the regression is silent by construction, exactly the "landmine for the first contributor who reaches for a Node 26-only API" D-079 already warned about, and that no automated check in this repo currently distinguishes the correct pin from the drifted one on its own. Restored the fix, ran `npm install` again, and diffed the two resulting `package.json`/`package-lock.json` states against each other — byte-identical, confirming the round trip introduced no incidental transitive-dependency churn.

**Local verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .` clean; `npx prettier --check package.json package-lock.json` clean; `npm run test` (44 files, 533 tests) green; `npm run build` clean. Run twice — once immediately after the fix, once again after the break/restore round-trip above — identical results both times.

**Alternative rejected:** leave `^26.2.0` in place and instead widen `engines.node`'s own floor to match, treating the Dependabot bump as a deliberate re-decision rather than a regression.

**Why it lost:** nothing about this audit round changed this project's actual supported Node floor — CI still runs Node 22, Railway's own deployment target is unchanged, and no feature work here has ever needed a Node 26-only API. Bumping `engines.node` to match a types package that drifted by accident would be reverse-engineering a real infrastructure decision out of an automated dependency PR's side effect, exactly backwards from how D-079 itself reasoned about this same pin the first time.

**Revisit if:** the same as D-079's own original clause, unchanged by this regression — a future Node LTS bump changes `engines.node`'s floor for a real reason; re-pin `@types/node` to match at the same time, not as an afterthought. Until then, any future Dependabot PR touching `@types/node` should be checked against `engines.node` before merging, not merged on trust the way the regression this entry fixes was.

## D-112 — `soundness_runs.dry_run` marks which rows came from a dry run, and `SoundnessRunOptions.dryRun`'s doc comment now discloses the same-seed concurrency limit (second full-repo audit, finding #13, LOW; finding #9, LOW)

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, finding #13; finding #9) · **Status:** settled

**Decision (#13):** `soundness_runs` gains a `dry_run boolean not null default false` column (`src/store/migrations/0005_soundness_runs_dry_run_flag.sql`), and `runSoundnessFuzz`'s own `insert into soundness_runs` now includes it as a 12th column, bound to the existing `dryRun` local (`options.dryRun ?? false`).

**Why this needed a decision at all:** D-063/D-066 already disclose that a dry run's cleanup (`cleanupDryRunArtifacts`) is best-effort, not transactional — every deletion category is attempted independently, and a process crash or connection loss mid-cleanup can, in principle, leave partial fixture rows behind. If the row that survives such a failure is the `soundness_runs` row itself, nothing previously persisted let a later operator tell "orphaned dry-run debris, safe to purge" apart from "a real, historically significant run that must be kept" — both looked like an ordinary row with no marker distinguishing them. This column closes that gap directly: every insert now records which kind of run it was.

**Decision (#9):** `SoundnessRunOptions.dryRun`'s own JSDoc now states plainly that two concurrent `runSoundnessFuzz` calls sharing the exact same `seed` with `dryRun: true` are unsupported. `generateFixture(seed, queryCount)` is a pure function of its inputs — same seed, same fixture, every time — so two concurrent same-seed dry runs converge on the identical `relation_tuples`/`namespace_configs` rows rather than getting independent ones. `cleanupDryRunArtifacts` deletes those rows _by value_ (`deleteTuple`, `deletePublishedNamespaceVersion`), not scoped to which specific invocation created them, so whichever call finishes first can delete the shared fixture out from under a sibling call still mid-`checkAllQueries`, producing spurious `false_deny` divergences that reflect the cleanup race, not a real resolver defect. Documentation-only: the lowest-risk fix for a LOW finding is disclosing the constraint (distinct seeds — the default, via `generateRandomSeed` — or caller-side serialization), not threading per-invocation row provenance through the whole call chain to make concurrent same-seed dry runs safe.

**Proof, not assumption:** confirmed directly that `deleteTuple`/`deletePublishedNamespaceVersion` delete by value (`WHERE object_ns/object_id/relation/subject...` and `WHERE namespace/version`, no invocation-scoped id), supporting finding #9's doc-comment claim, while the `soundness_runs` row itself is deleted by its own `id` — the new doc comment's "deletes by value" claim is correctly scoped to only the tuple/namespace rows, not the run row. Migration numbering (0005) required no separate registration: `runMigrations`/`copy-migrations.mjs` both discover migrations by directory listing, confirmed by `npm run build` picking up the new file automatically.

**Fail-check:** the insert statement was temporarily reverted to its original 11-column form (removed `dry_run` from the column list, the `values(...)` placeholder list, and the params array — undoing exactly the fix, nothing else). `test/unit/soundness/runner-dry-run-column.test.ts`'s 3 new tests failed for exactly the predicted reason: the captured SQL text had no `dry_run`/`$12`, and the "placement" test caught the column-list length dropping from 12 to 11. Restored the file and confirmed byte-identical restoration via matching md5sums before/after the round trip, then reconfirmed the full suite green. Performed once by the implementing pass and reproduced independently a second time (fresh worktree, no shared state) with an identical result — same md5sum, same three failure messages, same restore.

**Local verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .` clean; `npx prettier --check .` clean on every touched file (the new `.sql` migration is not covered by prettier — no SQL parser configured for this repo, confirmed pre-existing across all five migrations, not a gap introduced here); `npm run test` (45 files, 536 tests) green; `npm run build` clean, with the new migration copied into `dist/store/migrations` automatically. The existing, untouched `test/unit/soundness/dry-run-cleanup.integration.test.ts` still collects/parses cleanly under `vitest.integration.config.ts` in this sandbox (no Docker/container runtime here) and fails only at real container startup, not from a syntax or type error — it will run for real in CI's own `test-integration` job.

**Alternative rejected (#9):** thread a per-invocation identifier through `generateFixture`/`cleanupDryRunArtifacts` so concurrent same-seed dry runs can safely coexist.

**Why it lost:** this is a LOW finding whose actual trigger — two callers deliberately choosing the identical seed for concurrent dry runs — is already the uncommon case (the default path, `generateRandomSeed`, never collides). Building real per-invocation row provenance into the cleanup path is a materially larger change for a scenario a caller can already avoid for free by not sharing a seed; disclosing the constraint is the smaller, more honest fix for the actual risk level.

**Revisit if:** a real caller is ever found deliberately sharing one seed across concurrent dry runs for a legitimate reason (e.g. reproducing the exact same fixture from two different processes for a comparison) — at that point, threading a per-invocation id through `cleanupDryRunArtifacts`'s delete calls becomes worth the added complexity this entry declined to take on.

## D-113 — Five independent doc/comment corrections closing the second full-repo audit's remaining drift findings (second full-repo audit, findings #15–#19, all LOW)

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (second full-repo audit, findings #15/#16/#17/#18/#19) · **Status:** settled

Doc/comment-only — `git diff --stat src/` touches only a doc comment in `src/audit/checks.ts`; every other change lives in `docs/` or a test file's own header comment. Grouped into one entry since all five are independent drift corrections, not related behavior changes — listed individually for traceability, matching D-087's own precedent for grouping unrelated small doc-drift fixes under one entry.

- **#15** (`test/unit/migrate.test.ts`): the file's header comment still said a real-Postgres integration test for `runMigrations` "can be added once Phase 2 gives it actual migration content to run" — stale since `test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts` already exists and covers exactly that (idempotent rerun, concurrent-call race). Updated to reference it in the present tense.
- **#16** (`src/audit/checks.ts`): a doc comment speculated "a future API surface (Phase 8) should route through this same function" — stale since Phase 8 shipped; the API's `POST /check` route (`src/api/server.ts`) already routes through `performCheck` for the same reason. Updated from a forward-looking prediction to a statement of the real, current call graph.
- **#17** (`docs/RELATIONS.md`): cited `schema/malformed-example.authz` as "a worked example of a related rejection" for a tuple write targeting a `permission` name — that file contains no tuple write at all, only two undeclared-relation permissions, an unrelated rejection class. Replaced with a direct pointer to the real mechanism: the `relation_is_a_permission` error in `src/store/tuples.ts`.
- **#18** (`docs/DECISIONS.md`, D-092): D-092's own "Revisit if" paragraph named a structural barrier — `assertTokenObserved`/`currentToken`/`getLatestNamespaceConfig` all requiring a plain `Pool` — as the trigger for revisiting a disclosed duplication/gap. That barrier was already removed by D-097, which narrowed all three to the structural `QueryExecutor` type, but nothing recorded that the trigger had fired. Appended an **Update:** paragraph (this project's established convention for correcting a settled entry — never a silent rewrite) stating the barrier is gone, while clarifying the practical decision D-092 made is unaffected: it never rested solely on that barrier, since `WalkContext.schemaCache`'s own once-per-check fetch already made the one dangerous case structurally impossible independently, and points forward to D-107 as where the identical reasoning is later restated for `expand.ts`. Citation corrected during this fix from "(D-097/D-098)" to "(D-097)" alone — D-098 narrowed a different pair of functions (`publishSchema`/`runMigrations`), not these three.
- **#19** (`docs/DECISIONS.md`, D-103): a "Revisit if" clause referenced `docs/dependabot.yml`, a path that has never existed in this repo — the real file is `.github/dependabot.yml`, confirmed as the only `dependabot.yml` anywhere in the tree. Corrected the path.

**Verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .` clean. `npx prettier --check` clean on every touched file. `npm run test`: 44 files, 533 tests, all passing (no test assertions changed by this entry — coverage is unaffected by construction, since every change here is prose or a doc comment). `npm run build` clean. Fail-check discipline was deliberately not applied: all five changes are comment/prose corrections with zero production-code or test-behavior changes, so there is no assertion that could meaningfully flip red/green — each correction was instead checked directly against the real file/line it now points to (confirmed present, confirmed accurate) rather than assumed.

**Revisit if:** nothing structural — these are point-in-time corrections to references that had drifted from what they described. If a future change moves or renames any of the five things now pointed to (`test/unit/store/migrate.integration.test.ts`, the API's `/check` route, `relation_is_a_permission` in `src/store/tuples.ts`, D-097/D-107, or `.github/dependabot.yml`), the corresponding reference here or in the file it lives in should move with it — the same discipline whose absence created findings #15–#19 in the first place.

## D-114 — Schema DSL grammar frozen (tag `schema-dsl-frozen-v1`); a general-purpose random _schema_ generator lands on `main`, shared by the DST harness and the new schema verifier

**Date:** 2026-08-22 · **Phase:** post-9 (schema verifier, §2 prerequisites — `docs/DECISIONS.md`, this project's own scoped build spec) · **Status:** settled

**Decision (§2a — freeze):** the namespace DSL's grammar, as implemented today in `src/schema/dsl/{parser,compiler,types}.ts`, is declared stable and tagged `schema-dsl-frozen-v1`. Concretely, frozen means: the five `RewriteRule` kinds (`computedUserset`, `union`, `intersection`, `exclusion`, `tupleToUserset`) and no others; the informal grammar in `parser.ts`'s own header comment (`schema := namespace+`, ..., `atom := "(" expression ")" | IDENT ("->" IDENT)?`); `&` binding tighter than `|`/`-`; the reserved-word set (`namespace`, `relation`, `permission`); `IDENTIFIER_PATTERN`/`MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH`; `MAX_EXPRESSION_NESTING_DEPTH` (100). A repo-wide `grep -rn "TODO\|FIXME\|not yet\|not supported\|unsupported" src/schema/dsl/` returned nothing — this grammar has been stable, unchanged, through all nine build phases, the third full-repo audit, and the entire DST D0–D5 effort, without ever needing a grammar-level change; freezing it now is recognizing an already-true fact, not committing to something new.

**Explicitly out of scope for v1 — decided now, not discovered later:** wildcard subjects (`user:*`/`document:*`, no analog anywhere in this grammar); caveats/attribute-based conditions on a relation (SpiceDB-style); a `tupleToUserset` rule following more than one hop in a single atom (`a->b->c`) — the same effect is already reachable by chaining `computedUserset` across permissions, just not as one grammar production; and namespace declarations spanning more than one source file/compilation unit (`compileSchema` already accepts multiple `namespace` blocks in one source string with full cross-namespace reference resolution — see `compileParsedNamespaces`'s two-pass `buildLookup` — so this is a packaging question, not a semantic gap). None of these are needed for the schema verifier to model the grammar faithfully; they're recorded here so a future extension is a deliberate, dated decision against this entry, never a silent grammar drift the verifier's own IR would then be modeling incorrectly.

**Why this needed a decision at all:** the schema verifier's own build spec (§0 rule 3) is explicit that a second parser is a second set of semantics, and the whole value of that tool evaporates the moment it disagrees with the real compiler about what a schema means — which only holds if the grammar it's built against isn't still moving. Tagging it, and writing down what's deliberately excluded, is what lets that assumption be checked later rather than just assumed.

**Decision (§2b — random schema generator):** `src/schema/dsl/random.ts`, new — `generateRandomSchema(seed, options?)` emits a fully random, valid schema (namespaces, relations with type restrictions, permissions with rewrite-rule trees of configurable depth and operator mix), not a random tuple graph over a fixed schema shape. Distinct from `src/soundness/generators.ts`'s existing `generateFixture`, which is a fixed three-namespace-role skeleton (group/hier/resource) with randomized tuple _data_ layered on top, purpose-built for Phase 5's differential fuzzer — that generator was read first and found not to be reusable here for exactly that reason (see "Alternative rejected" below). Written once, on `main`, so both the DST harness and the schema verifier's own future differential tests (§8b of the verifier's build spec: "on small random schemas from §2b, run the verifier against a deliberately dumb exhaustive checker") draw from one generator rather than each growing its own copy.

**Constructive correctness, not generate-and-filter.** Every schema the generator can produce is built to satisfy `compiler.ts`'s real semantic rules by construction: permissions are built in strict dependency order (permission _k_ may only reference relations of its own namespace, or permissions _0..k-1_ of the same namespace — never itself or a later one), which makes `circular_permission_definition` structurally unreachable rather than merely unlikely; a `tupleToUserset` rule only ever follows a relation whose subject types are _all_ namespaces already declared in the same generated unit, ruling out `tuple_to_userset_unknown_namespace`/`tuple_to_userset_unknown_target`; and every name is index-derived (`ns0`, `rel0`, `perm0`, `principal0`, ...), ruling out `duplicate_namespace`/`duplicate_member_name`. The generated source is still always run through the real `compileSchema` before being returned — never hand-assembled into a `CompiledSchema` directly — both matching this package's own "don't reimplement the parser" discipline and because a thrown compile failure is the fastest possible signal this file's own correctness claim has a bug.

**A real bug caught during this construction, not shipped:** the first version of the subject-type generator picked a relation's `namespace#relation`-style target from _all_ of a namespace's member names (relations and permissions together) rather than relations only — `compiler.ts`'s `subject_type_targets_a_permission` rule requires a relation specifically (unlike a `tupleToUserset` rule's own `computedUserset` target, which may legally be either — a real, easy-to-miss asymmetry between two superficially similar-looking reference checks in `compileRelations` vs. `compileRewriteRule`). Caught immediately by this module's own "always compile the output" discipline — the very first 500-seed sweep threw on it — fixed before any test was written against the broken version.

**Proof, not assumption:** 500 default-option seeds and 1000 high-ceiling seeds (5 namespaces, depth 4, all four operators enabled) all compile through the real `compileSchema`, with every rewrite-rule kind (`union`, `intersection`, `exclusion`, `tupleToUserset`) confirmed to actually appear somewhere across the sweep — a generator that silently never emitted `tupleToUserset` (the verifier's own build spec calls this "the edge that produces cross-boundary leaks and the one to get exactly right") would otherwise pass every other check while quietly never exercising it. Determinism verified directly: the same seed and options produce byte-identical `source` and a deep-equal `schema`, twice, independent of whether any option was left to be RNG-resolved internally. Disabling `intersection`+`exclusion` together is confirmed to produce a schema containing neither operator, across 50 seeds — the exact dial the verifier's own §7 fragment-detection tests will need (monotone-fragment-only vs. full-language schemas, on demand).

**Fail-checked live:** the fixed subject-type-target bug described above was deliberately reintroduced (picking from all member names again, not relations-only), and the real test suite failed for exactly the predicted reason — 8 of 17 tests, every one on `compiler.ts`'s own `subject_type_targets_a_permission` message naming the exact offending construct (e.g. `ns0#perm0`, a permission where a relation was required). Reverted; `md5sum`-confirmed byte-identical restoration; all 17 tests green again.

**Alternative rejected:** extend `src/soundness/generators.ts`'s existing `generateFixture` to also randomize its own schema shape, rather than writing a new module.

**Why it lost:** that generator's entire design is anchored to Phase 5's specific fixture needs — a fixed group/hier/resource namespace-role skeleton, salted namespace names to avoid live-table collisions across fuzz runs (this module never writes to any shared store, so that concern doesn't even apply here), and a draw-pool sizing formula keyed to `queryCount`. Bending that file to also produce arbitrary namespace counts and rewrite-rule shapes would have meant carrying Phase 5's own constraints into a genuinely different concern, or forking most of the file anyway. A separate module, reusing only the proven _technique_ (`fast-check`'s seeded `sample(integer(...), { seed, numRuns })`, not the class itself — see the file's own top comment on why `SeededRng`/`hashSeedToInt31` weren't imported directly: `schema/dsl/` is the foundational layer every other module compiles against, and importing from `soundness/` would make it depend upward on a module built on top of it) is a cleaner boundary, at the cost of a small, clearly-documented duplication of a four-line hash function.

**Local verification:** `npx tsc --noEmit`, `npx eslint .`, `npx prettier --check .` all clean. `npm run test`: 47 files, 578 tests (+17 net new in `test/unit/schema/random-schema-generator.test.ts`), all green. `npm run build` clean.

**Revisit if:** any construct named "explicitly out of scope" above is ever actually needed — that's a new, dated decision against this entry (never a silent edit to it), and the tag itself is left standing as a historical marker of what "v1" meant. If a future caller needs `generateRandomSchema` to produce schemas _outside_ the grammar it currently guarantees compile (e.g. deliberately malformed ones, for parser/compiler fuzzing rather than downstream differential testing), that's a different, explicitly-scoped generator — this one's whole contract is "always valid," and weakening that silently would break every caller relying on it.
